## F: F69–fingerstones

### F69
 
 <i>n.</i> a decommissioned navy frigate sunk off Wellington

>  F69 will be sunk off the south coast of Wellington in November, creating an artificial reef for divers. <i>(May 18, 2005,Capital Times,2,18 - 24 May ed.</i>)

### fa'a Samoa
 
 <i>phr.</i> the Samoan way

>  There also seems to be embarrassment that some Samoans were suggesting that fa'a Samoa, Samoan culture, required reciprocity for favours done. <i>(Sep 26, 2005,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  <b>MEAD</b> A child will use the term fa`a Samoa, in `in Samoan fashion'. <i>(1928,Coming of Age in Samoa,x. 133,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KEESING</b> Yet the old Samoan ways, fa`a Samoa,.. still appear to predominate. <i>(1934,Modern Samoa,i.21</i>)

>  <b>FOX & CUMBERLAND</b> Ceremony is an integral part of fa`a Samoa. <i>(1962,Western Samoa,viii. 184</i>)

>  <b>WENDT</b> Our way of life, our precious faa-Samoa, is changing - some say for the worse, some say for the better. <i>(1979,Leaves of Banyan Tree,I.v. 37</i>)

>  In apparent retaliation for going outside the faa Samoa,or Samoan way, Tariu Tuivaiti's house was burned. <i>(Apr 24, 1983,New York Times,VI. 48/1</i>)

>  It was deliberately established here by the churches as an enduring expression of faa Samoa among the uncertainty which confronts ... any newly arrived immigrant group. Faa samoa is a carefully ordered living system of rules and obligations which in Auckland, particularly, finds expression through the churches; ... <i>(May 1987,Metro,112</i>)

>  <b>BURCHFIELD</b> faa-Samoa /|fa a sa:moa/. Also fa`a-Samoa and without hyphen. [Samoan fa`a-Samoa.] The Samoan way of life; the language, customs, etc., of Samoa. <i>(1988,Lexicographical & Linguistic Studies,194</i>)

>  Satoa is steeped in fa'a Samoa - the traditional Samoan way - and although the relatively new Christian religion is in its ascendency, the old gods still cast long shadows. <i>(Aug 2, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  On a drive around Savai'i you encounter <i>fa'a Samoa</i> - the Samoan way. <i>(Jan 31, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  "They usually stayed home during school trips and didn't have holidays. "That's not part of the fa'a-Samoa." <i>(Jun 12, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  New Zealand Samoans continue to keep fa'a Samoa - Samoan ways - even though today over half the New Zealand community is born here. <i>(Oct 9, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

### fa'aaloalo
 
 <i>n.</i> respect

>  Pacific culture has some wonderful qualities: alofa (generosity and love), tautua (service and hard work) and fa'aaloalo (respect), which can be intertwined into the world in which we live and work. <i>(Mar 30, 2005,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

### fa'afafine
 
 <i>n.</i> Samoan transvestite or cross-dresser [ORIGIN: Samoan]

>  <b>Fiu</b> Sometimes, boys in the islands choose to start being fafafines because they are awash with feminine mannerisms. <i>(2006,Purple Heart,169</i>)

>  Short, pithy and succinct jokes and stories that cover such topics as Samoan weddings, Fafafine, his childhood in both Samoa and New Zealand, and his visits to America, produced wave after wave of laughter rolling through the audience. <i>(Aug 27, 2003,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  <i>A Frigate Bird Sings</i> follows the struggle of a fa'afafine.. trying to understand and redefine her contribution to her Samoan family, now living in New Zealand. "It's an extremely delicate subject.. particularly among older Polynesians, because in New Zealand the fa'afafine are part of the gay scene; they've become much more visible and upfront about something that in Samoa is an accepted part of their culture. I think some Polynesians feel that the fa'afafine have become an embarrassment to who we are." <i>(Oct 19, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  They are incensed that New Zealand have a quasi-fa'afafine playing at second five... <i>(Nov 7, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C12</i>)

>  Boys will be girls in Samoa. That's the theme of <i>Paradise Bent</i>, a documentary about fa'afafine showing this weekend at the Outtakes Film Festival... "Most of the families consider having a fa'afafine in the house an asset, because in the plantations they are men and in the home they are very useful. They do the sewing, the ironing and the cooking and they look after people," says one of the women. Then there is the next generation of fa'afafine who are seen more as drag queens. <i>(Jun 7, 2001,City Voice,5</i>)

>  Lindah Lepou has many titles - Fa'afafine Extrordinaire, First Fa'afafine of Soul. <i>(Sep 24, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  A fa'afafine has been jailed for 15 months for pushing a glass into a woman's chin at a Porirua club. <i>(Oct 22, 2001,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  Daryla is what is known as a fa'afafine or feminine man... The tradition of fa'afafines, which literally means "like a woman", is unique to Samoan and Tonga culture. A fa'afafine dresses in women's clothes, has a woman's name and talks and walks like a woman. They prefer straight men... My understanding of the fa'afafine tradition was that in Samoan culture, if a family had no daughters, then the youngest boys is treated as a girl and kept at home to help the mother. These boys then grow up to be fa'afafines. <i>(Feb 3, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C13</i>)

>  "The fat fa'afafine is singing!" <i>(Oct 16, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),70</i>)

>  Pinot noir, pinot blanc, and pinot gris.. all hang under the same name because their bunches cluster in neatly packed shapes similar to pine cones, 'pinot' to Latins. The relationship doesn't end there; under the skin all are part of one of the grape world's most genetically unstable families; the fafafini of the grape world. <i>(Jul 20, 2001,Western News,8</i>)

>  Miss Tuttu Frutti is Samoa's queen of transvestites, crowned in a pageant that raucously celebrates the fa'afafine, or transvestite, lifestyle. <i>(Sep 21, 2004,Dominion Post,A12</i>)

>  "Is that a taro in your pocket or are you a fa'afafine?"... "I didn't realise we were writing a play about fa'afafines until we saw the play. I thought I wrote about a drunken father. Next minute there's all these homosexuals running around! <i>(Oct 26, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  But the President of the Samoa Fa'afafine Association, Roger Stanley, has warned some [fa'afafine] are tempting fate by getting tattoos that are traditionally worn only by women. <i>(Dec 6, 2007,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

>  I like the think of the pinot family as the fa'afafines of the grape world. <i>(Jul 19, 2003,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  Recently fa'afafine performer Lindah Lepou was given an apology for her removal from the stage at the Auckland Pasifica Festival because it was feared she would offend Christians in the audience. <i>(Jan 12, 2006,NZ Herald,A10</i>)

>  "He wanted to see how I did the fa'afine," says Ete. <i>(Nov 24, 2004,Capital Times,10</i>)

### fa'amafu
 
 <i>n.</i> Samoan home brew [ORIGIN: Samoan]

>  Native home-brew, fa`amafu, flows exceedingly freely. <i>(1934,Modern Samoa,iv. 239</i>)

>  <b>MARSACK</b> The making of fa`amafu may well at present be considered a major Samoan industry. <i>(1961,Samoan Medley,xvii. 168</i>)

>  The natives brew their own `beer', called Faamafu, out of potato peelings, malt and sugar. <i>(May 5, 1973,Province (Vancouver),58</i>)

>  The faamafu that costs two shillings a bottle. <i>Ibid</i>.274 The matai council ruthlessly suppressed all faamafu-brewing. <i>Ibid</i>. 275 The rest of the matai .. took furtive trips to the Apia faamafu dens. <i>(1979,Leaves of Banyan Tree,II. 208</i>)

>  <b>BURCHFIELD</b> faamafu /|fa amafu/. Also Fa`amafu. [Samoan fa`amafu.] Home-brewed liquor. Also attrib. or as adj.; also Comb., as faamafu-brewing. <i>(1988,Lexicographical & Linguistic Studies,194</i>)

### face
 
 <i>n.</i> [An extended mining use (cf. OED2 20 b. end of a tunnel or stope at which work is progressing) transf. to bushfalling and gumdigging.] of a tunnel, slope, at which work is progressing'; perhaps influenced by mining

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Face: The end of a tunnel, or edge of a terrace being sluiced. <i>(1947,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary of Mining Terms)]

>  <b>MAY</b> FACE: the end of a tunnel or a drive; the wall, cheek or side of a terrace or a reef. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1962 ed.</i>)

>  When these difficulties are considered and when [they?] were taken `on a face' (as the diggers say), the times made by these two [climbers] are very good. <i>(1895,NZ Alpine Journal,2/7/22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> take (?difficulties) on a face, to face up to (difficulties) [? a pun on miners' use of face.]



 
 <i>n.</i> If this does not refer to the edge of the area of bush being cut, it is n2 below, how to manage felling a steep face with bush on it.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The three [15] brothers spent the first few weeks clearing, learning the art of chopping, scarfing, driving and working a face. <i>Ibid.</i> k 168 He and a mate took on a contract to cut about 20 acres of light bush and scrub, but as neither of them knew anything about how to work out a face ... they soon got into difficulties. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [cf facing; sidling.]

>  Sheep seem all right as all on steep sunny faces which have partly cleared. <i>(Jul 11, 1857,St Leonard's Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1857 St Leonard's Station Diary 11 July in MacFarlane Amuri (1946) 126]

>  <b>201</b> We mustered about four miles of the fase of Ben McLeod. I went up some 7000 ft. ... <i>(Oct 20, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,1950</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> [sc. of hill] / In the afternoon T.A.P. & W.P.P. burnt the faces round the Point. <i>(Sep 17, 1866,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,81</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> expensive lawsuits which have occurred, owing to the difficulty of [141] exactly determining boundaries where the `faces' are from 100 to 200 feet in height; <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries in Otago,140,1962 ed.</i>)

>  Face, Facing: Side of a hill or range. (C.) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  Face and sidling.-S. is generally used in connection with mustering and connotes the idea of steepness or roughness, and is never used when discussing the aspect. You do not say `a dark s.,' though you often hear `dark side'; but f. can be used in either sense. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> On the face to the south the bush was either silver pine or silver birch and so open you could drive cattle through it. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,111</i>)

>  Glossary Face: open hillside. <i>(1847,Wayleggo</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... the stag up in the face gave tongue again. <i>(1949,High Country Days,137</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Face and Sidling. Sidling is generally used in connection with mustering and connotes the idea of steepness or roughness, and is never used when discussing the aspect. You do not say `a dark sidling', though you often hear `dark side'; but face can be used in either sense. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,367</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> White repeated in the high cloud [toward the Southern Alps] with a hint of nor'west arch. Grey shingle-slides clear on the nearest faces. <i>(1960,Word for Word,85</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The latter block ... then took in only the Wairau faces and a stretch of harsh manuka flats _ ... <i>(1973,Big Country,29,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DAVIES</b> Those sounds meant a full day on the hills ... climbing steep faces ...or descending into ... ravines to rescue trapped ewes. <i>(1975,Outback,8</i>)

>  <b>NORDMEYER</b> ... the fencing off of the more dangerous faces and gullies ... have made farming in the high country less of a gamble than it was. <i>(1981,Waitaki: the river and its lakes,31</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> They chased that old boar .../ Through fern, face and gully and hills far away!/ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,49</i>)

>  face ... 15. N.Z. the exposed slope of a hill. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,396</i>)

>  face ... NZ open hillside or slope <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,267</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> It was quite a common practice for experienced bushmen, to have a drive'. If you are chopping up a `face' and there is a large tree behind you further up the face, you select that as the `key'. To save time ... you do not waste time by chopping down each tree individually, but just `scarf' and partly chop each tree. When all is ready and you have worked back to the key, you scarf it very carefully. ... If everything goes according to plan it will `drive' the one below, which will in turn drive the next, and so on ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,22</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> with modifiers describing the composition or its aspect to the sun: esp. dark, shady, sunny.

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Esp. work (out) a face, open a face. This is seen on all sunny and shady slopes (the `sunny' and `dark faces' of the shepherds) ... <i>(1928,Vegetation of New Zealand, The,231,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> So up a pig track he started to the ferny face where the dogs were barking. <i>(1937,Thomas,102</i>)

>  Here it must be pointed out that what Cockayne described as sunny and dark faces are for the most part, and especially in Central Otago, essentially windward and leeward faces respectively. <i>(1938,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,20/232A</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> It was country with great open shingle faces. <i>(1949,Wayleggo,37</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> but I face north, and no rabbit goes voluntarily to the cold faces when the warm ones are open. <i>(Jul 25, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,41,1961 ed.</i>)

>  Subsequently, cattle have been used for grazing, and subdivision fences erected to adequately control the grazing of dark and sunny faces. <i>(1958,Report of Soil Conservation ...Council,11</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> during the summer on these high altitudes, sheep throve, and had to be mustered to the sunny faces of the ridges where the snow first melted, ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,9</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> The lambs stopped about halfway down the spur on a big tussock face, ... <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,40</i>)

>  <b>PRESTON</b> [a shady face] That year (1908) it was three of four weeks before we could get to the sheep in the back country; and then we could shift only those caught on the dark faces, and bring them across to the sunny side. <i>(1978,Woolgatherers,110</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> The dogs flushed the boar on a high ferny face,/ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,48</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Though the sunny faces may dry up, the dark faces carry considerable feed, the reverse of usual high-country conditions. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations NI,13</i>)

>  <b>FRANCIS</b> The dark faces of the spurs were now dangerous and it was easy to slip and fall disatrously. (The so-called dark faces were slopes and hillsides that got no sun during the winter and remained frozen.) <i>(1983,Wildlife Ranger,59</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We got past them all too soon.. getting a close view of the big bare clay "faces" on one side of the river, which look like cuttings. <i>(Jun 17, 1924,Otago Witness,68</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering and hunting] an exposed slope near the top of a hill or mountain.

>  Sheep seem all right as all on steep sunny faces which have been partly cleared. <i>(Jul 11, 1857,St Leonard's Station Diary,126</i>) [NOTES: in MacFarlane (1946) Amuri]

>  Vegetating of loose faces in high country .. Under what conditions will marram grass grow? Would it be suitable in the MacKenzie country to prevent shale-rock faces from slipping...? <i>(May 20, 1921,NZ Journal of Agriculture,313</i>)

>  On account of the extreme roughness of the country and the lack of suitable vegetation, the sheep do not graze on the high faces of the main range. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,98</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Face: open hillside. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,153</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Open faces and great shingle basins, it was not good deer country. <i>(1949,High Country Days,147</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... later, he 'came clean' and told that he was shooting in a high scrubby face not far from camp ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,60</i>)

>  - all of the 1423 acres of their property, with the exception of a few steep back faces and gullies, are ploughable. <i>(Nov 5, 1953,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This is the lowest part of the property and takes in some highly attractive tussock faces ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,160</i>)

>  .. a bulldozed track winds its way up a steep manuka face following a narrow, rocky ridge ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,164</i>)

>  It's plain logic to me that the dog who'll hunt three sheep up a face or pen a trio, will be much easier on the nerves, temper and throat back on the farm. <i>(Jul 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,46</i>)

>  <b>BEACH</b> The terrain here was gorsey faces, and native bush gullies which ran from the flat to the main ridge. <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,26</i>)

>  <b>BEACH</b> .. Bill and Linton sidled in towards a large native bush face. <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,105</i>)

>  <b>BEACH</b> It would be four hundred yards down a steep bluffy face into the creek ... <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,140</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> No one knew that a cloudburst the month before had scoured guts down the face depositing rocks large and small through the tussocks. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,112</i>)

### face as long as a crowbar
 
 <i>phr.</i> a long face

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Gus had been moping about with a face as long as a crow-bar for nearly a week, and the motor-bike was getting a spell, ... <i>(1927,Me and Gus,82,(1977 Gus Tomlins ed Sturm) ed.</i>)

### face check
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASSEY</b> Face check A drying split on the faces of boards and flitches. <i>(1987,Woodturning in New Zealand,93</i>)

### face cover
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the extent to which sheep have wool growing around eyes and head.

>  Systematic work on face cover on Romney sheep has been carried out at Massey College over the last six years ... <i>(Oct 15, 1962,NZ Journal of Agriculture,366</i>)

### face cut
 
 <i>n.</i> The first piece of timber sawn from a log on the breaking-down bench. [Forestry. Transf. use of face = surface. Recorded earliest in NZ.]

>  <b>BAINES</b> Suppose a piece of timber - a flitch nine inches by twenty-four and sixteen feet long - is on the carriage, and that we intend cutting nine by three-quarter boards. After taking off a `face' cut, setting the gauger rollers, and placing the flitch in position, the man at the head starts the machinery. `Kreash,' says the saw. and in twelve seconds she is out at the other end. The man at the `tail' at that instant reverses the motion of the table, which gigs back. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,180</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> So far we had asked for `facecuts', the first slices off the logs with the bark adhering ... <i>(1950,My First 80 Years,105</i>) [NOTES: [c1890-1900]]

>  <b>WILSON</b> There were good boards among these facecuts which today would be classed as O.B. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,28</i>)

### face doesn't fit
 
 <i>phr.</i> [Can't identify positively the original clause which provoked this description but the nearest is the clause in the State Sector Act of 1988 which refers to the dismissal of a Chief Executive: The [Commissioner] may, with the agreement of the Governor-General in Council, for just cause or excuse, remove the Chief Executive of a Department from office. [<i>NZ Statutes</i>, 1988, 1(20) 244 or <i>Revised Statutes</i>. 33 (1995) : 73.]]

>  Let's take a face-doesn't-fit situation. <i>(Aug 26, 2004,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Nine to Noon]

>  MPs who had built fortresses around their electorates - Brian Neeson and Warren Kyd - were overthrown under the new 'face doesn't fit' clause. <i>(May 19, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

>  Within three days of the Government taking power, State Services honcho Michael Wintringham had sent a letter to his new Minister with the words "face doesn't fit" in an attached note... "Face doesn't fit" is a term used to described clauses which have been included in some public service contracts, creating provision for termination of the job if there are irreconcilable differences between two parties. <i>(Jul 4, 2001,Evening Post</i>)

>  Their careers are surely jeopardised because they stood by her, especially if we are to have government that's churned over each time a new government is elected, each time a bureaucrat's face doesn't fit. <i>(Jul 15, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)

>  It's equally likely, though, that her problem is her brute refusal to accept political reality. Sometimes, in public life, it's no more complicated that that your face doesn't fit. <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  We have seen the argument taking place where ministers want to be able to sack chief executives whose face doesn't fit. <i>(Aug 2, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

>  "Other senior figures in the state sector will now wonder who will be next for the 'Rankin treatment' when Helen Clark and her colleagues decided the face doesn't fit any more," Dr Newman said. <i>(Aug 3, 2001,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Legal advice had made it clear that the "face doesn't fit" clause could not have been used to oust Ms Rankin as soon as the new Government came to office - and that its use may never have been legal, he said. <i>(Aug 6, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

### face fart
 
 <i>v.</i> to belch

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> `Arp barp', went her husband. `For God's sake stop that face farting all the time,' said Mrs Ransumeen. <i>(1982,Some Other Country,237</i>) [NOTES: 1982 Marshall in 1984 Some Other Country 237]

### face louse
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a type of sheep louse.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> There are several species of lice found on sheep, but those which occur in New Zealand are: 1) the red louse or body louse, or wool louse (Damalinia ovis); 2) the face louse (Linognathus ovillus); and 30 the leg louse (Linognathus pedalis). The face louse is rare and the leg louse more common in New Zealand... <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,83</i>)

### face plant
 
 <i>n.</i> falling on your face [also face-plant verb (2005 below)]

>  "Falling off is nature's way of telling you you've found your limit," is a philosophical definition of a very pertinent problem to readers of <i>Classic New Zealand Mountain Bike Rides</i>..." The inevitable result is a <i>Face plant</i>. <i>(Jan 15, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  Each time I get off, I face-plant into the snow or almost do the splits. The only consolation is when I ride next to a girl who is carrying her board. When I ask why, she says she fell over so many times when getting off, she gave up. <i>(Aug 30, 2005,NZ Herald,D8</i>)

### face-brand
 
 <i>v.</i> [(? obs farming)]

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> All hands face branding rams and then mustering Snowy Peak. <i>(Jan 30, 1867,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,93</i>)

>  All were face-branded U ... <i>(Feb 22, 1868,Marlborough Express,5</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> face branding sheep. <i>(Apr 23, 1868,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,147</i>)

### face-grease
 
 <i>n.</i> face-cream

>  <b>McKEARNEY</b> I buy a jar of `face grease' at intervals. <i>(1938,Just Me,63</i>)

### face-moko
 
 <i>n.</i> traditional facial tattoo (See [cf. moko](../M#moko))

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Soot for use as colouring matter for tattooing the body was sometimes made from the burnt <i>awheto</i> or `vegetable caterpillar' (Cordiceps robertsii), but was not black enough for face-<i>moko. </i> <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,259</i>)

### facial eczema
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The incidence ... [is] best described by reviewing the farming conditions ... in the 1938 facial eczema outbreak. <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,24/201A</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> In seasons when facial eczema is likely to occur, ... Weather conditions ... appear to have an intimate relationship with facial eczema outbreaks. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,60,1970 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fungus-induced contagious disease of sheep.

>  Acute Facial Eczema in Sheep/ [heading] This disease, which is manifested by an eruption on the face and ears of lambs, is solely due to dietetic errors. Some years ago I investigated what might have been termed an outbreak. <i>(Jul 1900,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,8 : 200</i>)

>  Breeding-ewes have been at times heavy sufferers from facial eczema (or facial dermatitis), as this trouble of the skin of the head is termed. <i>(Jan 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,578</i>)

>  Facial eczema appeared in areas of plentiful growth of autumn feed ... <i>(1916,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture</i>) [NOTES: 1916 Dept Ag. Annual report in 1941 NZJST 22/38A]

>  It was considered desirable to determine ... the effect of the so-called `facial eczema' on sheep-skins ... <i>(1938,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,20/92A</i>)

>  Out breaks of facial eczema occurred ... in 1916, 1917, ... 1928, 1929, and 1933. <i>(1941,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,22/30A</i>)

>  The name `facial eczema' was first applied years ago, and was later changed to `facial dermatitis'. Popular preference was, however, so strongly in favour of `facial eczema' that this, though not truly descriptive, was finally adopted as the common name of this disease. <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,24/186A</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> In seasons when facial eczema is likely to occur, ... Weather conditions ... appear to have an intimate relationship with facial eczema outbreaks. -<i>Ibid</i> For many years facial eczema was linked with a certain autumn pasture condition ... <i>Ibid</i> 334 The year 1938 saw one of the worst incidences of facial eczema ever encountered in New Zealand, or for that matter in the world. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,60,1970 ed.</i>)

>  facial eczema NZ contagious disease of sheep. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,268</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 175]]

>  Although the Facial Eczema Advisory Committee.. had agreed that it could see no benefit from spending more money on facial eczema at this stage, it was proposed to maintain the same commitment of research staff. <i>(Dec 1971,NZ Dairy Exporter,20</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stock disease caused by a toxic fungus Pithomyces chartarum [first mentioned in 1882, although not officially recorded until 1898.]

>  Acute facial eczema in sheep: This disease, which is mainfested by an eruption on the face and ears of lambs, is solely due to dietetic errors. <i>(1900,Report,200</i>) [NOTES: Eighth Report of the Dept. of Ag]

>  The prevalence of so-called facial eczema among sheep last year. <i>(Nov 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,464</i>)

>  Another and more practical use of chloromycetin is in the treatment of pink eye, which is often seen in sheep that have travelled in mobs or are being held in paddocks during periods of danger in facial eczema. <i>(Jan 16, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,83</i>)

>  The goal of the Houghton's breeding programme is simple: to breed into their flocks such a level of resistance to facial eczema (FE) that they can safely expect the disease to be a production-reducing nuisance only once every half-century. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,5</i>)

>  We've been here for 30 years, and we'd never had a problem with FE until two years ago. Facial eczema is caused by the spores of a fungus, Pithomyces chartarum, which lives in the base of the pasture. [FE] <i>(2001,Wool Grower,Autumn 11</i>)

### facing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> We saw the fire sweep away up the further sides of the range, and leave our unconscious sheep safe on the still grassed facings of their run. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,143</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> ... growing together in the alpine district above the gorge of the Ashburton, on the facings of Trinity Hill ... <i>(1882,Out in the Open,77</i>)

>  It [kea] seeks as a nesting-place the shelter of almost inaccessible rocks or burrows its hole in steep facings. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,481</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>HOSKENS</b> [Quoting a roadside stone erected in 1917, north of Burke's Pass township] So shall your mountain facings and river flats be preserved to your children's children and for evermore. <i>(1964,Life on a Five Pound Note,112</i>)

>  Face, Facing: Side of a hill or range. (C.) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> When sunny facings have been fixed ... <i>Ibid</i> 82 ... before lighting a rough facing ... covered with roughage. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,80</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> Mr. Tripp was particularly keen about burning but as far as the tussock land was concerned, especially the sunny facings, he always said the grass should be burnt as far as possible when the ground was damp so as not to destroy the roots. The dark facings and the lawyers and scrub should be burnt whenever the country was dry enough. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,58</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a broad, steep hillside rising from a river gorge or deep valley.

>  <b>ACLAND</b> The runs of which Mt. Algidus originally consisted took in all the river facings and safe country ... <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,213</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This is all beautiful country, and some of the river facings were as attractive as any I saw. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,236</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> We saw the fire sweep away up the further sides of the range, and leave our unconscious sheep safe on the still grassed facings of their run. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,143</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The pick of the station, of course, is the great stretch of sunny facings running right from the Woollies to the lower end of the block. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,159</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. climbing steeply up through some sunny facings thick with willows and silver poplars. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,206</i>)

### facing-up table
 
 <i>n.</i> [Post Office.]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> There was [postal] work [in the 1960s], hard satisfying work. The volcanoes of letters on the facing-up table, the checker chanting the reds onto the lorry, the checker calling the overseas in, London 45. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,85</i>)

### factory
 
 <i>n.</i> In NZ very often a dairy factory manufacturing cheese, butter, and other dairy products. Compare creamery; and contrast the meat industry's works. [Shortened form of "dairy factory", "cheese factory", "butter factory"]

>  The factory system is now fairly well established.... It is hardly necessary to point out that all butter and cheese intended for export will have to be factory-made, ... <i>Ibid</i> Classification of Dairies. - Dairies are registered as - `Factories' or `creameries,'i.e., milking at least fifty cows; ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,102</i>) [NOTES: 1892 Murphy in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ (Lands Department) 102]

>  <b>WOOLLASTON</b> [cWW1, Taranaki] ... the cows were dry and our fathers not going to the factory (called that [instead of `creamery'] now that they made cheese there instead of butter). Hence, factory butter (or shop butter) as distinct from dairy (or farm) butter. <i>(1980,Sage Tea,56</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> (George) Factory butter was one and two a pound, separated farmers' butter one shilling a pound [c1910]. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,70</i>)

>  Quite a number of factories have inserted machines during the past year. <i>(1898,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,VI/104</i>)

>  The Directors informed the milk suppliers that they would have to reduce the price of the milk supplied to the Factory to 3d the gallon or 10 lb. <i>(Sep 2, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  "Factory" or "creamery" shall mean a place established for receiving the milk or cream of cows from the public for the purpose of manufacturing the same or any portion of the same into butter or cheese, or both, as the case may be. <i>(1892,NZ Statutes,56 Vict. 30/178</i>)

### factory bach
 
 <i>n.</i> accommodation provided for dairy factory hands

>  He moved that the Minister of Public Health's attention be called to it, with a view to having factory baches periodically inspected. <i>(Dec 14, 1918,Stratford Evening Post,8</i>)

### factory butter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  They [sc. English brokers and merchants] comment very favourable concerning the quality of our "factory" or "creamery" butter. <i>(1894,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,II/202</i>)

### factory hand
 
 <i>n.</i> [(dairy)]

>  At Pungarehu a paid cook was supplied to the factory hands. <i>(Dec 14, 1918,Stratford Evening Post,8</i>)

### factory lorry
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The factory lorry comes swift and soon, / Like hell down the road, 'twixt midnight and noon'. <i>(Jul 25, 1928,NZ Free Lance,42</i>)

### factory wire
 
 <i>n.</i> The thin wire used to bind the old double-pack octagonal wooden cheese crates was known in Taranaki as factory wire and served the needs of those needing a more flexible No. 8 around the farm. [Known from at least the 1930s.]

### factory-supply
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of dairy herds] producing milk for dairy factory processing [rather than town milk supply.]

>  <b>EDGAR</b> In factory-supply herds the main food at this time should be autumn-saved pasture. <i>(1959,NZ Farmers' Veterinary Guide,15</i>)

### fade
 
 <i>v.</i> to blend colours (in graffiti)

>  Graffiti Speak... Fade: To blend colours. <i>(Nov 30, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

### fade-it
 
 <i>n.</i> [(marble term)]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Here are some [marble] terms from Taranaki around 1880-1890: firsts, lay-up, stakes, tips, sticking-taw, nothings, anythings, fade-it, knuckledown stiff, dubs. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,269</i>)

### fadge
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an unpressed part-bale of wool, sewn up as it stands, weighing less than 100 kg [Farming]

>  Regarding the respective values of the clips, it is hardly necessary to state that fleeces from March-shorn sheep were not able to be rolled. From them only stained wool and bellies were sorted out .. A star bale sold at a crutching sale made 101/4d., while a fadge of the same wool pooled later made 121/4 pence ... <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,10/326</i>)

>  Wool bale or w. pack. - Becomes a bale of wool when filled and pressed and a fadge when loosely filled. (C.) <i>(Dec 30, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  fadge n -s 3 Austral : an irregular package of wool weighing from 60 to 150 pounds <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,814</i>)

>  <b>REID</b> .. sold 3,710 bales at the auctions in that [1894-1895] season and many more bags and fadges at their own private Sales. <i>(1979,Otago Farmers Ltd The Centennial History 1878-1978,39</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>MAF</b> Fadge:Package of wool in a wool pack weighing less than 100 kg. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,55</i>)

>  A Winton correspondent has sent me a cutting from the Southland Times, its Lost and Found column .. Only one object was reported found, but what a find! `Found fadge of wool'. <i>(Feb 5, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),72</i>) [NOTES: [I.A. Gordon]]

>  Fadge underweight package of wool (used on packhorses). <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)

>  fadge n. NZ & Austral. a small pack of wool; a part-filled wool-bale. [etym. uncert.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,268</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> the bag into which wool is baled

>  This week she filled two fadges with the rubbish, but it tells only part of the story when she thinks back to another of the floods in the 18 years since she moved on to the property about 1986. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Hawke's Bay Today,Hawke's Bay Weekend B11</i>)

### fadge holder
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Google search 11/2006: NZ 364, Austral. 10 hits.]

>  GALVANISED pipe fadge holder $70. [sic] phone 843 5215. <i>(May 9, 2006,Hawke's Bay Today,23</i>)

>  LICENCED AUCTIONEERS .. carrier, fadge holders ... <i>(May 14, 2005,Hawke's Bay Today,Weekend Market : 15</i>)

### fafa
 
 <i>n.</i> a Samoan transvestite or cross-dresser [ORIGIN: abbreviation, abbreviation of fa'afafine]

>  <b>Fiu</b> The chances are that the youngest boy of the family becomes the fafa of the household. <i>(2006,Purple Heart,169</i>)

### fag
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a younger stag in a pair of feral red deer that is trained by the older one to take risks for the couple.

>  <b>LENTLE & SAXTON</b> Fag: younger stag in a company of two, usually a spiker. The fag takes the risks, in emerging first from cover, for example, but learns cunning from the older animal. (The term derives from the English public-school system in which a fag was employed by senior boys to do chores). <i>(1991,Red Deer in New Zealand,183</i>)

### fag bag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  She says other characters likely to make an appearance include a nerdy guy who has just "come out", a female parking warden who moonlights at the gay bar, and a gay guy's straight female friend (known as a fag bag) who is making her first visit to a gay club. <i>(Jan 26, 2006,Wellingtonian,28</i>)

### fagus
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> ... in which the brown-tinted totara, the silvery phyllocladus with its purplish points, the small leaved kohai and the soft bright foilaged ribbon-wood contrast well with the dusky hue of the dark-leaved fagus. <i>(1882,Out in the Open,186</i>)

>  47. <i>Platycercus novae-zealandiae</i>, Sparrm. Red-fronted Parroquet, Kakariki. - Years ago it was very common in the numerous Fagus woods in the Malvern district ... <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,480</i>) [NOTES: POTTS]

### fail to flatter
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Sport) perform badly [esp. horseracing. Australia and New Zealand]

>  The surberb batting returns couldn't hide ordinary series for Canterbury stalwarts Nathan Astle and Craig McMillan and the Marshall twins, Hamish and James - who all failed to flatter on the recent tour to Zimbabwe and South Africa. <i>(Dec 13, 2005,NZ Herald,C14</i>)

### failing mouth
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Failing Mouth: see Ages of Sheep. <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

### failing subject
 
 <i>n.</i> [(secondary schools')]

>  Children often study one more subject than the minimum required to pass an examination; they call the extra subject their failing subject. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,173</i>)

### failing-mouth
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an aged ewe with no teeth.

>  lambs, prime-freezers 11s to 12s 2d. failing-mouths, from 4s 6d. <i>(May 1897,NZ Farmer,167</i>)

>  Four and six-tooth ewes made up to 16s 1d; failing mouths 10 s to 11s 6d. <i>(Jun 3, 1909,Weekly News,38</i>)

>  After four years a sheep is full-mouthed, failing or gummy. <i>(1919,Livestock in New Zealand,17</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> failing mouth: a sheep losing its incisor teeth through age. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,29</i>)

>  Stock Sale: 150 Romney full and failing mouth ewes. [failing mouth] <i>(Aug 5, 2000,Otago Daily Times,37</i>)

### failing-mouthed
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] losing teeth.

>  The principal demand is for sound-mouthed sheep, with one or two years' service in front of them and even the failing-mouthed ewes are in request. <i>(May 2, 1932,NZ Farmer,328</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> The teeth begin to deteriorate and the animal steadily becomes broken- or failing-mouthed. [failing mouthed] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,11</i>)

### fair
 
 <i>adv.</i> completely; thoroughly; unequivocally; forcefully. (compare real adv.)

>  <b>I A GORDON</b> If you strike something "fair and square" you strike it accurately and with force. A new meaning had been born - complete(ly), forceful(ly). A woman who tells you she is "fair affronted" is (a) severely insulted and (b) from the north of the UK. This usage has found its way into New Zealand colloquial idiom: " It fair gets me down"; "I feel fair sick when I come to think of it"; and (of course) "It's a fair cow" which takes "fair" a long way from its original sense of "beautiful". <i>(Mar 14, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> I feel fair sick. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,38</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> It fair gets me down to see the poor brutes dying. <i>(1904,Sons o' Men,88</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> I get fair sick of it ... <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,190</i>)

>  <b>CHERRILL</b> I'm fair thrilled to bits. <i>(1950,Story of a NZ Sheepfarm,183</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sale of livestock.

>  The recent Hawke's Bay ram fair was remarkable for the high proportion of poor quality rams. <i>(Feb 17, 1911,Hawke's Bay Herald,11</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> Farmers of the period avoided the necessity of buying rams at ram fairs ... <i>(1958,Sturdy Sons,83</i>)

>  <b>FARAM</b> .. in 1916 one Brown, a picker, got 86 bullocks and 20 more a month later, so Dad promptly bought another 90 weaners at the fair. <i>(1987,Jonathon Hutchinson of Parihohonu,32</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> By the time I had broken in the horses the annual weaner fairs were fast approaching. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,115</i>)

>  Mr Orr says vendors are feeling positive about the sale, following on from early autumn fair prices ... [ewe fair, ram fair, cattle fair, weaner fair, lamb fair, hogget fair] <i>(May 14, 2002,Manawatu Evening Standard,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> absolute, complete, thoroughgoing; `real'. (See [fair cow](../F#fair-cow),[fair dinkum](../F#fair-dinkum))

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> While it [sc. rain: chk ?money] held out it would be a fair pour. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,26</i>)

>  Fair.-Absolute (in a bad sense): f. cow, f. knock out, etc. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  Fair nark - a very unpleasant person. To nark: to spoil - e.g., now you've gone and narked the whole show! ... [nf P8] Fair treat - denotes emphasis - eg, the boss went crook a fair treat. <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,39</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary of Slang)]

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... fair treat: exceedingly; e.g. he went crook a fair treat: he became very angry <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> I just want it to be really good, that's all. I'm laying out a fair whack on all this, you know. <i>(1979,Skin Deep,30</i>)

### fair buck
 
 <i>n.</i> a fair deal, a fair spin [ca1935 Havelock schoolchildren e.g. He didn't get a fair buck from the teacher.]



 
 <i>exclam.</i> expressing disbelief, incredulity, or an appeal for fair play or a fair hearing [ca1935 Havelock 1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 5 FAIR BUCK]

>  He was clearly uncertain whether or not to believe him. `Fair buck!' another digger chimed in. <i>(1941,Convoyager HMT 22</i>) [NOTES: Fourth Reinforcements]

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> He'd go up behind her and grab her arse like half a ton of blancmange. ... He did, fair buck. <i>(1985,Shuriken,22</i>)

>  I haven't seen "fair buck" as a synonym for "I am telling you the truth" in print before. <i>(Mar 4, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

### fair burl
 
 <i>exclam.</i> fair spin! (of pennies) (phs the orig. meaning: this is the earliest recorded use in a two-up or pitch-n-toss context).

>  So up they [pennies] went spinning well/ And bettors [OEDS betters] cried `Fair burl!' [`Faer `burl'!'] <i>(May 16, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,137</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>exclam.</i> used like fair spin! fair do! fair go! as an (esp. children's) exclam. inviting belief, or with query tone, indicating disbelief.

### fair cow
 
 <i>n.</i> [1960 17C M B6 St Bede's, Chch 3 COW (A FAIR COW)] (See [cow](../C#cow))

>  [Hdg] A fair cow's handmaiden. <i>(Nov 1, 1943,NZEF Times,III/123 : 13</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `Did you have much trouble getting all this circus up from Tapuwai?' Genevieve asked ... `A fair cow,' drawled Potty Barker. <i>Ibid.</i> 59 Pat had only Potty Barker's `a fair cow of a winter' to conjure scenes of dripping desolation. <i>Ibid.</i> 70 Potty Barker ... said with emphasis for the second time: `A fair bloody cow.' <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,52</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `A fair nark' or, more emphatically `a fair cow', is anything inexpressibly tedious or baffling. <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,174</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> We are scarcely less adept than the Australians in describing anything objectionable, including persons, as <i>a cow </i>or <i>a fair cow. </i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The phrases, `a cow', `a fair cow' must have had their origin in paddocks or milking sheds (they are also current in Australia); they are now used to describe anything difficult or unpleasant, from an obstinate engine to a tough problem in mathematics. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,90</i>)

>  `Cow' is a common word of abuse, not so surprising when so many of the people have to struggle with the beasts. If something is godawful, they'll say it's a `fair cow,' or they'll call a bad day a `cow of a day.' <i>Ibid.</i> 39 <i>Cow </i>- may just mean cow, but may also mean an unpleasant man, woman, or situation. These things may also be called progressively, <i>a fair cow</i>, and a <i>fair adjectival cow. </i> <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,38</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... <i>cow</i>: disagreeable; <i>a fair cow</i>: very disagreeable <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115</i>)

>  By and large ... there is remarkably little difference in English here and in Britain. That is ... after making due allowance for such atrocities as `joker', `it's a fair cow', the ubiquitous `Eh' at the end of every other sentence, and the normal vowel flattening. <i>(Nov 1950,Here & Now,9</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>fair cow </i> <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <i>fair cow n. (Sl.)</i> obnoxious or unpleasant person. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  <i>cow</i>: woman <i>a fair cow</i>: unfair, not pleasant; see <i>crumb </i> <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>cow ... NZ & Austral. sl.</i> troublesome or unpleasant person or thing (<i>a fair cow</i>). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,171</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 258]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an annoying task, event or impediment to work.

>  <b>MARSH</b> 'A fair nark' or more emphatically, 'a fair cow' is anything inexpressibly tedious or baffling. <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,174</i>)

>  <b>CURTIS</b> It was a fair cow of a ridge to follow. <i>(1996,Beneath the Mountain Mist,73</i>)

### fair crack of the whip
 
 <i>n.</i> a fair go

>  "The manner in which the defence counsel conducted the trial and the nature of the summing up, left the appellant feeling he had not had a fair crack of the whip," Chief Justice Eichelbaum said. <i>(Dec 18, 1998,Dominion,6</i>)

>  We've done the decent thing and called the Russkies up and told them the rules are there for a purpose and what they've done just isn't a fair crack of the whip. <i>(Feb 2, 2000,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  Hey. Fair crack of the whip, 92. Don't just slope off and leave a joker in the pooh. <i>(Jan 2, 1993,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  "What the plaintiff complains about is that, when it came to consideration of reapppointment, she did not get a fair crack of the whip and was deprived of the chance of making a out a case for staying in the job," he said. <i>(Aug 3, 2001,Dominion,6</i>)

### fair die
 
 <i>n.</i> an assertion (or question) of a doubtful truth; (? fair do + dinky-die qv). [(children's) -->ca1935]

### fair dinkum
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  "Fair dinkum.. my mate was that fed up and ashamed that he drove back to the garage through all the back streets he could find." <i>(Oct 17, 1928,NZ Free Lance,18</i>)

>  Fair dinkum, a man should be thumped for knowing you blokes. <i>(Sep 20, 1943,NZEF Times,III/117 : 10</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> genuine, real [straight dinkum 1940 citation is of intensifying adverb (truly)] (See [straight dinkum](../S#straight-dinkum))

>  Williams was an "Ak Emma" and Henrys was a "Dink", a fair dinkum "digger". <i>(Nov 27, 1926,NZ Observer,Xmas Annual : 28</i>)

>  The people that are fair dinkum about wanting something generally ask the funeral director if they can talk to me first... <i>(Oct 11, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  The butcher would say no, it was the fair dinkum truth. <i>(1977,NZ Listener Short Stories,20</i>) [NOTES: 1945 Sargeson in 1977 NZ Listener Short Stories]

>  I'm fair dinkum windy! I'm scared stiff! I might get shot or wounded. <i>(Jul 4, 1940,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> He realised that things weren't going to be too good, once that swarm [sc. of bees] cut loose, and he wanted the job over fairly quickly, so he told Bill to get stuck in and give her a fair dinkum shake. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,67</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> Now these blokes like Riverina Dick, the established swagmen, they didn't seek company - the fair dinkum swagger, he liked to be on his own - but they're prepared to take on an apprentice. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,68</i>)

### fair dos
 
 <i>exclam.</i> indicating a request for fair play; or ironically) doubt at an exaggeration. [[Spec. or extended sense of fair equitable, reasonable. cf OED2 aj 10.]] (See [also fair go](../A#also-fair-go))

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> [Sailor Jack speaks] He cut right under me again. `Fair doos! you hamimal!' says I. And with that, hup I jumps and goes for him straight, and we had a set to. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,140</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> fair do yet another appeal, sometimes fair dos; eg `Oh, fair dos, don't I get a turn?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,44</i>)

### Fair Go
 
 <i>n.</i> a long-running TV consumer programme

>  .. <i>Calendar</i>, has been leaning over the fence and spinning yarns, making it New Zealand's longest running television series by about 12 years (beating the popular <i>fair go</i>.) <i>(Feb 27, 2005,Sunday Star Times,Escape p1</i>)

>  Last year 3000 letters poured into <i>Fair Go</i> <i>(May 17, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

### fair go
 
 <i>exclam.</i> give me a break

>  <b>WILSON</b> Fair go. It really hurts. If you listen, I'll tell you why. <i>(1976,Pacific Star,8</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> fair go the most popular appeal of all; originally the call in two-up game to indicate everything was in order, the betting was closed and the spinner would toss the coins. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,44</i>)

>  Fair go! A while back there I was seriously beginning to think that this old world had a chance of lasting a few more years. <i>(Jul 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also fair go exclamation](../A#also-fair-go-exclamation))

>  What I want to know is whether they reckon this bull-ring stuff is a fair go? <i>(Sep 20, 1943,NZEF Times,III/117 : 6</i>)

>  Has anyone bothered to ask how such draconian measures were implemented in a small country so firmly wedded to the principle of "a fair go"? <i>(Mar 10, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  Stewart did not get a "fair go." <i>(May 27, 1924,Otago Witness,48</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> She didn't want any mollycoddling ...; she hated burdening herself with a child as much as he did, but she was going to have a `fair go'. <i>(1929,Riven,184</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> There is nothing lousy about him, and I always believe in giving a man a fair go and that's why I object to the word crayfish and I think you fellows are making it too hot when you say that. He is a shrewd head, but I think he would give a man a fair go although he is a base-walloper. Well, you have had a fair go behind the line and it's just about time you turned hostile. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> He wanted to give the girl a fair go for [the business]. [`an honest run for her money')] <i>(1939,Bushman Burke,186</i>)

>  <b>FAIRBURN</b> [To editor Listener] Fraser has, you say, given you a fair-go. <i>(Jul 25, 1947,Letters,167,1981 ed.</i>)

>  (Havelock) I do all the work and get no thanks.It's not a fair go, is it really now. <i>(1949,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>LAWSON</b> [? Austr] This is the fairest town on the Coast - no cliques, no clans, just a fair go - That's our motto, a fair go. [=`a just chance or deal'] <i>(1951,Gold in their Hearts,94</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> `... But if you buy a dog or two, and give it a fair go, you will be all right'.[sic] <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,15</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Break it down, Evan. ... Give him a fair go.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,213</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> On putting the bottle down after having had a fair go, he came to light with the idea that someday the track would become a road linking Puketitiri with the Inland Patea road, ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,42</i>)

>  <b>MacCALLUM</b> First I plant the cabbage and the lettuce, but the slugs and snails have a fair go [`a good run, chance'] and eat them all up. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,1</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> `You go ahead and take the gate, and I'll hunt them up the race,' Mike said. ... `You're hunting them through too fast!' Legge shouted back. `Give a man a fair go, can't you!' [26] Legge and Ustice hunted the sheep forward, and they ran nicely for a while. <i>(1970,Tat,25</i>)

>  A fair go is a FAIR CHANCE. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> An interjection, occas. a mere intensifier `be fair, reasonable!', do you mean it?, expressing: (a) an assertion of the the serious truth, genuineness, honesty etc. of the speaker's statement, suggestion, or general attitude; (b) sometimes, according to the tone used, an ironical questioning of the truth etc. of another's statement etc., or as a protest against some action or command felt to be unjust, severe etc. [[It is popular with children. I heard it first used in 1941 when I came to the North Island to secondary school, and thought it a strange expression, as fair do! was the main form in Havelock. fair go! has a much wider current popular use, as slang or colloq, than any of its counterparts: fair buck, do, (spin) etc.]]

>  <b>GASKELL</b> `No, fair go Bomb. This is dinkum.' <i>(1944,All Part of the Game,26</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Gaskell `Pig and Whistle' in 1978 All Part of the Game 26]

>  I'll not say which side I'll be barracking for. `No, fair go,' said Bob `It's just general knowledge.' `I mean it,' I said. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,9</i>) [NOTES: (1944) Gaskell in 1977 N.Z. Listener Short Stories 9]

>  <b>GASKELL</b> `I bet he carries a calendar.' `No, fair go,' said Bob seriously... `I bet he carried a calendar,' grinned Mac to me. `No, fair go,' said Bob seriously. `It's just general knowledge.' ibid 23 No, fair go, Bomb. This is dinkum. <i>(1947,Big Game,11</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> ... I've heard it over and over and over and I'm sick of hearing it fair go. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,197,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GASKELL</b> `But Auntie, fair go, will he be here next year?' `I suppose he will. ...' <i>(1984,Some Other Country,90</i>) [NOTES: 1950 Gaskell in 1984 Some Other Country 90]

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> `Right, here's a bargain! ... Fair go?' `What does `fair go' mean?' <i>(1960,Incense to Idols,24</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `Fair go. The whole idea's up the wop. I can think of a hundred explanations straight off.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,212</i>)

>  `You can't go out to the pictures tonight ...' `Aw, fair go, Dad - all me mates are.' <i>(1962,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>WEBBER</b> `He [sc. Ombudsman] is supposed to look after the interests of the ordinary Joes like you and me,' I said. ... `Fair go?' said the Bloke. <i>(1962,Look No Hands!,123</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> Don't interfere. Leave the two contestants to have fair fight between themselves. .in 4;`All right everyone! Stand back! Fair go!' Be sure you present the issue in a fair way as you describe it. `Fair go now. that wasn't all that was said ...' Be fair in dealing out the penalty, the rebuke, or the blame. `So I said `I think the lads have had about enough. Fair go, they've had all the punishment they deserve.' ...<i>Ibid.</i> 326 A claim to what is right and just, anywhere, anytime. .in 4;`Fair go! Give him a chance, stand back everyone.' <i>Ibid.</i> 327 Really. Is that so ... Does the same duty as `I see', `Oh' and similar signals given by the hearer to show interest in what the other is saying. .in 4; I've caught 3 and 4 pounders here myself'. `Fair go? I've never put a line in here, never thought of it.' You surprise me. I would not have expected this. `... and you're to be in charge of that section.' `Me? I'm to be in charge? Fair go? This's all a big surprise to me! <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],325</i>)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> But fair go. Those peas were murder on your thumb after a while, ... <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,55</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> `Is that a true yarn, Crump?' I gasped. ... `Fair go! he said ... `Top it if you can!' <i>(1964,Stag Party,57</i>)

>  `That's why. You should see his gut, fair go, like a possum.' <i>(1976,Islands,15/53</i>) [NOTES: 1976 Evans (Girl Story Story) in Islands 15/53]

>  <b>HALL</b> The Boss has got promotion. Going to Ag. and Fish. JIM. Fair go? HUGH. True. JIM. Beauty. About time. <i>(1977,Glide Time,36</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `That vindictive, she is. Fair go - I wouldn't put it past her. Now.' <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,202</i>)

>  fair go?: honest fair go, mate: take it easy <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: Goldie Brown Collection]

>  <b>STEWART</b> ... so there was old Jacko, I mean, on his ear in front of the microphone in a plate full of pea, pie and pud. Fair go. <i>(1981,Hot & Copper Sky,151</i>)

### fair spin
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of fair go

>  We're getting a fair enough spin. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),3(6) : 15</i>)

### fair suck of the sauce bottle
 
 <i>phr.</i> usu. (and in Aust.) 'a fair go' (cf. fair suck of the sav), but in cit. below more like '(more than) a fair share'

>  But Coutts and Holmes have had a fair suck of the sauce bottle from the public purse and/or quangos, and to put themselves into their hypothetical moral glasshouses is only inviting the ridicule and further contempt they both so richly deserve, <i>(Dec 26, 2002,Dominion Post,A14</i>)

### fair suck of the sav
 
 <i>phr.</i> variant of fair go

>  The New Zealand Exchange (NZX) is trying to give first timers - investors and companies alike - a fair suck of the sav under its new regime. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Hawke's Bay Today,A13</i>)

>  Fair suck of the sav <i>(Aug 17, 2003,Sunday Star Times,16</i>) [NOTES: headline for wine column re sauvignon blancs]

>  Contrast [Kopu] with Myles, a middle-aged white male, who had already had a suck of the parliamentary sav, who had changed parties serially for reasons few people can now remember, and a person, moreover, of such garrulousness that any leader could reasonably expect to be driven barmy by him. <i>(Jan 9, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Why, even on their holidays, a whole swag of them went all the way to Auckland to suck savs with onetime caucus pariah Phil Goff. <i>(Apr 10, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  The sudden cooling in US-NZ relations.. led directly to the attacks on New Zealand consulates and tourism offices in several North American cities, and the withdrawal from Washington of ambassador Jim Bolger, whose offhand remark to a reporter - "New Zealand hasn't had a fair suck of the sav" - also had a worsening effect on relations. <i>(Mar 27, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  However, pub owner Ray Sharawara is asking for a fair suck of the sav, and reckons politicians shouldn't assume hotel drinkers are responsible for the mess. <i>(Jul 9, 2002,Independent Herald (Wellington),17</i>)

>  [Hdg] Suck of the free sav now treat of the past. <i>(Aug 16, 2000,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  By comparison, today's twenty- and thirtysomethings feel (not to put too fine a point on it) that they have not had a fair suck of the sav. <i>(Apr 15, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  The Australian electoral system delivers power to the Liberal loser, opposition to the Labor winner, and denies One Nation's Pauline Hanson her fair suck of the sav - if she was running in NZ under MMP, she'd probably hold the balance of power. <i>(Oct 17, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  "All we want is a fair suck of the sav," she said. <i>(Oct 27, 1992,Dominion,1</i>)

He said 'well, yinno, when I was a young boy at night, yinno, we'd go down to the station, to see who was on the train and have a bit of a yarn.' <i>(Dec 1999,North & South,94</i>)

### fairy lantern
 
 <i>n.</i> the plant <i>Thismia rodwayi</i>

For most of the year, the plant exists as a white root devoid of chlorophyll and deriving its nutrients from an association with a mycorrhizal fungus. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/60</i>)

### fairy prion
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SANSOM</b> With [the titi] were scatterings of the smaller titi-wainui, the Fairy Prions, and a sprinkling of kuaka, the Southern Diving Petrels, which ducked and rose on the outskirts. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,91</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> FAIRY PRION (<i>Pachyptila turtur</i>), Titi Wainui of the Maoris, is the small dove grey petrel which skims the surface of the sea in its energetic quest for food. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,79</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> FAIRY PRION <i>Pachyptila turtur Maori name</i>: Titiwainui (Stewart Island). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,40</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Prion, fairy .. .. titi-wainui .. ..<i>Pachyptila turtur</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>51. Pachyptila turtur</i> (Kuhl, 1820) <i>Fairy Prion (Titi Wainui) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,25,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Fairy Prion Pachyptila turtur</i> (KUHL, 1820) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Titi-wainui, narrow-billed prion</i>. ... Fairy prions patrol the seas in large, loose flocks. ... Fairy prions are one of New Zealand's most abundant petrels ... <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,87</i>)

>  Over the past three years 240 fairy prion chicks have been transferred to Mana Island from Stephens Island. <i>(May 2004,Footnotes,No. 21/6</i>)

>  [Caption] A fairy prion chick, one of 40 nestlings brought to Mana Island off the Wellington coast, from Stephens Island in Cook Strait. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,304/8</i>)

>  All 100 fairy prion chicks transferred from Stephens Island to Mana Island in January fledged successfully after being handfed sardine smoothies daily for three weeks. <i>(May 2003,Footnotes,No. 19/7</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### fairy tern
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) an endangered native bird.

>  Two endangered shorebirds, fairy terns and New Zealand dotterel, breed on North Island beaches over the summer months when they are particularly vulnerable to disturbance by 4WD vehicles on the beaches. <i>(Nov 1999,AA Directions,50</i>)

>  Moon was the first in New Zealand to document the nesting and feeding habits of not only the morepork, kingfisher and kereru, but the very rare fairy tern (down to only 12 pairs at the time), the white-faced heron (when it first colonised New Zealand in 1969 after a drought in his native Australia), the shy reef heron ... <i>(Feb 1999,North & South,69</i>)

>  <b>MONCRIEFF</b> <i>(1925,NZ Birds,24</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> FAIRY TERN<i> Sterna nereis </i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,163</i>)

>  <i>203. Sterna nereis</i> (Gould, 1843) <i>Fairy Tern</i> New Caledonia, Australia and Tasmania; New Zealand, rare and local; a few pairs breeding North Auckland. <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,55,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Fairy Tern Sterna nereis</i> (GOULD, 1843) At the turn of the century the fairy tern was a common bird along the coasts of both main islands. ... Today it is rare. ... OTHER NAME: <i>Tara-iti. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,230</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The rarest tern breeding in New Zealand is the fairy tern (<i>Sterna nereis</i>,) reported from Lake Ellesmere in 1944, and breeding also, sparingly, in Northland and a few other places. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,267</i>)

>  For the first time in this district Mrs. Moncrieff saw a little fairy tern on 7th March, and on 4th April a large Caspian tern put in an appearance. <i>(Apr 1, 1926,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  According to the observations of Mr. A. C. O'Connor, of Wellington, the fairy tern, black-fronted tern, and black-billed gulls have been seen by him in the North Island during the past few years. <i>(May 21, 1932,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  One of our rarest birds, the New Zealand fairy tern, had a successful summer breeding with 11 chicks surviving, including one at Papakanui Spit on the Kaipara Harbour for the first time in five years. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,56</i>)

>  The New Zealand fairy tern, once widespread in the North island, has teetered on the brink of extinction since the 1970s .. Fairy terns nest on shell and sand banks just above high tide .. The fairy tern now breeds at only four nesting sites, at Papakanui Spit, Pakiri, Waipu and Mangawhai. <i>(Jan 11, 2007,NZ Herald,A4</i>)

### fairy trumpet
 
 <i>n.</i> a foxglove

There they sat down on the blowing grass and pink `fairy trumpets' or on the white and yellow daisies and dandelions, and while his father had a chew of tobacco, Old Tar (who was very young Tar indeed then) sat quiet ... `Wot do you mean?' asked the little boy one day, sitting, his legs straight out, making finger gloves of the fairy trumpets. <i>(1974,Undiscovered Country,299</i>) [NOTES: 1913 Mansfield Old Tar in 1974 Gordon Undiscovered Country 299]

### fale
 
 <i>n.</i>

### fall
 
 <i>n.</i> a strip or wedge of ground split off in one piece by undermining the working face of an excavation in gum-bearing soil. [(gumdigging)]

>  <b>Reed</b> He purchased the land and put a number of men on to it to take it down in `falls' and remove the gum like a crop of potatoes. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,104</i>) [NOTES: [c. 1900]]



 
 <i>v.</i> to fell (trees) [[1819 see cit falling axe-->.]]

>  <b>RIDGWAY</b> Large quantities of bush have this season been `fallen.' <i>Ibid.</i> 67 When to fall this bush? When to burn off? <i>(1860,Voices from Auckland,67</i>)

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> I would not be in the least surprised if I were to learn that they could fall a tree and split it into planks <i>(Feb 24, 1865,Journal</i>) [NOTES: (MS ATB)]

>  <b>MAY</b> We had a dread of commencing to fall bush. <i>(1869,Guide to Farming in NZ,18</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> [sc. bush] But the black birch of this country takes the cake for toughness and shortness of grain and stem. It is tough to fall and tough to saw, and ... it has tobe blasted with powder to get a crack in it to put a wedge in. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,33,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1872 in c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 33]

>  <b>BAINES</b> A bushman ...entered into a contract with me to fall, cross-cut, and roll handy to the creek's side 200,000 feet of timber. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,170</i>)

>  Sawmillers confind men to `fall' kauri only during these two months.... During these two months, July and August, let them `ring' the trees, that is cut out a ring of bark and sap 3 or 4 inches wide round all the trees they intend to cut down or `fall' during the next 12 months. ... I have no doubt that these serious defects arise from the practice of `falling' the kauri all through the year. <i>(1874,TrNZI,7/186</i>) [NOTES: (Firth `On Forest Culture')]

>  <b>KIRK</b> ON THE BEST SEASON FOR FALLING TIMBER IN NEW ZEALAND. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,23</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> In felling bush, or `falling' it, as we say out here, advantage is taken of the lay of the land. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/186</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> ... wood - `falled' in our bit of standing bush. <i>(1904,Life in NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1904 Baughan `Life in NZ' in 1902 Mar Empire Review 175]

>  <b>ALLEY & HALL</b> The bush was then felled, or `falled' ... <i>(1941,Farmer in New Zealand, The,99</i>)

>  fall ... NZ & Austral. cut down (trees etc.). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,269</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng.Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 176.]]



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Blue, Currie, and Littlecote were occupied in clearing the bricks away prior to removing the `pins' to fall the wall. <i>(Jul 22, 1911,NZ Truth,317/6/3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the process of felling trees; also the trees or timber cut down

>  The trees are simply cut down ... and piled round the outside, to form a rude fence to keep out cattle or pigs. We have enclosed about 20 falls within the last two days, and will have all the area clear of wood tomorrow; ... <i>(Sep 13, 1849,Letters from Otago,36,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> The axes had been ringing out cheerily from early morning, and the `falls' had been frequent and far-sounding. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,94</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> see cit. 1933. [also stockwhip (station)] (See [also stockwhip](../A#also-stockwhip))

>  Fall ... (2) A plain strip of leather at the end of the lash of a whip to which the cracker is attached. (C.) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The [bullock-driver's] whip was rawhide plaited sinnet, about six feet long, thick at the upper end ... but tapering nearly to a point, to which was attached a narrow strip of rawhide, called `the fall', finishing off with a twisted `cracker' made of `weetan' or dressed flax in place of whipcord ... . <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,42</i>) [NOTES: [? quoting W.H.S. Roberts Diary 1856 check]]



 
 <i>n.</i> (mining) in phrase run out of fall: to have insufficient (?slope ?pitch ?gradient) to allow a tail-race to operate from the lower level of a mine.

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Many of the sluicing claims ran `out of fall' (that is the ground being worked was too deep to dispose of the tailings). <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,48</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a short unplaited leather strip to which the cracker of a stockwhip is attached.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The fall was made from a tapered piece of green hide. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,151</i>)

>  .. the length and weight of the blood-stained, butt-end and lead-loaded 'fall' of an old stockwhip .. 'Do you belt the bull with that?' I asked, jerking my thumb at the said waddy. <i>(Dec 31, 1964,NZ Farmer,14</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> fall: the lash of a stockwhip is of plaited leather. At the end of this is the fall, a short unplaited strip to which the cracker is attached. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,29</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> A narrow unplaited thong at the end of this [whip tail] is the fall and it carries the crack or cracker. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,79</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to clear (land) by falling (trees)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> That bit o' bush paddock I falled myself/ <i>(1903,Reuben & Other Poems,88</i>)

>  `Can we not all admire the stout-hearted man who goes on to the bush, falls the land, and with sweat of his brow carves a home for himself and family out of the wilderness that's left?' <i>(Jun 25, 1910,NZ Truth,261/1/4</i>)

### fall muster
 
 <i>n.</i> autumn muster [SI Farming.]

>  Fall. (1) Autumn. For some reason station people usually speak of fall and fall-muster, though so far as I know the word is not in common use elsewhere in New Zealand. (C.) ... <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They were in dangerous snow country, where every sheep left in the fall muster could be written off. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,118</i>)

>  April: Fall muster commences [on Glenaray, Canterbury] <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,356</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> The fall muster is one of the big occasions in a high-country year. It is ... one of the few occasions on which New Zealanders use the word `fall' instead of autumn. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,118</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> For several weeks after the fall muster they carted quantities of our material. <i>(1965,Skippers Road,40</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> In the autumn, in late April or May, would come the `Fall Muster'. Incidentally I never found out how this American term for autumn came to be used in the back country of New Zealand. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,21</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Jack Sinclair's wife wrote: In 1933 the musterers started the fall muster from Jack Mac's on 8 April and landed the 18,000-odd, the first mob, on the station on 18 April. <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,110</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> [caption] Horses were being shod, some quite unwillingly, for the fall muster. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations NI,5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an autumn muster [when stock are brought down from the high country to pasture that is safe from snowfall.]

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> In the same way that it's a tradition in the Navy, it's [rum] become a tradition on the fall muster. <i>(1983,Stockman Country,12</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They were in dangerous snow country, where every sheep left in the fall muster could be written off. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,118</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> The fall muster is one of the big occasions in a high country year. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,118</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Horses were being shod, some quite unwillingly, for the fall muster. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,5</i>)

### false bottom
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  ... the existence of `false bottoms.' <i>(1863,AJHR,D-6-15</i>) [NOTES: (Pyke Goldfield Reports)]

>  <b>MOFFATT</b> ... as the bar [in the river] commenced to dry in patches [as the river dropped], our hopes were high. The prospects were splendid. The wash was about six inches thick, lying on a false bottom, consisting of a thin layer of rusty cement, and under that thin white gravel [at Gabriel's Gully]. <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,58,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Dredging through false bottom of green sandy pug, very hard pipe clay bottom, about 8 colours to the dish, under the false bottom. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,39</i>) [NOTES: 1906 Dec 7 in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 39]

>  The basement rock is known to alluvial miners as `reef bottom'; and the clays or other material interposed between the wash and the bed-rock as `false bottom.' <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.5 (NS) 37</i>) [NOTES: (Park, Cromwell)]

>  <b>HEINZ</b> False bottom: A bottom on which gold has been deposited above the true bottom. <i>(1947,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> In some places it was found that the original claims had been worked on a `false' bottom of tough, stony clay, ... <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,84,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> False bottoms fooled even seasoned diggers, particularly at Kaniere. Claims considered worked out were frequently re-worked at a different level. This explains the many small rushes which occurred on `old ground'. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,195,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> False bottom: a floor of compact clay or cemented material appearing to be the base of the alluvial deposit but beneath which lies a further, perhaps richer, deposit and the true bottom. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,112</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> FALSE BOTTOM: a movable or floor in a cradle, sluice-box, stamper-box or puddling machine; a stratum on which alluvial gold rests but which has other bottoms below it. The last bottom is the `true' one. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1962 ed.</i>)

>  The terraces on either side of.. the Kawarau river, if examinedi minutely, will show that the only deposits of auriferous wash have been made at various periods upon clayey sand-drifts (called by the miners "false bottoms"). <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,12</i>)

 <i>n.</i> see cit.

>  <b>MAY</b> DUFFER: applied to a rush, a shaft, or a claim generally. A failure. Synonyms were `shicer', `stringer' or, in the case of a false rush, `store-keepers' rush'. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,526,1962 ed.</i>)

### family allowance
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  FAMILY ALLOWANCES (Title) New Zealand was one of the first, if not the first, country to adopt the principle embodied in the Family Allowances Act, 1926, of the State bearing to some extent the responsibility for the well-being of the families of those who were in poorer circumstances.] Formerly a payment made by the State to a parent for the support of a child or children; ?now incorporated in the Domestic Purposes Benefit. Also called family allowance. <i>(1950,Growth and Development of Social Security,29</i>)

>  <b>DOW HALL</b> A cash allowance paid to a mother for each dependent child. <i>(1955,Portrait of NZ,179</i>) [NOTES: W3]

### family benefit
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> Live in a state house, raise forcibly-educated children/ Receive family benefits, and standard wages ... <i>(1950,Imaginary Islands,25</i>) [NOTES: (`Secular Litany')]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> `Family benefits' were in 1938 paid only for the second and subsequent children of poor families; in 1946 they were given for the first child as well, in 1941 the means test was abolished and the-then-large sum of ten shillings was paid to the mother of every child under sixteen (or, in some cases, under eighteen) in the country. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,262</i>)

>  <i>(1979,Heinemann Dictionary of NZ Quotations</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> Family Benefit. New Zealand PAYS its residents FOR HAVING CHILDREN <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,39</i>)

>  family benefit NZ weekly allowance paid by State to parents of child(ren). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,270</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 176]]
