### Family Court
 
 <i>n.</i> a division of a District Court which administers family law. [Not in OED; seems to have originated in US (see W3)]

>  family court NZ & Austral. court administering family law. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,270</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Chan points out that the Family Court has a lower standard of proof for findings of violence than the criminal court, which would make the statistical reporting problematic. She says far from masking abuse, the court system involves a raft of professional assessment processes that can and do take it further, outside of the Family Court system. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  <b>Charlotte Grimshaw</b> The Family Court had taken the boy from her and awarded full custody to the father. <i>(2007,Six Pack 2, The,87</i>)

>  For the first time since the Family Court was created, almost a quater of a century ago, the media will be permitted to attend children's cases as of [sic] right. <i>(Dec 16, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### family farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm owned and farmed by several family members through several generations.

>  The original block was 184 ha, since supplemented with 55.2 ha from the family farm next door. <i>(Sep 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,16</i>)

>  Let's look at this sacred New Zealand cow of passing on the family farm. <i>(Dec 9, 1976,NZ Farmer,45</i>)

>  He comes from a family farm near Inglewood in Taranaki. <i>(Jun 1982,NZ Dairy Exporter,39</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> A good number of Lane's former staff will become farmers in their own right, many by taking over the family farm. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,81</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> And that sums up one of the greatest strengths of New Zealand agriculture - that it's been built up by a nationwide network of family farms. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,102</i>)

>  <b>YEREX</b> The term 'family farm' has been such an accepted part of the language in this country that it is used without thought of its true meaning. <i>(1989,Empire of the Dairy Farmers,43</i>)

>  <b>YEREX</b> They were 'family farms' because the whole family was fully involved. <i>(1989,Empire of the Dairy Farmers,56</i>)

>  Is the family farm becoming a thing of the past? <i>(Jun 27, 1995,NZ Farmer,29</i>)

>  Miss Aitchison helps market organic produce off the family farm, which has been organic for 13 years. <i>(Oct 28, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  The Coplestones moved from the 600-acre family farm at Pukengahu, east of Stratford, to this 1100-acre block. <i>(Dec 22, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

>  .. Ben McNeill's ideas are helping to transform the family farm from traditional livestock to a mixture of forestry, animals and possibly orcharding. <i>(May 2002,Growing Today,9</i>)

### family farmer
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a member of a family actively involved in farming.

>  His [John Barrett's] grandmother was the last of the family farmers on Kapiti Island. <i>(Nov 2007,VUW Continuing Education,Summer 2007/2008/1</i>)

### family farming
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the farming of a single property by many family members or through generations.

>  Family farming is still the backbone of New Zealand agriculture. <i>(Jul 27, 1995,NZ Farmer,29</i>)

### family group conference
 
 <i>n.</i> a formal meeting of family members to make proposals and plans for dealing with children and young persons in need of care and protection, or who have committed offences. [Jim Cameron writes : Introduced by the Children Young Persons and their Families Act 1989 as part of the procedure for the care and protection of young people.]

>  At the heart of this is the family group conference, which brings together offenders, victims, police, government agencies, family and others to deal with the criminal behaviour. <i>(Mar 6, 2002,Dominion,13</i>)

### family home
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [joint family home](../J#joint-family-home))

>  FAMILY HOMES PROTECTION ACT, 1895... Any owner of land.. may settle such land.. not exceeding, with all improvements one thousand five hundred pounds in value, as a family home. 68(1) The settlor during his lifetime may regulate the occupation of the family home by himself and his family as he thinks fit. (2) After his death such occupation shall be regulated pursuant to any direction contained in his will. (3) So far as no such direction occurs.. the widow or husband of the deceased settlor shall, while living, regulate such occupation. <i>(1895,NZ Statutes,59 Vict. 20/66</i>)

### family protection
 
 <i>n.</i> a scheme whereby certain close relatives of a deceased person may apply to the court for a larger share of the estate than the will or the rules of intestate succesion give them [Jim Cameron writes : A NZ innovation dating from 1900 (known originally as testators' family maintenance). See Family Protection Act 1908, and now Family Protection Act 1955. A similar scheme in England, introduced in 1926, is known as <i>family provision</i>. (I suspect that in recent popular usage, <i>family protection</i> may relate rather to the protection against violence that is the purpose of the Domestic Violence Act.)]

>  Even the Dictionary of New Zealand English (DNZE) (1997) is patchy in its coverage of legal terms, having for example no entry for disputes tribunal, family protection, incorporated society, Maori option, protection order, resource consent. The most recent edition of Butterworths New Zealand Law Dictionary (1995) is useful but commonly gives specialist definitions taken from the interpretation sections of statutes <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,1</i>)

### family services
 
 <i>n.</i> abbreviation of Department of Child, Youth & Family Services (CYFS)?

>  A few months ago, the family services workers at the Kelvin Rd Whanau Centre in Papakura had a call from the principal of the school next door. <i>(Jul 6, 2005,NZ Herald,A19</i>)

### family shirker
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) [WW1]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Under the sub-clause in the Conscription Act, popularly known as the Family Shirkers' Clause, men belonging to families, no member of which had gone to the War, could be called up without having appeared in the ballot. <i>(1939,We Will Not Cease,10</i>)

>  The total number called up under the "Family Shirker" section (35) of the Military Service Act is 2851. <i>(Mar 21, 1917,Otago Witness</i>)

>  An Act to make Further Provision for the Raising and Maintenance of Expeditionary Forces during the Present War. <i>Ibid.</i> 95 35. (1). If the Minister of Defence is satisfied with respect to any family that it consists of or includes two or more brothers who belong to the First Division of the Reserve,and are not permanently unfit for military service, he may.. give or cause to be given notice to all or any of those brothers to show cause before a Military Service Board why they should not be called up for service with the Expeditionary Force. <i>(1916,NZ Statutes,7 Geo.V 8/84</i>) [NOTES: The act to which the term "family shirker" applies]

>  Interesting details are available in reference to the operation of Clause 35 of the Expeditionary Force Act, popularly known as the "Family Shirker" Clause. When it was discovered that the clause was to come into operation, the Defence Department was flooded with names of alleged "family shirkers." <i>(Jul 14, 1919,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

### family trust
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [The lawyers] say many family trusts would fall under the new buzz-word in trust-busting - the "alter ego" trust... [He] explains the concept of an alter ego trust as a trust under which "control never really leaves the control of the settlor and the settlor treats the trust as their alter ego". <i>(Oct 24, 2004,Sunday Times (NZ),D7</i>)

>  Typically many trusts are "house trusts" containing little more than a family home. <i>(Oct 24, 2004,Sunday Times (NZ),D7</i>)

### faminer
 
 <i>n.</i> someone who participates in the 48-hour famine fast [anonymous contribution; seen on famine receipt book]

### fan
 
 <i>n.</i> A deposit, fan- or cone-shaped, of alluvial shingle brought down when a river is in flood; a similar deposit or slip on a hillside. [See 1846 cit. Also called alluvial fan, gravel fan, shingle fan.]

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... Dr Hector and myself, in drawing up a synopsis of the geological formations of New Zealand, have adopted for the formation of those subaerial accumulations the expression `Fan', for those of regular watercourses; and of `Half-cone', for those of intermittent mountain torrents, and we shall for the future use these two expressions. <i>(1864,Report on the Formation of the Canterbury Plains,19</i>)

>  In previous publications (amongst others, `Report ... Canterbury Plains, 1864' page 22 et seq) I have shown how in post-pliocene times from the material brought down by the enormous glacier torrents forming huge shingle fans at the foot of the glaciers, two bars were thrown across the sea; <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/55</i>)

>  Content with our day's walk, we camped on the large fan of Pass Creek. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/352</i>) [NOTES: (Parke)]

>  [The Canterbury Plains] have been formed by the overlapping fans of great glacier streams ... <i>(1909,TrNZI,40/38</i>) [NOTES: (Speight)]

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> One word now universally accepted in geological literature, viz., `fan', was first used by [Haast]. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  Fan: Hill creeks in Canterbury and Otago bring a lot of shingle down with them when they are in flood. On steep hillsides this shingle shingle spreads on each side of the creek, narrow at the top and wide at the bottom. This natural feature is called a fan. In time tussocks grow over the shingle and some large fans of 500 to 1000 or more acres contain very good land. (C.) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <b>DAWSON</b> In the Cook Strait area the grey-leaved spaniard (<i>Aciphylla squarrosa</i>) is also a common plant on fans. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,158</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... extensive surveys ...; made for railway and other purposes, have confirmed in a remarkable degree my views concerning the `fan' character of the deposits of the principal rivers in every respect. <i>(1874,TrNZI,6/241</i>) [NOTES: (Haast)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a group of leaves, or clump, of the NZ flax growing in the shape of a fan.

>  During growth the young leaves arise from the centre of the [flax-] plants, so that the outer leaves of a `fan' are the oldest, ... <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/274</i>) [NOTES: (Miller)]

>  Egg-laying is carried out during the night. ... A favourite place is between the tree edges of leaves at the base of fans, where a single row may be inserted. <i>(1931,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,13/165</i>) [NOTES: (Gourlay)]

### fan country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mountain country] land adjacent to steep hills that has been built up from gullying or wash-downs from higher altitudes.

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> It was proposed to subsidise the topdressing and oversowing of 1450 acres on the uncultivatible fan country and on part of the hill ewe block. <i>(1970,Soil Conservation and Land Retirement,7</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> The fifth year was to include 120 chains of fencing on another hill block .. and the cultivation of turnips on a further 80 acres of native tussock or the better fan country. <i>(1970,Soil Conservation and Land Retirement,10</i>)

### fan mussel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kukluku ... Atrina zelandica, fan mussel. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,181</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> As well as ten small mytilid mussels, New Zealand has the large horse mussel or fan shell, <i>Atrina pectinata</i>, which belongs to the related pen shell or fan-mussel family (Pinnidae). <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,169</i>)

### fan shell
 
 <i>n.</i> [Atrina pectinata zelandica]

>  The sea is just at the end of the garden, and there is such a nice beach, where you can get very pretty shells - muttonfish shells, fan shells, trumpet shells, cat's-eyes, and heaps of others. <i>(Feb 18, 1903,Otago Witness,67</i>)

### fan(-leaved) fern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> (And) <i>G. flabellata</i> fan-leaved fern <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

>  <b>FIELD</b> (And) <i>G. flabellata</i> fan-fern <i>(1890,Ferns of NZ</i>)

### fancy
 
 <i>adj.</i> a grade of fruit for sale

### fancy biscuit
 
 <i>n.</i> an ornamental or fine biscuit as opposed to plain. cf. OED2 C. 1 a. [Spec. use of fancy : ca1941 Orsman's Fancy Mixed (a mixture of `fancy' biscuits)]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> She bought a bottle of soft-drink, tore the cake-bag down the seam and ate the fancy-biscuits for lunch. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,76</i>)

### fanfish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> Sometimes known as `Fanfish'. The New Zealand Batfish bears no relationship or resemblance to the several Australian fish of the same name [sc batfish ?]. <i>Ibid.</i> 31 FANFISH: See Batfish <i>Ibid.</i> 71 FANFISH See Batfish <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,27</i>)

>  [species name] fanfish .. [scientific name] Pterycombus petersii .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### fang
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BAUME</b> Paul gulped his tea and Tapere fanged the bread. <i>(1933,Half Caste,61</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to speed (?)

>  It isn't worth trying to explain to them (truthfully) that I am by far the slowest fanger of the lot - the rest have been fanging themselves silly for decades, while I am strictly a novice. <i>(Dec 26, 2001,Dominion,13</i>)

>  The people spat and cursed: "But the bleedin' cars will still bottleneck after fanging it up the flash new road, and merging into a 50km zone at Pukerua Bay!" <i>(Jul 19, 2001,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  The road trip has been in the planning for years, but one day they seize the moment and off they fang through the provinces of their native Argentina to Chile... <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

### fang spanner
 
 <i>n.</i> a long-handled spanner with elongated jaw-prongs. [(railway gangers')]

>  Not for me the noisy proline/ but the quiet of the auger and fang-spanner - / long tom and jim crow,/ strumming on the old banjo./ ... <i>(Mar 1, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>) [NOTES: (David George `Euchre Weather')]

### fanging
 
 <i>n.</i> speeding, hooning (See [fang verb](../F#fang-verb))

>  German-Kiwi relations deteriorate during a further anti-fanging lecture but then it's on with the show after diplomatic talks soothe ruffled feathers on both sides. <i>(May 26, 2001,Dominion,13</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [fans](../F#fans))

 <i>n.</i> In extended application in NZ.

### fannie train
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CHURCHMAN & HURST</b> The 6.35 am from Greymouth was the 'Miner's Train', while the return working, the 7.35 am from Rewanui was the 'Fannie Train", as the women from Dunollie and Rewanui used it to go to work in Greymouth. <i>(1990,Railways of NZ,196</i>)

### fanning
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the ventilating of hives by bees.

>  They [the bees] are facing the hives with their abdomens pointing upwards. This work is called "fanning" and they are ventilating the interior of the hive. <i>(Oct 20, 1914,NZ Journal of Agriculture,295</i>)

### fanny
 
 <i>n.</i> fantail [(boys')]

>  I have got a great many birds since you have been away, which I shot with your gun or a catapult (excuse spelling), and which I stuff and hang up by the beak. ... One of them is a New Zealand bittern ... I have also the mako-mako, silver-eye, riro-riro, miro-miro, fanny etc. ... <i>(Aug 16, 1875,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/399,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (?Bill Atkinson, Nelson, 15 years)]

### fans
 
 <i>exclam.</i> A children's truce or choosing word. Also fangs.

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... the way to ask for a truce in a game is to call Pax ... At the school I went to, the word was Fritz, and the truce was terminated by the call Fritz all over, but we knew that some schools used Fans (from south-west English dialect fain I or fainits ?). Apparently some New Zealand schools use or have used baldy, which sounds like a variant of English barley. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,158</i>)

>  Thanks to a flood of correspondence, my ignorance is now abated. The commonest truce word in New Zealand seems to be `fans' with a variant, `fangs' ... As children grow up, the words fall into disuse. They are not forgotten. One of my daughters complained that I had not consulted. Her word had been `fans'. <i>(Feb 5, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),72</i>) [NOTES: (I.A. Gordon)]

### fantabulous
 
 <i>adj.</i> [Coined on radio by Rhoda Lillie in 1950s re opening of Gourmet restaurant in Auckland or the visit of the Old Vic with Leigh and Olivier (from her daughter, Anna Adams)[Used elsewhere but recorded earliest in NZ.] 1966 Oct 15 ? 3/1 : ... entries in the `remarks' column of the visitors' book at the Aviemore power project [Otematata include] ... fantabulous ...]

>  Air travel - it's fantabulous. <i>(Jan 9, 1959,Listener (New Zealand Listener),4</i>) [NOTES: this is also OED2's first citation]

### fantail
 
 <i>n.</i> A handsome flycatcher of the New Zealand bush. Pre-settlement North Island writers prefer piwaka(waka) (Markham 1834, Yate 1835); early more-or-less scientific writers prefer fantailed (less often fantail) flycatcher, or merely flycatcher; fantail as a popular name is attested in 1845 (1854) (Wellington), 1851 (Taranaki) and sanctioned by Buller (1873 (1888)). Also called piwakawaka, tiwakawaka, shortened from fantail(ed) flycatcher, <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>, Pied or North Island Fantail, =piwakawaka; and <i>R. fuliginosa</i>, Black or South Island Fantail, =tiwaiwaka.

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... Pohebirds, Fantails, Thrushes, Mocking birds, Yellowheads & various other birds inhabit these impenetrable Forests. <i>(May 13, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/278</i>)

>  <b>GOLDER</b> ... fantail ... <i>(1845,Pigeon's Parliament,37</i>)

>  <b>LEICHHARDT</b> `We also observed the..fantailed fly-catcher (<i>Rhipidura</i>).' <i>(1847,Journal,ii/80</i>)

>  There is plenty to be said about the parrots and the owls, and the beautiful little fantails. <i>(Jun 15, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/96,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (H.R. Richmond)]

>  <b>MACKIE</b> Strolling in the bush adjoining our inn, observed the native pigeon a large and beautiful bird ... The lively and elegant little fan tail was continually flitting about me spreading and erecting its tail. <i>(Apr 1, 1853,Traveller under Concern,96,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> BIRDS MET WITH IN PELORUS ... Stilts - two kinds, called Toria by natives. Flycatchers and Fantails. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,66</i>)

>  <i>(1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/645</i>)

>  Fantail ... the pied Flycatcher. <i>(1868,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  Rhipidura flabellifera. Piwaka-waka. Fantail. Common. <i>Ibid.</i> I 1/164 The other birds ... were ... the Fan-tail (<i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>, the Ground-lark (<i>Anthus Novae Zelandiae</i>), the New Zealand Titmouse (<i>Certhiparus Novae Zelandiae</i>) ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  RHIPIDURA FLABELLIFERA, Gml. Piwakawaka. Fan-tail. The pied Flycatcher seems to prefer proximity to water in selecting its nesting- place ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/63</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Piiwakawaka<i>, n. <i>rhipidura flabellifera</i>, or<i> fantail</i>; a bird. <i>(1871,Dictionary of the Maori Language,117,3rd ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... a fantail <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,232</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Waka-waka (<i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>) is the robin of our Brighter Britain. It is a fantail, or flycatcher. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/226</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,i/69</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Fan-tail</i> ... bird-name applied in England to a pigeon; in Australia and New Zealand, to the little birds of the genus <i>Rhipidura</i> (q.v.). It is a fly-catcher. The New Zealand species are. -->Black F.- <i>Rhipidura fuliginosa</i>, Sparrm. (Tiwaiwaka). -->Pied F.- <i>R. flabellifera</i>, Gmel. (Piwakawa). In Tasmania, the <i>R. diemenensis</i> is called the Cranky Fantail, because of its antics. <i>(1898,Austral English,141</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> See here are met .../ The robin's chirp and lintie's trill,/ And mocking bird, with fantail heard,/ And parroquet's green clamouring shrill. <i>(1912,Castle Gay,60</i>)

>  The tit (<i>Myiomoira</i>), the fantail (<i>Rhipidura</i>), the wax-eye (<i>Zosterops</i>), ... also appear to be in no danger of extinction ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/328</i>)

>  The tit (<i>Myiomoira</i>), the fantail (<i>Rhipidura</i>), the wax-eye (<i>Zosterops</i>), ... also appear to be in no danger of extinction ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/328</i>)

>  <b>GREY</b> I liked especially the fantails. What delicate lovely birds! <i>(1926,Angler's Eldorado,170</i>)

>  Birds seen occasionally [on Three Kings were] kingfishers - kotaretare (<i>Halcyon sanctus</i>); moreporks - koko (<i>Ninox novae-zealandiae</i>); fantails - tireeraka (<i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>); ... <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/154</i>)

>  <i>fantail</i>: small insect-eating bird. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,173</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> FANTAIL <i>Rhipidura fuliginosa</i> Maori name: Piwakawaka. ... Birds of normal colour pattern (`Pied Fantail') make up most of the population in North Island, but the `Black Fantail' (i.e. dark phase) accounts for perhaps a quarter of the population of the South Island subpecies; ... <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,198</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Fantail - piwakawaka, tiwakawaka, etc. <i>Rhipidura fuliginosa</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  ~ n. small fly-catching bird. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),615</i>)

>  <i>fantail<i/I> n.small fly-catching bird. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  <i>fantail </i>... 2. an Old World flycatcher of Australia, New Zealand, and SE Asia, having a broad fan-shaped tail. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,401</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 271; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 177]]

>  Fantail <i>Rhipidura fuliginosa</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787) North Island Fantail <i>R.f. placabilis </i>BANGS, 1921. South Island Fantail <i>R.f. fulginosa</i> (SPARRMAN, 1787). Chatham Island Fantail <i>R.f. penitus</i> BANGS, 1911. OTHER NAMES: <i>Piwakawaka, pied fantail, black fantail. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,280</i>)

>  ... Read the papers & did Maori, went for about half an hour in the Domain & found a fantail nest (young ones) & a riroriro's with two young ones & an egg. I am inclined to think the egg is a pipiwharauroa's (white, with purple spots at the larger end). Splitting firewood in the afn. I found a fine large centipede - a weri - body brown, with the articulations very plainly marked, legs a pale blue ... c. have an appetite like a fantail. See appetite. <i>(Oct 20, 1860,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/645,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>HELMER</b> <i>(1964,Stag Party,83</i>)

>  There are thought to be about 10 subspecies of fantail, three of which occur in New Zealand: the North Island, South Island and Chatham Islands fantail. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/76</i>)

>  The small piwakawaka (fantail) sings most cheerfully at the head of the spring. <i>(Oct 29, 1947,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  It's an old Maori belief - when I was growing up a fantail in the house meant death. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  It sits in scattered beech forest in which fantails and bellbirds flit and sing, and is right on the edge of Abel Tasman National Park where the Takaka Hill tumbles to the sea. <i>(Aug 28, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  That special little bird, the fantail flycatcher, Rhipidura flabellifera, accompanied me. <i>(Dec 17, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  The last I shal [sic] mention is the Fan-Tail. Of these there are different sorts, but the body of the most remarkable one is scarce larger than a good Filbert, yet spreads a tail of most beautiful plumage full 3/4 of a semi-circle, of, at least, 4 or 5 Inches radius. <i>(1961,Cook's Journals,2/786</i>) [NOTES: 1773 Wales Journal in 1961 Cook's Journals 2/786]

>  Two fantails follow us down through the trees, flitting for invisible insects. <i>(Aug 29, 2001,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  Numbers of native birds such as tomtits, white heads, grey warblers and fantails are expected to increase in the Otaki catchment of the Tararua Forest Park ... <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 11</i>)

>  Fantails particularly love the water's edge. <i>(Mar 4, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

### fantail crutch
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. fantail style]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The Fantail crutch, involving two positions, is physically harder, and in operation is slower. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,53</i>)

### fantail style/fantail method
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a style of crutching wool from a sheep's rear.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The fantail crutch, involving two positions, is physically harder, and in operation is slower .. the old traditional method of coming in from the hock out to the tail , with the sheep turned the same way as in shearing. This is known as the fantail style. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,53</i>)

>  <b>GAMBRILL</b> In the morning the sheep we crutched were of the last mob of the season. I was taught the 'fantail' method, but still prefer the first style. <i>(1987,Flock House Farming,64</i>)

### fantailed flycatcher
 
 <i>n.</i> fantail [also fantail flycatcher] (See [also flycatcher](../A#also-flycatcher))

>  The Fan-tailed Flycatcher (<i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>) is the commonest. ... The Black Flycatcher (<i>R. melanura</i>) belongs exclusively to the South. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/10</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... my Son had [241] been on an other party & had got the same Thrush & a little Fantailed Flycatcher likewise new. [ed. fn] <i>Muscicapa ventilabrum</i> of <i>Descr. Anim.</i>, p.86; ... Now the South Island Fantail ... <i>(Mar 28, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/240</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The Piwaka-waka [a2's-acute] is the Musicapa flabellifera, or fan-tailed fly-catcher. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/298</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Muscicapa flabellifera (fantailed flycatcher) ... fn A Muscicapa flabellifera; (fantailed fly-catcher); its little body is scarcely larger than a walnut, though it spreads its little tail to a radius of six inches. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/125</i>)

>  The neat fantail flycatcher is busy ... <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/29/1</i>) [NOTES: (Dieffenbach)]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> In the lower regions the forest was much enlivened by birds. I shot some parrots [fn Nestor meridionalis. Kaka of the natives.], pigeons [fn Columba spadicea, Lath. Kukupa.], and wattle-birds [fn Glaucopis cinerea, Gmel. Kokako.]. Among the branches of the trees hopped the Neat fantail flycatcher [fn Muscicapa flabellifera, Gml. Piwakawaka.], and a social bird of a yellow colour, which has been depicted by Forster [fn Orthornyx heteroclytus, Lef. Popokatea.], and which had much the habits of the finch. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/77 Sometimes, indeed, the mako-mako [fn Philedon Dumerilii] pours forth ... its melodious notes; or perhaps a hawk [fn Falco brunnea] is seen watching the movements of a smaller bird; or the fan-tail flycatcher busy in the lower branches. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/29</i>)

>  Small birds, such as the fan-tailed fly-catcher (<i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>) ... are also introduced into the [Maori] ear as ornaments; ... <i>(1850,Savage Life,1/326</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> There are still two others, which are always near the tents: the fan-tailed flycatcher, belonging to the family Muscicapidae, ... and the New Zealand wren (Certhiparus maculicandus), both never tired of flying and hopping round the tents. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,140</i>)

>  Fantailed Flycatcher of the North ... Black Flycatcher of the South. [a translation of the Latin scientific names: now popularly called <i>Pied Fantail</i> and <i>Black Fantail</i>: check this and get longer cit.] <i>(1868,TrNZI,37</i>) [NOTES: (Ornithology of NZ)]

>  [The tui was] accompanied by a lively pair of fan-tail flycatchers (<i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>) <i>(1883,TrNZI,15/317</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso)]

>  ... the piwakawaka, <i>Rhipidura flabellifera</i>, Gml. - the pied fantail flycatcher ... <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/205</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> The tamest little bird we have is the Fantailed Fly-Catcher. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,70</i>)

### fantasy
 
 <i>n.</i> [(drug)] (See [GHB](../G#ghb))

>  It is believed the 18-year-old took the gamma hydroxybutyric acid (GBH), commonly known as Fantasy or Liquid E, during the ball. <i>(Aug 4, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Fantasy (liquid Fantasy, GHB,): Central nervous system depressant. Comes in various forms, most notorious one is 1,4-B which has been blamed for a couple of deaths in New Zealand in recent years. Also known as date rape drug. <i>(Apr 5, 2003,Dominion Post,F1</i>)

>  Fantasy produces a euphoria similar to alcohol but its side effects include severe respiratory depression, coma and death. <i>(Aug 6, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

### fanto
 
 <i>adj.</i> an alteration of fantastic, a common adolescent expression of delight, etc., in the 1980s. [1984 16 F E7 Pakuranga Coll. 8 FANTO -->1984 16 F E7 Pakuranga Coll. 18 FANTO]

### FAQ
 
<b>1</b> <i>abbr.</i> (Rural) fair average quality [a grade of beef.]

>  <b>BARTON</b> FAQ ox, heifer or cow = Fair average quality. Carcasses of this grade have less fat covering than those in the GAQ grade and they may be less plump and longer in the body. Rather more carcasses of dairy-bred cattle will be graded FAQ than GAQ. <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,72</i>)

### far back
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  One of the effects of the disappearance of the rabbit in the far back is the increase spread of the blue tussock. <i>(Dec 18, 1912,Otago Witness,33</i>)

### Far Downer
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a resident of South Westland [isolated by 160 kms of rugged terrain from the rest of Westland].

>  <b>NOLAN</b> I have been impressed as I recorded, how very much the rhythm of country life for the Far-downers (a phrase I've picked up in Whataroa, I think, for those who live further down the road, in South Westland), revolved around the twice yearly trek to the cattle sale. <i>(1998,Droving Days, The,42</i>)

>  <b>BRADSHAW</b> The title The Far Downers comes from the name given to the occupants of the far south by residents of the more northern settlements, such as Whataroa, Weheka (Fox Glacier) and Bruce Bay. The people of the Haast district lived 'Far Down' the Coast .. I hope that readers will enjoy meeting the Far Downers as I [sic] much as I have. <i>(2001,Far Downers, The,8</i>)

>  It was the beginning of the air service in 1931 that opened up the world for the 'Far Downers' ... [far-downer] <i>(Mar 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

### Far North
 
 <i>n.</i> Northland

>  <b>BULLER</b> The Red-fronted Parrakeet is very generally dispersed over the whole country - but is more plentiful in the southern portion of the North Island than in the far north, where the yellow-fronted species predominates. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,58,{1888 2nd edn 1/137} ed.</i>)

>  The cave paintings in the South Island suggest some connection with the motifs of carvings recently dug up in the far North, and have to be re-examined in the light of a suggestion that such pre-Fleet peoples such as Waitaha ... were responsible for the paintings ... <i>(1988,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/200</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Sep 20 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 3/200 (Ngata)]

>  <b>GRIEVE</b> ... the people of the more frigid South have very little good to say for the almost tropical warmth of the `Far North'. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,149,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GOLDBLATT</b> The writer spent his first night in New Zealand in a simple hotel in the Far North. <i>(1957,Democracy at Ease,89</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Kauri forests. In the forests of the far north, to the north of the `kauri line', ... kauri ... occurred frequently, or was locally dominant. <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,24</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> During the next few years the `Far North' (as it is now called) of New Zealand was the scene of incessant tribal warfare. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,36</i>)

>  <b>ALLAN</b> The name koromiko (korokio in the Far North) is commonly used for the larger ... [Hebe]. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,1/887,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> With the exception of a few individuals engaged in raising cattle on unfenced Crown land, ... the whole population of the far north of New Zealand at the turn of the century depended on ... kauri gum. <i>Ibid.,</i> 43 He told his audience that Houhora was in the far north where whales were caught in nets and oysters grew on trees. <i>(1970,Way Up North,29</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>ELATOSTEMA RUGOSUM, parataniwha.</i> The botanically improbable name `begonia fern' is heard in the Far North. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,82</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> ... I was with a group ... waiting out some foul weather in a hotel bar in a small town in the far north. <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,240</i>)

>  City drug users.. are turning away from the unsophisticated "bush dak" of the Far North to trendier designer drugs. <i>(Aug 27, 1998,Dominion,10</i>)

>  The Far North is the land of.. the Taihoaites and the "never tires". <i>(Jun 9, 1921,NZ Observer,9</i>)

>  There is also a really big push to expand the Far North visual arts with more than 70 visual artists being represented in exhibitions. <i>(Feb 1, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  But the Far North has had booms and busts for decades, leaving a lack of infrastructure. <i>(May 1, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  The "far north" is a long way from Dargaville. <i>(Mar 29, 1950,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### Far Northerner
 
 <i>n.</i> a resident of the Far North

>  Far Northerners are different. <i>(Feb 1, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

### Far South
 
 <i>n.</i> the islands to the south of the South Island; now usually the southermost parts of the South Island

>  <b>DOUGALL</b> [title page] Far South - Stewart Island, The Snares, Auckland, Campbell, Antipodes and Bounty Islands <i>(1888,Far South</i>)

>  A strange bird in Maori eyes is the hakuai of the off-shore islands in the far south. <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/340</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Yes, these folk in the Far South were going to be dear friends of mine, and so they proved to be. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,177</i>)

### far-back
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of areas] extremely isolated. [1903 citation is farback adv.; the rest adj.; see aslo far back n.]

>  Some of the smaller flocks on front country have been wiped out and farback, the snow is still frozen and there can be only one result. <i>(Aug 15, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,5</i>)

>  The far-back stations, where shearing has now begun, have been so much affected by the weather that it is most difficult to muster the sheep. <i>(Dec 22, 1904,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  He was shot through the lungs while fighting for King and country in France and having returned to New Zealand was munificently rewarded for his bravery and sacrifice by being allowed to take up this section of virgin bush in this far-back rough country. <i>(Oct 1918,NZ Farmer,1339</i>)

>  To begin with adventurous sheep men went into the far back country and took up land for their flocks. [farback; far back] <i>(Aug 13, 1942,NZ Farmer,4</i>)

### FARE
 
 <i>abbr.</i> families apart require equity

>  "These tests are just giving people the right to know," says Bruce Tichbon, of Families Apart Require Equality (FARE) .. FARE advised fathers to get the test done overseas. <i>(Jan 30, 2005,Dominion Post,p1</i>)

### farewell
 
 <i>n.</i> a ceremonial occas. to honour a departing or retiring person. (usu. to give somebody a farewell).

>  <b>BARR</b> DRAMATIC FAREWELL OF MAORILAND. [Then came the sensational departure of the British team from Auckland ...] <i>(1908,British Rugby Team in Maoriland,133</i>)

>  <b>McELDOWNEY</b> Staff farewell and presentation ... to Joy Smith, who leaves soon for a job in America. <i>(1963,Warm South,38,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The farewell under the stand, the half pies and the plates of tomato sauce, the jugs of beer. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,172</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> One of my duties during the War was to be the chief speaker at the Farewell Functions extended to those who were going overseas. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,27</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to honour a departing or retiring person at a formal function; to give a farewell party to.

>  <b>BARR</b> There were several ... groups on ... Dunedin's handsome railway station to `farewell' the members of the British team ... <i>(1908,British Rugby Team in Maoriland,56</i>)

>  Opportunity was taken to farewell the Rev. E. Drake and Mrs. Drake. <i>(Mar 22, 1931,Auckland  Star</i>)

>  New Zealand has developed many phrases that depend on local interpretation: e.g. `The Limited', `the six o'clock rush', `off the bitumen', `motor camp', `to farewell', `to pencil in', `to check up on', `return (at meals)'. All these are immediately understandable to New Zealanders, but not always to others. <i>(1950,Post Office English Course [TS],14</i>)

>  <b>NORRIS</b> [He] was suitably farewelled when he left Hamilton a few months later. <i>(1964,Settlers in Depression,5</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> Mr Falloon spoke for those being farewelled, and his first words were, ... <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,27</i>)

>  [Some omissions from NZPOD] are farewell as verb), judder bar(s), morning team, Zip (P) (water-heater); some widely used children's words would seem to deserve a place alongside neat ...: hex (a spell), growl (`Does your mother growl?' <i>(Apr 26, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>) [NOTES: (Forrest Scott)]

>  On 22 February at 11 am the Victoria Child Care Centre is holding a party brunch at the Centre to farewell Maggie Haggerty ... All past and present children and adults welcome. Please B.Y.O. and a plate of nibbles. <i>(Feb 13, 1987,Victoria Univ. Weekly Staff Circular</i>)

### farm
 
 <i>n.</i> an area where penguins gather.

>  <b>DOUGALL</b> On the Snares the penguins are in what are called farms, that is to say in isolated groups, whereas on the Bounty Islands they are all over. <i>(1888,Far South,7</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> farm-killed [C.M. writes: <i>Farm</i> here means "farm-killed". Although this has some similarity to the listing of <i>farm butter</i> in the dictionary, it is not quite the same.]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> My mother used to practically salivate when offered farm meat. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,66</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> 'Farm lamb!' she cries. 'I can't believe I'm me.' <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,136</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> In NZ as elsewhere; in early use for usu. the term for an agricultural or `mixed' landholding smaller or less grand than that of a pastoral station.

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> ... some small farms were cultivated for potatoes, upon which the poor slaves were at work. <i>(Jun 19, 1820,Letters & Journals,256,1932 ed.</i>)

### farm bike
 
 <i>n.</i> a trail-bike for farm use.

>  A farm bike is a substitute for a horse. It is a small-engined off-road motorcycle, two wheels with knobby tyres decently arranged one behind the other and usually with a platform at the back for dogs to ride on. <i>(Jul 31, 1999,Dominion,18</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> But the happiest dog I saw on this trip was riding on the front of a farm bike with a shepherd, ... <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,92</i>)

>  <b>SHAW</b> They were made by Barry's farmbike. <i>(1983,Longfin,60</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> The rattle of dogs' chains ...; the sound of a farm bike on the hills above Kelly's. <i>(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,16</i>) [NOTES: (Takahe Man)]

>  Then a second person took a tumble from a farm bike on the cattle grid, got an arm stuck in the grid and broke a wrist and had to be taken to hospital. <i>(Nov 14, 1986,Contact (Wgtn),4</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Two drovers behind the flock, one on a horse, the other on a farmbike, are pushing them hard. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,45</i>)

>  farm bike, farm trike NZ two-/three-wheeled motor cycle for use on farms. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,271</i>) [NOTES: [Nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Farm bike `obeys' whistle ... The Clinton farmer has an unusual problem with his Yamaha Bullfrog three-wheeled motorcycle. <i>(Aug 5, 1987,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  He gets his farm bike and leads us up the hill to Rimu A1. <i>(Apr 15, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  The one concession to an easier life is a farm bike which sits against the milking shed. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Evening Post,27</i>)

>  Other four wheel drive farm bikes have more power, but they are not built for comfort. <i>(Jan 1995,Growing Today,60</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a motorbike/trike/quad designed to be ridden on hills and in muddy pastoral conditions.

>  The Mountain Goat farm bike seems to run on 'the smell of an oily rag.' <i>(Feb 22, 1968,NZ Farmer,18</i>)

>  Mountain Goat is not a road bike with a fancy name. The first farm bike with disc brakes. <i>(Jul 1, 1971,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>)

>  What other farm bike is reliable and rigged enough to offer you a 25,000 mile or 25 month warranty? <i>(Sep 27, 1973,NZ Farmer,66</i>)

>  <b>RENNIE</b> Later (in the 1960s) farm bikes, which were both cheaper and more convenient, were introduced. Following on from them there have been farm trikes and even farm quads. <i>(1988,Agriculture in NZ,22</i>)

>  "More often that not, while the men are out on the farm bike, the women farmers are taking care of running the business ..." <i>(Feb 22, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

>  Catherine's death was the fourth of a child aged under 13 on a farm bike in five months . . Mr Ward said people need to "get past a carefree attitude" about farm bikes. <i>(Jun 6, 2001,Dominion,9</i>)

>  Wairarapa can lay claim to one of the first farm bikes ever manufactured [in the sixties] .. What I made first was considered to be the second farm bike ever made in New Zealand - the first being the Mountain Goat machine made in Taranaki. <i>(Jan 15, 2002,Wairarapa Midweek,01</i>)

### farm butter
 
 <i>n.</i> butter made on or bought directly from a farm, as opposed to factory-made, shop-bought [(also farmers', dairy, separator butter); also e.g. farm eggs.]

>  <b>LEVY</b> There was [337] no doubt about the unpleasantness of these feed flavours in the spring, particularly in home-made farm butter. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,336,1970 ed.</i>)

### farm cadet
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an apprentice farmer.

>  There will be some changes in the format of the North Canterbury farm cadet scheme this year. Mr W.T. (Trevor) Simpson, field officer for the North Canterbury Farm Training Council cadet scheme, said that the scheme was continuing to grow ... This year Mr Simpson said it was expected that there would be about 120 cadets training ... <i>(Jan 16, 1981,Press, The,6/6</i>)

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> I left Christ's College after three years, at the end of 1925, and started as a farm cadet at Otaranui Station, Ruatoria. <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,22</i>)

### farm clip
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a total amount of wool produced on a single property.

>  .. it is now even more important for the farm clip to be adequately prepared in the woolshed where it can be done more easily and cheaply. Scientific measurement has recently shown that the straightbred farm clip is the most uniform lot of wool available. <i>(Nov 13, 1975,NZ Farmer,48</i>)

### farm forester
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a landholder who combines livestock farming and forestry.

>  Farm foresters descended on Hawke's Bay last week for their national conference ... <i>(Apr 5, 2001,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

>  Farm foresters - high quality seedling available. <i>(May 22, 1969,NZ Farmer,86</i>)

>  For the farm forester, the fact that there's no need to make a direct choice between pasture and trees for land use is obviously important. <i>(Aug 10, 1972,NZ Farmer,73</i>)

>  I encourage all farm foresters to grow a quality product. <i>(Aug 1, 1996,NZ Farmer,23</i>)

>  ... lifestylers and small farmers can talk to regional council staff and farm foresters about some of the following points. <i>(2000,Lifestyle Farmer,Autumn 65</i>)

>  The book is very much about tree people, with many inset black and white photographs of farm foresters ... <i>(2000,Tree Cropper, The,??</i>) [NOTES: The Tree Cropper 24 Winter 26 ??]

>  There are three main groups of alternative species grown by farm foresters. <i>(May 25, 2001,Dominion,23</i>)

### farm forestry
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a combination of livestock grazing and forestry.

>  He has helped put together four forestry partnerships along with another farm forestry person. <i>(Aug 1, 1996,NZ Farmer,26</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> ..because 'farm forestry' and 'agroforestry' are much more dependent on these microfactors than 'blanket' corporate plantings, microfactors (such as changing stock carying capacity or tree diameter and height growth within a paddock, relative to topography) have major influences on any farm scale agroforestry study. <i>(1989,Report,52</i>) [NOTES: Economic Evaluation of Agroforestry in Otago]

>  He looked at several areas of farm forestry, beginning with radiata pine. <i>(May 25, 2001,Dominion,23</i>)

### farm gate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Details of inspection.. could be recorded to the devices as they moved through the supply chain - enabling overseas customers to track the history of the product right back to the farm gate. <i>(2001,NZ Infotech Weekly,No. 480 : 13</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) of farming economics, the site of production. (See [farmgate price](../F#farmgate-price),[farm-gate returns](../F#farm-gate-returns))

>  The demand was still being driven by record returns to the farm gate. <i>(Oct 21, 2001,Sunday Star Times,E5</i>)

>  The impact of New Zealand's rising United States dollar-based foreign exchange rate will become apparent on sheep and beef farm gate incomes soon ... <i>(May 24, 2002,Dominion,15</i>)

### farm hack
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an old farm truck

>  This car is not just an old farm hack or a rust bucket. <i>(Jun 2000,Growing Today,5</i>)

>  An old farm truck: a farm hack. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### farm handing
 
 <i>n.</i> employment as a farm hand

>  He turned to farm handing and it was then that he picked up a bit of work with the local shearing contractor Clint Balamy. <i>(Oct 2005,Country-Wide,36</i>) [NOTES: Country-wide Stock~Land]

### Farm of Instruction
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a demonstration or research farm where new techniques and materials are demonstrated.

>  At the Ruakura Farm of Instruction potato experiments, to ascertain the most suitable varieties for the district .. have been carried out for several years. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,281</i>)

>  The Department of Agriculture has Pedigree Stock for sale at its several Farms as follows:- Ruakura Farm of Instruction, Hamilton East ... [Instruction Farm] <i>(Jul 20, 1923,NZ Journal of Agriculture,20 xv</i>)

### farm out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to give an animal or child into the care of another person.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> We farmed out the kids so we could go to the Hunt Ball and not have to rush home. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,33</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> As I'd farmed out my other dogs, I asked Sol Steedman if he'd take him until I came back from Australia. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,110</i>)

### farm park
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm property administered by Department of Conservation [usually in a recreational area and open to trampers and campers.]

>  Puponga Farm Park is one of a number of farm parks created throughout New Zealand and managed by the Department of Coservation. <i>(Feb 28, 2006,Dominion Post,14</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> New Zealand has 12 farm parks which are located in the northern halves of both islands. Farm parks portray the natural scene ... <i>(1981,Farm Parks for People,3</i>)

>  He says interest in the farm park is flowing in. <i>(Jul 20, 1995,NZ Farmer,14</i>)

>  <b>WALTON & BAGLEY</b> The farm had recently been acquired by the Department of Lands and Survey and with other areas was to become Puponga Farm Park. <i>(2000,Archaelogical Survey, An,5</i>)

### farm plan
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a strategy developed for a farm property with the object of sustainable land management [to alleviate erosion and conserve wetlands and native forests.]

>  <b>BRADSHAW & WILLIAMS</b> Farm plans are seen as a useful tool to educate landowners ... <i>(1998,Information and Communication Needs,20</i>)

>  <b>BRADSHAW & WILLIAMS</b> Those with farm plans consider the plans provide good site specific information that could encourage and assist them to adopt more long term farming practices. <i>(1998,Information and Communication Needs,27</i>)

### farm school
 
 <i>n.</i> a short-term course for farmers and rural workers.

>  The [week long] farm school which opened at Ruakura last month is the fifth gathering of its kind to be held there .. This year the Department had held six farm schools, three in the North and three in the South. <i>(1923,NZ Farmer,July 2. 1045</i>)

### farm senior
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) farm manager or overseer.

>  Our client requires a Farm Senior for their 2500 acre (10,000 su) sheep and beef property at Mt Damper. <i>(Mar 24, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,40</i>)

### farm sitter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a short-term farm caretaker.

>  Farm sitter: We are looking for a farm sitter from May 25 until June 18 on our small dairy farm (49 cows, Omata.) <i>(Mar 24, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,40</i>)

>  Farm sitters: I would imagine farm sitters to be quite expensive. <i>(Feb 15, 2002,Lifestyleblock.co.nz</i>)

### farm sitting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the short-term caretaking of a farm property and stock.

>  My husband and I did relief milking and farm sitting for 7 years before going sharemilking. <i>(Feb 15, 2002,Lifestyleblock.co.nz</i>)

### farm technician
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an employee responsible for recording, developing and analysing production data on a large farm holding.

>  Farm technician - A position has become vacant for a Farm Operations technician on Landcorp's Rangitaiki Station. <i>(Feb 3, 2001,Dominion,63</i>)

>  Farm technician - A position has become vacant for a Farm Operations Technician.. <i>(Feb 3, 2001,NZ Herald,D12</i>)

>  A position is available for a Farm Technician on Corrland's Waikeria farm, 15 km from te Awamutu. The Farm Technician will be responsible for: Collection and analysis of data to support management decisions; maintaining farm records; preparing reports for senior management; liaising with the farm managers and herd supervisors to achieve the best possible management outcomes. <i>(May 19, 2001,Dominion,27</i>)

>  A position is available for a farm technician on a central North Island farm at Turangi. <i>(Aug 10, 2002,Dominion,B10</i>)

### farm trike
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  farm bike, farm trike NZ two-/three-wheeled motor cycle for use on farms. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,271</i>) [NOTES: [Nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a three-wheeled farm motorbike, introduced in the 1970s.

>  <b>RENNIE</b> Later (in the 1960s) far bikes, which were both cheaper and more convenient, were introduced. Following on from them have been farm trikes and even farm quads. <i>(1988,Agriculture in NZ,22</i>)

### farm walk
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a demonstration or inspection of a farming property to view stock or land [possibly for sale.] (See [open farm](../O#open-farm))

>  .. new 4 stand woolshed with covered yards. Farm walks Sun 2 & Wed 5 Dec 1.30-2.30 pm. <i>(Dec 7, 2001,Manawatu Evening Standard & Property Guide,25</i>)

>  I haven't got the time to do farm walks on both farms ... <i>(Dec 20, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

>  At Rongotea we have a lower-order sharemilker and the only time I go there is if I am needed for something or a farm walk with our dairy team consultant. <i>(Aug 22, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,16</i>)

### Farm-a-rama
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm field/display day.

>  Town and Country Farm-a-rama by kind permission of Mr Peter Cowley, Ellesmere Ave, bell Block. <i>(Oct 28, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

### farm-gate return
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) net profit from a farming property.

>  Unlike New Zealand's mature and traditional livestock markets based on cattle and sheep (neither of which are demand-driven), emus offer a substantial improvement in their farm-gate returns. <i>(Sep 10, 1996,NZ Emu Farming Association Journal,Promo</i>)

### farmer
 
 <i>n.</i> In NZ usu. one following `mixed' agricultural and pastoral farming, in earlier times often on a small holding (see cockatoo, cockie). [Also dairy farmer, sheep farmer, mussel-farmer. q.v.]

>  <b>ELDER</b> [? first use of farmer in NZ] He [Duaterra's half-brother] is every day at work either as a carpenter or farmer ... <i>(1814,Marsden's Lieutenants,122</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  Farmer n. see cockatoo. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),615</i>)

### Farmer's Co-op
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Many things they knew because they had always known, the same way they knew the door at the Farmer's Co-op with the powder puff and mirror was theirs and Dad's the one with the top hat. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

### farmers' butter
 
 <i>n.</i> = farm butter [also farmers' friend number 8 wire; farmers' jelly cake (ironic) one with no jelly in it.]

>  FARMERS' JELLY CAKE ... eggs ... milk ... butter ... sugar ... flour ... soda ... cream of tartar ... Bake in meat dish. <i>(1938,Souvenir Book of Cookery Recipes,36</i>)

>  Use number 8 fencing wire, the farmers' friend. <i>(Sep 1, 1975,TV1</i>) [NOTES: gardening (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>HUNT</b> (George) Factory butter was one and two a pound, separated farmers' butter one shilling a pound [c1910]. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,70</i>)

### Farmers' Union
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> An opportunity to make themselves heard more clearly came in 1899 when some well-to-do farmers formed a Farmers' Union. By reason of their numbers, the `cockies' quickly became the dominant influence in the new organization [Cockies here = dairy farmers]. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,200</i>)

### farmgate price
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) return and profit to the farmer.

>  It is not the Dairy Board or dairy company which takes the risk if dairy returns fall - it is the farmgate price that is hit. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,04</i>)

>  The required farmgate price for meat and wool farming has to be established ... <i>(Jul 20, 1995,NZ Farmer,9</i>)

>  Market returns have been eroded by lower meat and pelt prices, poorer pelt quality, and a stronger NZ dollar, but intense competition for a diminishing supply of lambs has pushed procurement premiums to very high levels and farmgate prices to record highs. <i>(May 29, 2001,Dominion,13</i>)

>  .. lower numbers coming to the works have kept farmgate prices up. <i>(May 10, 2002,Dominion,15</i>)

### farming family
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the siblings or mixed generations involved in farming a property or properties.

>  About 75% of farming families want to treat all of the children equitably .. Ms Trafford says talking to farming families, particularly farming women, she realised that they were very worried. <i>(Jul 27, 1995,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  An off-farm income has become a cornerstone in the strength of the farming family ... <i>(Jul 27, 1995,NZ Farmer,29</i>)

>  .. the story of the New Zealand farming family, who were the economic backbone of this country through the 20th century. <i>(Nov 8, 2001,Dominion,17</i>)

### farmkill
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a slaughter of stock for human consumption on a farm property.

>  I always phone the farmkill operator to say I have given another pig away .. The farmkill operator makes a note in his diary of my phone call ... <i>(Nov 4, 2001,Lifestyleblock.co.nz,Althea</i>)

### farmlet
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EVANS</b> The Waikaka Commonage [heading] When the dredging commenced in 1897 more and more people began to settle round Waikaka village and the land on the hill on the east side of the township became a common land where anyone grazed cows and hacks. Finally a large area ... was declared a commonage ... Between 1900 and 1914 this Commonage was cut up into farmlets varying from thirty to fifty acres. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,175</i>)

>  The new [Heinemann's] dictionary carries: ... mall, ... M.O.W. and o.n.o. ... dropper (for fencing) ... and garage sale. It does not contain diff and carby (which have now moved from slang into general use among at least half the population), leg-in, woolly (as a noun), farmlet, fat (or sharp) sand, nor, amazingly, home-brew. <i>(Feb 28, 1979,Otago Daily Times,21</i>) [NOTES: (G. Griffiths)]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Dolly's sister ... had a small farmlet ... where they milked a few cows. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,108</i>)

### farmstay
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm offering accommodation in a homestead, cottage or shearers' quarters [perhaps with breakfast].

>  Farm to Farm deals with farmstay groups throughout the country. <i>(Jul 13, 1995,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  The farmstays consist of the self-contained three-bedroom Glendoon homestead and the two-bedroom Stonemead cottage which offer breakfast and country home comforts. <i>(Oct 18, 2001,Dominion,18</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> At Te Hau the Mebans run a farmstay business. <i>(2001,Riding Country,49</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a farm offering accommodation (either at the homestead or more usually in former shearers' quarters etc), usually breakfast and sometimes other meals, to holidaymakers. [Jim Cameron noted reference in a detective novel published in England in 1995.] (See [OED online under homestay; farmstay used in NZ citation](../O#oed-online-under-homestay;-farmstay-used-in-nz-citation))

>  "A lot of people are looking for links with the land," farmstay operator Judy McDonald says... Mrs McDonald and her husband Donald have run a farmstay on their 1000 hectare sheep and beef ranch for the past 14 years. <i>(Aug 27, 1999,Dominion,Farming Advertising Feature : 15</i>)

>  ...in desperation, the Macdonalds try to run a farmstay. <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

>  Two dwellings no one is an 11year [sic] old 3 bedroom home and the second a near new 2 bedroom log style cottage suitable for farmstay. <i>(Sep 13, 2003,Hawke's Bay Today,C7</i>)

>  A homestay/farmstay mini-industry has sprung up to service this traffic, plus a slew of cafes, galleries, gardens, antique shops and related cultural activities. <i>(May 23, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

### farmy
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) to do with the business of farming.

>  <b>GORDON</b> The popular titles of billy and nanny were replaced with the more 'farmy' terms of buck and doe. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,14</i>)

>  Many [women] were not gorgeous in the conventional sense, but the southern crowd saw the beauty in their rugged competence, their farmy ways. <i>(Nov 2, 2002,Dominion,F12</i>)

### Farmy Army
 
 <i>n.</i> rural volunteers assisting following Christchurch's earthquake February 2011 [word modelled on Barmy Army (travelling British cricket supporters); members of the Farmy Army from Palmerston North might be referred to as the Palmy Farmy Army, according to a wag quoted on RNZ National.]

>  Mayor Bob Parker labelled our volunteers the 'Farmy Army', because they hopped in their utes and headed to Christchurch. <i>(Mar 6, 2011,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

>  Farmy Army to wind down [heading] Federated Farmers will wind down its 'Farmy Army' of volunteers from today. The decision by the Christchurch City Council to cease its major clean-up operations, in which it had been aided by the Farmy Army, based at the A and P Showgrounds, and student volunteers, meant the rural volunteers would 'scale back targeted assistance' after today, Federated Farmers said on its website. <i>(Mar 5, 2011,Otago Daily Times,2</i>)

### farmyarder
 
 <i>n.</i> a haymaker blow [(?nonce)]

>  <b>BRERETON</b> Richards swung a farm yarder at him. <i>(1947,No Roll of Drums,136</i>)

### Farouk's revenge
 
 <i>n.</i> diarrhoea

>  He went down with hepatitis, which at that time was as common as Farouk's revenge - one of the troop's less offensive names for diarrhoea. <i>(1984,Islands,34/107</i>) [NOTES: Davin (Cassino Casualty)]

### fart tax
 
 <i>n.</i> a proposed flatulence tax q.v., as described by farmers

>  Opposition parties call the [emissions research] levy a "flatulence" or "fart" tax... ACT MP Gerry Eckhoff, who is touring New Zealand in a "fart tax bus", urged farmers to stand firm against the Government's "back door attempt to save face." <i>(Aug 30, 2003,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  The Government has abandoned its plans for a flatulence tax in the face of fierce farmer opposition and international derision... "Animal fart tax puts wind up New Zealand farmers," the Canadian Broadcasting Corporation said on its website. "Farmers raise stink over New Zealand fart tax," said <i>The Guardian</i> newspaper in Britain. <i>(Oct 17, 2003,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  The access issue does not lend itself to a mocking "fart tax" label that brought them the support of urban dwellers and the media. <i>(Nov 27, 2004,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  The battle over perceptions was lost when the Government was forced to fight a scatological, absurd-sounding levy - "the fart tax" - which incidentally gave the rest of the world a laugh at our expense. <i>(Oct 23, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  We don't want no fart tax, no bloody way. <i>(Sep 20, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  The very term "flatulence tax" is controversial; the Government says it is not a tax but a research levy; environmental groups believe the farming lobby deliberately uses terms such as "fart tax" to make the charge sound more ludicrous. <i>(Jul 26, 2003,Dominion Post,F2</i>)

### fart-sack
 
 <i>n.</i> A sleeping-bag; occas. a bed. See also farter-->. sack-->. [ca1945 University Students', Wellington]

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> `What about that fart-sack of yours, huh?' `What's wrong with my sleeping bag?' Stan retorted. <i>(1982,Wild Pig,45</i>)

### farter
 
 <i>n.</i> A sleeping-bag; occas. a bed. See also fart-sack. [(Tramping.) ca1940 or earlier: Les Cleveland. 1945 Used by members of the Victoria College Tramping Club.]

### farty
 
 <i>adj.</i> (pejorative epithet)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> There were all sorts of bright flowers and creepers and vegetable patches and farty banana trees where you'd have seen only grass, cows, fences and old Huberts pine trees before, ... <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,99</i>)

### fascia
 
 <i>n.</i> a board under the edge of the roof at the eaves, to support spouting and divert water away from the wall [Jim Cameron writes : NSOD gives "a horizontal band of wood etc. esp as used in an architrave" [i.e. a part surrounding a door or window"]. Collins is similar, as is the definition in Freeland, <i>Architecture in Australia</i>, p. 317. If these (and Salmond) are accurate the NZ meaning appears to differ from the British and Australian. The point may be worth following up.]

>  <b>SALMOND</b> fascia : a board under the edge of the roof at the <i>eaves</i>, to support spouting and divert water away from the wall <i>(1986,Old New Zealand Houses,232</i>)

### fascine
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to line drains with manuka or lay trunks of manuka in order to corduroy a road in swampy country. [1800s.]

>  He proposed that he and Mr Morrison should be empowered to expend GBP8 in fascining the road. <i>(Jul 7, 1880,Hawera Star,4</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but sp. using manuka in NZ] a trunk of manuka used to line drains and form roads in swampy land.

>  If the fascines were cut and tied in bundles by the Board, he would cart them and lay them down himself. <i>(Jul 7, 1880,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> Drains were dug and lined with manuka fascines. <i>(1961,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol III,103</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Such roads as led into the swampland were built on fascines of manuka and would tremble and shudder at the passage of the bullock teams. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,75</i>)

### fash
 
 <i>v.</i> In NZ not common, and mainly heard as an occasional Scotticism in Otago-Southland; W3.] To bother; be annoyed. [(Otago-Southland Scots')]

>  <b>CHJERRILL</b> No need to fash about feedin' them people. I killed a couple o' days since, so there's plenty o' meat. <i>(1950,Story of a NZ Sheepfarm,67</i>)

### fast
 
 <i>adj.</i> [See cit. ?omit???]

>  [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... fast, a farce, theatrical or otherwise; <i>(1935,Slang Today,285</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 285]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Fast. A farce: New Zealanders', esp. soldiers': 1915. A sol. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,267</i>)

### Fast Ferns
 
 <i>n.</i> a NZ athletics team

>  Ron Palenski [informs us] that <i>Fast Ferns</i> has been used in media releases from Athletics New Zealand (Ferns for men's teams now?). <i>(Aug 2000,NZWords,4: 1</i>)

### fast ferry
 
 <i>n.</i> a hydrofoil or any other unconventional ferry faster than conventional shipping

>  The dream of fast ferry competition on Cook Strait might have dimmed with the demise of Fast Cat Ferries, but it has not died. <i>(Mar 21, 2001,Dominion,10</i>)

### fasten
 
 <i>v.</i> to successfully harpoon (a whale). Also fasten on, to. [(Whaling.)]

>  <b>BELL</b> It is a pity that it should often be necessary to fasten to the calf in order to secure the cow, but I do not apprehend that it will cause such a diminution of numbers as to injure the fishing ... <i>(Nov 1831,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Nov Bell Letter in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 10]

>  <b>McNAB</b> [Whalers] were under the impression that ... they would have no further labour ... than to fasten to whales alongside, cut in, try out, stow away without intermission, until their cargoes would be completed. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,154</i>) [NOTES: >1836 Capt. Greene Report in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 154]

>  Two Boats out fastened to a Whale the Iron drew and we lost her <i>Ibid.</i> 73 Saw a large Whale was in chase best part of the day, but she was so Galeyed we had no chance of fastening. <i>(Apr 28, 1838,Piraki Log,71</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> An old-time bay whaling station consisted ... of at least two boats, with their crew of six men each. The headsman, or mate, four ordinary oarsmen, and the harpooner, or `boat-steerer,' who pulled the bow-oar, and drove in the harpoon, and who, when the whale had been struck and `fastened on,' went aft and steered the boat, while the mate took his place, and by repeated lance-thrusts killed it. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,75</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> Fasten - To successfully harpoon a whale. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,226</i>)

### fastie
 
 <i>n.</i> a trick, a piece of sharp practice. pull a fastie. [ca1950 at least: Wellington]

### Fastpost
 
 <i>n.</i> [Get date of introduction.]

>  The men ... are mostly preoccupied with sex and the time that it actually takes for fast post mail to get from Levin to Auckland. <i>(Mar 1990,Landfall,173/109</i>)

### fat
 
 <i>adj.</i> of stock, in prime condition and ready for slaughter [Spec. use of fat fattened (for slaughter).]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Practically all the stock [sc sheep] on the runs in New Zealand is fat; and, as there is an over-supply for consumption, squatters are forced to turn their fat stock [96] into money by boiling them down for their tallow. There is a boiling-down works at Featherston, ... where thousands of these large fat sheep ... are boiled down. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,95,1935 ed.</i>)

>  If these sheep are well done to when hoggets they can be turned off fat, good weights and excellent mutton, at about twenty to twenty-two months; ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,94</i>)

>  The products from this type of farm are wool, some store lambs, a few fat wether lambs, some culled hoggets, store cattle, varying numbers of store or fat wethers, and a draft of `cast-for-age' (c.f.a.) ewes. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,59</i>)

>  A FAT LOT.- We hear that a sale of 2000 wethers, from the station of the Hon., Ernest Gray, was made by Messrs. Miles and Co. for the West Coast, the sheep averaging 62lbs in weight. <i>(Jun 28, 1866,Lyttelton Times,2</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> In collocations or combin. with an animal name esp. fat bullock, cattle, sheep, stock; see also fat lamb.

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> When he left that station he gained further experience, ... , driving fat bullocks from Longbeach to diggings in Central Otago and the West Coast. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,36</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]



 
 <i>n.</i> The capitalistic employing class, or a member of it, often portrayed as a greedy, grasping employer. Occas. as a name (Mr Fat, Harry Fat, etc.; popular in `Truth' c1905-1910. cf modern fat-cat. Get Baxter cit Harry Fat.

>  At an Auckland Chamber of Commerce meeting a representative of fat proposed that he and his fellow fats ... <i>(Sep 16, 1905,Dominion Post,13/1/5</i>)

>  It was these same men who were called upon to give evidence on behalf of "Fat". <i>(Dec 15, 1911,Maoriland Worker,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  She finished her test, doing 816.64 fat in 365 days. <i>(Sep 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,56</i>)

>  Cows averaging 5969 lb. of milk and 300 lb. of fat did so at a feed cost of 21 cents a lb. <i>(Nov 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,59</i>)

>  <b>BELSHAW</b> Individual cow yields may range from 150 to 1,000 lbs. of fat per year. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,464</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fat lamb, ready for sale. [Later replaced with 'primes' to avoid connotation of poor nutrition for humans, but still used in agribusiness.]

>  .. it was intended to keep the ewe lambs for breeding purposes and to use the Border Leicester where it was intended that all the lambs should go off the farm as fats. <i>(May 1909,NZ Farmer,360</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> I remember .. watching the well-known dealer, Andrew Grant, picking 'fats.' <i>(1921,Tutira,362</i>)

>  I put 75 on the 4 acre paddock and one month later sold 55 as fats at 25s each. <i>(Oct 30, 1926,NZ Dairy Exporter,56</i>)

>  Last year we drafted 2035 fats from 1840 ewes, of which 1222 were drafted on 17th December. <i>(Aug 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,153</i>)

>  Twenty years ago the 'fats' had to be driven to the nearest railway siding for loading on to trucks. <i>(Dec 16, 1953,Free Lance,44</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> When drafting was finished, my mate and myself helped the drovers take over 5,000 'fats' four miles across country, while the rest of the shepherds returned the rejects to their block. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,119</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. these lambs are now sold as fats and the wether flock has been disposed of. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,214</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [Also (ii) FAT CATTLE CUT [Nf dicts] See stockwhip (a stockwhip cut).]

>  Fattening-farms. - Marketable products: Fat lambs, fat sheep, wool, fat cattle ... <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,24/200A</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an erection [1960s Wellington]

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> [sic] she didn't know much else she said except taking her clothes off that was the trouble, grinding her bum so the wankeroos had themselves a free fat. <i>(1990,Snow in Spain,71</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> Of clay, limestone, with few impurities, hence pure. [Check G Griffiths, that fat and sharp are synonym rather than synonymous terms: get meaning]]

>  <b>21</b> The new [Heinemann's] dictionary carries: ... mall, ... M.O.W. and o.n.o. ... dropper (for fencing) ... and garage sale. It does not contain diff and carby (which have now moved from slang into general use among at least half the population), leg-in, woolly (as a noun), farmlet, fat (or sharp) sand, nor, amazingly, home-brew. <i>(Mar 28, 1979,Otago Daily Times</i>) [NOTES: (G. Griffiths)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a prime lamb or cattle-beast ready for slaughter. [Usu. pl.]

>  Fat (f&abreve.t), n. [Add.] 4. (Agric.) A head of fat cattle; - commonly used in the plural. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2022</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> Fat (f&abreve.t), n. [Add.] 4. (Agric.) A head of fat cattle; - commonly used in the plural. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,20</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> ... under favourable conditions a good percentage of the lambs can be sold as fats off the mothers. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,69</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> fn [a fat sheep, fat-stock] 1. I remember shortly after arrival in New Zealand watching the well-known dealer, Andrew Grant, picking `fats.' When asked by me for his hesitancy and discrimination in regard to several apparently prime blacks, his answer was that they `killed' badly. So many statements, nevertheless, continue to be accepted because it is nobody's interest to disprove them, that this dealer's belief is given for what it was worth. On the other hand, what Sandy Grant did not know of sheep was not worth knowing. <i>(1926,Tutira,362</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> During the Gold Rush most of the fats went to the West Coast. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,123</i>)

>  A Build up ... a greater reserve of dry feed ... so that when stock numbers must be reduced, such `surplus' stock are disposed of as `fats' and not as stores. In addition, permanent stock are maintained in good condition. <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/327</i>)

>  <b>MACFARLAND</b> ... a mob of fats. <i>(1946,Amuri,111</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> `... For eight years I have done nothing but cook and wash up, bake scones and serve morning tea, listen to dull talk about fats and stores, or put up with being ignored.' <i>(Mar 12, 1953,Shepherd's Calendar,116,1961 ed.</i>)

>  fats n.pl. shortened form of fat cattle. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  <b>HAYES</b> Rape was now being grown, and lambs fattened on it could be sold sold as fats and not as stores. <i>(1978,From the Toss of a Coin,111</i>)

>  fat ... (in pl.) NZ & Austral. cattle or sheep ready for slaughter. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,272</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng.Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> [attrib.]

>  <b>SHARPE</b> FIRST FAT DRIVE [heading] ... So, after long, anxious conversations with the local fat lamb buyer, ... the farmer fixes a day for the dispatch of a first draft to the works. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,43</i>)

>  <b>SCOTTER</b> [Prices] were reported as good; ten and sixpence to a guinea for stud ... rams and lambs at `fat' prices - 8/- to 13/7. (Mail, March 17, 1909.) <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,36</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [(rabbiters')]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> One night we sat fatting skins by the fire. We'd had a good kill of rabbits that day, ... <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,107</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> After I would help to pick up and skin the rabbits. After tea ... Archie would `fat' while I wired. -->sc. rabbit skins <i>(1965,Skippers Road,48</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> We searched our lines and found quite a number of rabbits, which we skinned and then fatted and wired the skins ... You had to scrape the fat off each skin with a pocket knife ... <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,65</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... the skins were wired and fatted in the evening. <i>(1973,Big Country,(1977) ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a prime farm animal destined for the freezing works and/or export trade.

>  Masterton Stock Sales: No fats were offered. <i>(Jun 2, 1904,Eketahuna Express & County Gazette,2</i>)

>  .. the fats, both cattle and sheep, had a reputation all over the island. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,48</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> .. the manager asked me if I would give a hand to muster the cattle, as the fats had to be drafted out and sent to Dunedin. <i>(1922,Reminiscences of a New Chum in Otago,48</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> The wether lambs are carried over until the following seasons to be sold as fats. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,463</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> When a farmer has got rid of a draft of fats he descends on the pub with a powerful thirst ... <i>(1962,Country Occasions,48</i>)

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> We sold them all [cattle] next summer and autumn as fats to Richmond. <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,61</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to strip rabbit skins of fat.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> One night we sat fatting skins by the fire. <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,107</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Many a winter's night I spent sitting huddled up in the woolshed fatting and wiring skins. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,228</i>)

### fat as mud
 
 <i>phr.</i> applied to prime beasts and to babies: very fat (but in complimentary sense). [ca1935 Havelock]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In the 'sixties Fairlight was mostly a cattle run, although carrying a number of sheep. There was beautiful feed on the run, the real sweet native vegetation, and the stock were fattened for the big Queenstown market which came into existence when the hungry diggers rushed the goldfields in the Shotover and Arrow districts in 1862. When the cattle were in prime condition they were described as being `as fat as mud.' <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,44</i>)

### fat buyer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an agent who selects prime stock on farm properties for purchase.

>  <b>SHARPE</b> It pays to open gates for a fat buyer. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,45</i>)
