 <i>n.</i>

>  Graffiti Speak... Cap, fat or skinny: Interchangeable spray can nozzle fitted to can to vary width of spray. <i>(Nov 30, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

### fat client
 
 <i>n.</i> hospital jargon for individual personal commuters

>  Thus, fat clients would be individual personal commuters. <i>(Feb 21, 2002,Dominion,3</i>)

### fat draft
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a muster and selection of prime stock for slaughter. (See [fat-drafting](../F#fat-drafting))

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Clearing up sheep left in yards after the fat draft. <i>(Mar 10, 1943,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

### fat hen
 
 <i>n.</i> [Var. <i>Chenopodium</i> spp., esp. as eaten as vegetables: earliest recorded occurrence.] <i>Chenopodium</i> spp. Also with distinguishing epithets. Also called goosefoot (transl. of <i>Chenopodium</i>. =white fat hen, =goose foot, =red fathen ex</i> which are eaten as vegetables. [OED: A name for certain plants of the Goosefoot tribe, <i>Chenopodium Bonus-Henricus</i> and <i>Atriplex patula</i>. Also, in Australia, applied to various species of <i>Chenopodium</i> and <i>Atripl]

>  <b>BANKS</b> We also once or twice met with a herb like that which the country people in England call Lambs quarters or Fat hen, which we boiled instead of Greens. [fn 10] The New Zealand variety of this herb is <i>Chenopodium triandrum</i>. It may be added, as a philogical curiosity, that Bank's words, taken over into Hawkesworth, become in OED the first literary mention of `lambs' quarters', though `fat-hen' is here ignored in favour of a 1795 appearance. <i>(Mar 1770,Banks Journal,2/8,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> -->1980 Taylor Weeds of Crops 67 NETTLE-LEAVED FATHEN (<i>Chenopodium murale</i>). ... This cosmopolitan plant is widespread in New Zealand but not abundant enough to cause much trouble. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,67,[1967] Hilgendorf [and Calder] ed.</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> FAT HEN (<i>Chenopodium album</i>), called also white fat hen or goose foot, is found in gardens and cultivated fields in great abundance almost everywhere, but [sic] most aggressive in the drier agricultural districts. <i>Ibid.</i> 68 RED FAT HEN (<i>Chenopodium murale</i>), also called nettle-leaved fat hen or roadsides and waste places throughout both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,67,[1967] Hilgendorf [and Calder] ed.</i>)

>  <i>nettle-leaved fathen [s] Chenopodium murale</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,52</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> FATHEN (<i>Chenopodium album</i> agg.). ... This cosmopolitan plant is widespread throughout the world as a weed of cultivated ground. It was introduced to New Zealand to become the most important weed of crops and gardens because of its abundance in all districts. <i>Ibid.</i> 67 NETTLE-LEAVED FATHEN (<i>Chenopodium murale</i>). ... This cosmopolitan plant is widespread in New Zealand but not abundant enough to cause much trouble. <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,41</i>)

>  Huainanga, or fathen (<i>Chenopodium album</i>) is an introduced plant that was keenly adopted by the Maori. <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,41</i>)

>  "Fat hen," a New Zealand noxious weed, is now sown largely as a paying crop in England. They call it "New Zealand spinach." <i>(Jan 2, 1904,NZ Free Lance,22</i>)

### fat lamb
 
 <i>n.</i> a prime lamb ready for slaughter

>  Lambs, fat .. per head .. 7/ to 12/6 <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,165</i>)

>  Sheep-farmers are ... giving up this ruinous practice [of selecting the fattest of their flock lambs each year for freezers] ... and it is to be hoped that before long the rearing of fat lambs will be looked upon as quite a distinct branch of sheep-farming. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,304</i>)

>  And, again, there are the highly cultivated properties within easy distances of the freezing-factories, where fat lambs are grown on rape, turnips, and clover, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,96</i>)

>  And, again, there are the highly cultivated properties within easy distances of the freezing-factories, where fat lambs are grown on rape, turnips, and clover, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,96</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> It ... is a good breed where fat lambs are the aim. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,23</i>)

>  <b>STAPLEDON</b> Fat lambs are also largely produced on a Romney basis ... The Romney ewes would perhaps be crossed with an English Leicester, and the resulting flock ewes run with a Southdown ram to produce the `fat lamb'. <i>(1928,Tour in Australia and NZ,59</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> These gave a very good return, and the fat lambs brought about nine shillings per head. <i>(1937,Pioneering in Otago,101</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Fat Lambs at Seventeen Bob [heading] <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,86</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> This is the ram paddock. ... I don't go in for fat lambs right through. Like to breed my own ewe-hoggets. <i>(1953,Breakfast at Six,17</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Farmers use the term fat lambs for the meat trade. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,175</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> If I may venture to offer a suggestion to our fat lamb producers, it is that they should dock their lambs shorter than they do now ... <i>(1912,Impressions of a NZ Pastoralist on Tour,83</i>)

>  CONTROL OF FAT-LAMB PASTURE [heading] <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,292</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... white clover and perennial ryegrass are now dominant on large areas of ploughable country that is now devoted to fat-lamb production or to dairying. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,8,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> ... in all fat-lamb areas, gasping full sheep are a problem. They should be in yards for three or four hours at least before shearing. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> in combin. fat-lamb buyer, fat-lamb farm(ing) fat-lamb raising (and attrib.), fat-lamb trade

>  All lambs were picked for slaughter by a fat-lamb buyer as in normal commercial practice ... <i>(1945,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,26/62</i>)

>  On fat-lamb farms the bulk of the lambs are sold before the seasonal growth is fully advanced ... <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,24/201A</i>)

>  <b>COOP</b> On the other hand, shearing on fat-lamb farms is often delayed until the first draft of lambs is taken ... <i>Ibid.</i> 20 On fat lamb farms ... the shearing muster cannot be considered a muster at all and there should be no check or mismothering at the December shearing. <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,5</i>)

>  [caption] Right: Fat-lamb farming on the flat land in western Otago <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,15</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> [=export lamb] FAT-LAMB RAISING. The industry of raising lambs for the export trade and home consumption ... <i>(1927,Sheep & Wool Industry,50</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> In the summer irrigation could play a very much greater part in many dairying and fat-lamb-raising districts. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,1970,205 ed.</i>)

>  Lambs of the fine wool breeds of the South Island and lambs reared for the fat lamb trade are seldom shorn. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,710</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a lamb bred for sale at weaning time for the prime lamb trade.

>  Fat lambs 8s; good store lambs 6s 7d. <i>(May 4, 1883,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> Any of the various breeds or crosses now in the country will suitable mothers for fat lambs, provided they are good milkers. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,369</i>)

>  Which breed of sheep is best for fat-lamb production? <i>(Jan 20, 1923,NZ Journal of Agriculture,50</i>)

### fat lamb country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) farmland used for breeding and/or raising prime lambs for slaughter.

>  <b>COOP</b> In general one could say that store sheep hill country offers greater possibilities for early shearing than fat lamb country. <i>(1950,Shearing Ewes before Lambing,30</i>)

>  The breed [Wiltshire] is envisaged for 'genuine fat lamb country' and that includes Canterbury and Southland ... <i>(Aug 1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,27</i>)

### fat lamb farm
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a breeding or finishing farm where each year's crop of lambs are sold for slaughter as prime lamb.

>  On fat lamb farms topdressing now will improve the feed position and ensure that ewes are better fed before and during lambing. <i>(Apr 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,349</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> For fat-lamb farms the ramp could well be reduced to a width of 18 in. .. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,347</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Between the shearing shed practice of the store sheep run and the fat lamb farm there should be a fundamental difference so far as the owner or manager is concerned. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,165</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Four-year-old ewes passing from the hill farms to the fat lamb farms each autumn had increased in price ... [fat-lamb farm] <i>(1970,Pyne Gould Guinness: the jubilee history,108</i>)

### fat lamb farmer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer who breeds and fattens prime lambs for trade.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> The fat lamb farmers sell fat lambs and cull ewes to the export processing works and buy store lambs for fattening and cast-for-age ewes for breeding from the hill-country farms. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,89</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The fat lamb farmer buys ewes from the hill country farmer, and the lambs are fattened quickly by the mother's milk if possible. The store sheep farmer produces wool and some store lambs, and sells his older ewes to the fat lamb farmer. <i>(1961,Pastoral Farming in New Zealand,14</i>)

### fat lamb unit
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of farm

>  [Advt] Santoft:.. 5 dairy units estimated to carry approx. 45-55 dairy cows anbd replacements, and 1 fat lamb unit estimated to carry about 700-900 ewes and 50-70 cattle. <i>(Mar 24, 1953,Evening Post,4</i>)

### fat man
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  These farmers are described by these "gilded gentlemen," when speaking to other constituents, as the "fat man," their born and natural enemy. <i>(Mar 15, 1912,Dominion,2</i>)

### fat man's alley
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He hardly saw [the ball] thereafter, having to content himself with a couple of useful runs up fat man's alley. <i>(Aug 12, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

### fat man's track
 
 <i>n.</i> the shortest route?

>  I could run the fat man's track and score under the posts most of the time... <i>(Jun 25, 2004,Dominion Post,C12</i>)

### fat off the mother
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [of unweaned lambs] milk-fat.

>  70% lambs turned off fat off the mothers. <i>(Aug 1, 1919,NZ Farmer,1096</i>)

>  The lambs of the lucerne-fed ewes were fattened on green lucerne, and those of the grass-fed ewes were sold fat off their mothers. <i>(Aug 20, 1924,NZ Journal of Agriculture,123</i>)

>  In every case it should be the aim of the farmer to get as many lambs fat off the mothers as is possible ... <i>(Feb 1, 1930,NZ Farmer,153</i>)

>  .. only two out of every five of the light lambs were fattened by 1st March, and only one out of ten was fattened off the mother, while all the lambs heavy at birth were fattened by 1st March, and two out of every three of them were fattened off their mothers. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,112</i>)

>  Alternating three-months periods of growth and consolidation make it essential that, if the lambs are to be got away fat 'off the mother' they should have abundant milk during the first three months and first young growth or supplementary food to finish them off. <i>(Sep 1, 1937,Weekly News,81</i>)

>  This leaves just over 100 lambs not fat off the mothers, principally late lambs as a result of leaving the rams out late. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,501</i>)

>  <b>COOP</b> This lamb is known as 'milk lamb' since it is sent away fat off the mother. <i>(1949,Animal Nutrition,92</i>)

>  Their ultimate goal is 40 lb lambs fat off their mothers. <i>(Nov 5, 1953,NZ Farmer,20</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The usual Ngamatea policy is to keep all wether lambs and carry them until they are full-mouths, but this year for the first time they sent 2,000 wether lambs away fat off their mothers. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,153</i>)

>  Up to 50% of the lambs are drafted fat off their mothers the first week in January. [fat off their mothers] <i>(Nov 13, 1975,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

### fat pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pen of stock for sale in prime condition.

>  Graziers have neglected the fat pens, being satisfied to draft to the works. <i>(Jul 10, 1947,NZ Farmer,34</i>)

>  Most prices held up surprisingly well, with hoggets selling stronger in the fat pens. <i>(Jul 13, 1995,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

>  In the fat pens at the August 2 Feilding sale the top male hoggets fetched $71.50 ... <i>(Aug 8, 1996,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

### fat pigeon
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  He was one of the "fat pigeons" whom the commercial law was not meant to protect. <i>(Jan 16, 1904,NZ Observer,16</i>)

### fat score
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to assess the amount of fat on a sheep's live carcass.

>  Recognising the importance of lean growth rates has led Early to have a commercial drafter fat score each and every one of his recorded ram lambs for the past three years. <i>(Sep 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,19</i>)

### fat sheep
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There are other estates partly cultivated, and growing half-[96]breds, where stores and fat sheep provide half the gross profits. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,95</i>)

### fat show
 
 <i>phr.</i> a fat chance (a great opportunity, but always ironical implying no show or chance); also as an interjection

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> It would be corker if he could go outside with Carole Plowman ... Fat show! <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,63,1963  (1947 ed. 171) ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> fat show little or no chance, or `fat chance' (used in America too); eg `Fat show we've got of making the finals.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)

### fat steer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAY</b> We usually send up our fat steers in batches of a dozen or so ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/209</i>)

### fat stock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WELD</b> ... he will be guided by the accessibility of some shipping port, ... where he may dispose of his fat stock ... <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,6</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> Fat stock realized full prices. <i>Ibid.</i> 226 [quoting Weld's Sheepfarming] The wool ... ought ... to cover the expenses, leaving the increase, and the fat male stock as clear profit; ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),84</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Practically all the stock [sc sheep] on the runs in New Zealand is fat; and, as there is an over-supply for consumption, squatters are forced to turn their fat stock [96] into money by boiling them down for their tallow. There is a boiling-down works at Featherston, ... where thousands of these large fat sheep ... are boiled down. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,95,1935 ed.</i>)

>  Year by year the imports of potatoes, fruits, butter, and fat stock are decreasing, owing to increased local production. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,22</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> The fat stock of Westland is another adjunct to our pastoral wealth, and the district now turns out some of the primest beef. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,89</i>)

>  Under normal conditions the freezing season is from about the middle of November to May or June. The fat stock - i.e., cattle, sheep, and lambs - are ... bought outright from the farmer by the frezing company or meat-exporter ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/164</i>)

### fat stock buyer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an agent who selects prime stock for purchase on a farm property.

>  <b>SHARPE</b> .. the fat stock buyer is taught how to mix with country people without giving offence ... <i>(1962,Country Occasions,43</i>)

>  Wanted - a competent fat stock buyer. <i>(Dec 28, 1920,Otago Witness,1</i>)

### fat-drafting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the sorting of stock for sael and slaughter.

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> .. in late October 8000 wethers were mustered into the outback yards for fat-drafting. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,119</i>)

### fat-stock
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Fat-stock farms of the highest efficiency are situated on land of high fertility, ... <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,427</i>)

### Father of the House
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Father of the House member of UK House of Commons or NZ House of Representatives with longest continuous service. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,272</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### Father of the Nation
 
 <i>phr.</i> Edward Gibbon Wakefield

>  It still galls a little that he [sc. Edward Gibbon Wakefield]would later claim to be "father of the nation". <i>(Apr 19, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### Father of the Turf
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> One of these was Henry Redwood, known throughout New Zealand as `The Father of the Turf'. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,109</i>)

### fatherland
 
 <i>n.</i> England.

>  Possessing as we do some of the best and healthiest characteristics of the British constitution, I sincerely hope that we shall never adopt many of the absurdities which have so long oppressed the commercial and industrial classes of our fatherland, ... <i>(Aug 8, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

### fats
 
 <i>n.</i> a marble game played in and out of an ellipse. ? [ca1935 Havelock check]

### fatted
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of animal skins] stripped of fat.

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> On one occasion I could not understand why my heaps of fatted skins were not getting any bigger. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,228</i>)

### fattener
 
 <i>n.</i> a farmer who specialised in producing fat stock.

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Gradually introduced at the rival saleyards to many dealers, fatteners, lamb raisers, and other likeable `sons of the soil'. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,160</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stock dealer/farmer who buys in stock, fattens then sells them. (See [lamb-fattener](../L#lamb-fattener))

>  <b>STEVENS</b> More young cattle are needed by fatteners. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,194</i>)

>  Mr Sim is a fattener and turns off good lambs and wethers in season ... <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,50</i>)

>  ..while the prices paid by the fattener from season to season are closely related to fat stock and wool values, they show a much wider fluctuation from bad to good times. <i>(Aug 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,119</i>)

### fattening
 
 <i>n.</i> Used with reference to producing prime stock, of land or pasture, esp. fattening farm; or of animals, denoting the ability to be fattened.

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> ... supplying a good stamp of store sheep for the better and more fattening lowland country. <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,30</i>)

>  Fattening-farms. - Marketable products: Fat lambs, fat sheep, wool, fat cattle ... Fat-lamb and Dairy Farms. .. <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,24/200A</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Before long he had begun experiments on fixing a breed of sheep we call to-day `Corriedale'. Hitherto merinos were pastured on the runs and a few cross-breds on the farms. How to dispose of the merino ewe lambs? Crossed by the Leicester or Lincoln, the half-bred progeny was disappointing in size and fattening quality. ... Little gave it the name of the estate he managed - Corriedale. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,57</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> `...My extra choice store lambs are [pound]1 per head'. That evening a Mr. Wright ... asked casually if an offer could be procured of 800 to 1000 lambs for immediate fattening. `Yes,' said Jim, `I know of a draft,' roughly describing same. ...' `I leave to-morrow for Fielding [sic] to attend a sheep sale there, and may pick up some suitable fattening lambs.' <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,162</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> In 1880 a breeding property was acquired ..., while good fattening properties were later purchased at Makotuku ... <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,34</i>)

>  Fattening Farms [heading] These farms are on flat or undulating land that is highly fertile, either naturally or through topdressing. <i>Ibid.</i> 63 Some large holdings contain both hill country and the lower `fattening' type of land. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,61</i>)

### fattening block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm property used to fatten or finish stock.

>  This property has been presented in excellent order. Currently being run as a fattening block with another farm. <i>(Nov 3, 2001,Dominion,49</i>)

### fattening country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that produces pasture for fattening or finishing stock.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The Bartons had an area of fattening country on the flats ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,18</i>)

>  The Cowin holding is not good fattening country and the majority of lambs sent to the works are seconds. <i>(Sep 27, 1951,NZ Farmer,9</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The coastal flats of Homewood and the adjoining properties are great fattening country, producing big drafts of fat stock. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,48</i>)

### fattening farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a flat or low-country property used to fatten or finish stock for both local and export markets.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> In common with Lagoon Hill the 2-tooth wethers are shorn and sent to one of their fattening farms to be topped off. <i>(1969,Big Country of the South Island,43</i>)

>  Lower hill-country farms form a very important section of our sheep farming in both North and South Islands because of the large aggregate area covered and because they are the Source of supply of stock to the low-country fattening farms .. there is no clear demarcation between this group and the fattening farms but the two groups together probably account for at least 20 million acres. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,51</i>)

>  On most high production, heavily stocked dairy and fattening farms the strong growth of pastures will break the droppings quickly at most times of the year. <i>(Jul 1959,NZ Journal of Agriculture,27</i>)

>  4366 stock units wintered. All stock fattened. This is definitely a fattening farm. <i>(Nov 25, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,38</i>)

>  Three were six horticultural properties .. three grazing and each dairy and fattening farms. <i>(Aug 23, 2002,Dominion,C5</i>)

### fattening farmer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer who runs a fattening or finishing farm.

>  An opportunity for dairy farmers, breeding and fattening farmers or the large lifestyle person. <i>(Nov 27, 2001,Manawatu Evening Standard,12</i>)

### fattening land
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) farmland on which pasture is used to fatten or finish stock.

>  <b>WELD</b> He also looks on the country in a twofold point of view, as wool-growing or as fattening land. <i>(1860,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,18</i>)

>  In the 1960s he moved down from the hill country ("young man's country") and started buying fattening land around nearby Ohau. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 4</i>)

### fattening operation
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm that finishes stock for slaughter.

>  He runs a 406 ha beef fattening operation. <i>(Aug 8, 1996,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

>  Livestock position: A highly motivated person is required to assist in the management of our intensive fattening operation based 5 minutes from Hastings. <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,26</i>)

>  Together they bought 50 ha near Takapau for a fattening operation. <i>(Nov 2002,NZ Deer Farmer,08</i>)

### fattening paddock
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a swaggers' round or beat.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The Fattening Paddock stretched for swaggers from Kaikoura to Flaxbourne. <i>(1976,Swagger Country,64</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> Nor did I ever walk in the 'Fattening Paddock' as the swaggers called Hawke's Bay in the great station days. <i>(1977,Roughnecks, Rolling stones & Rouseabouts,32</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Man of War Jack tramped Hawkes Bay and the Wairarapa - an area referred to by swaggers as "the fattening paddock" on account of the large stations and friendly runholders. <i>(Nov 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 54/76</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced area where stock are placed for a short time to be finished for sale.

>  In a few months he had in his fattening paddock the lowest yielding cows ... <i>(Aug 30, 1912,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  Many of them [sale cattle] were passed and went back to the fattening paddocks. <i>(Nov 23, 1939,NZ Farmer,34</i>)

>  <b>ELLISON</b> The road went on past the old red woolshed and emerald-green holding paddock of R H Bailey, while on the left was Main's fattening paddock. <i>(1956,Whirinaki Valley, The,27</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> The only satisfactory plan, I soon discovered, is to keep drafting off the top sheep on to prepared fattening paddocks. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,122</i>)

### fattening pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small yard where stock are fed intensively.

>  Personally, I think the layout of the fattening pen should be copied by more dairy farmers. <i>(Jun 15, 1939,NZ Journal of Agriculture,544</i>)

### fattening station
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large property where stock are finished or primed for sale.

>  Shepherd - Ouepoto Station for 1780 hectare sheep and beef breeding and fattening station. <i>(Jun 22, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,29</i>)

### fattening unit
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a property where stock are finished or primed.

>  "Keith Hall" is a highly productive parcel of land that Mr Ian Parks and his family have farmed for over 30 years as a fattening unit. <i>(May 4, 1993,NZ Farmer,29</i>)

### fatting
 
 <i>n.</i> the scraping of fat deposits from rabbit skins to prepare them for sale.

>  <b>STROBACH</b> Here is done the most unpleasant part of the job - the `fatting' of the skin. On the flanks beside the tail and under the `armpits' are deposits of fat, and these must be removed. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,61</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> Fatting, wiring, and packing [rabbit skins] were some of the jobs I took on ... <i>(1965,Skippers Skins,48</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the scraping of fat deposits from rabbit skins to prepare them for sale.

>  .. the rabbiters have sat through the previous evening until past midnight, working by candle-light under the tarpaulin awning, turning each skin inside out, for 'fatting', and stretching it over a wire frame. <i>(Jul 10, 1947,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Here is done the most unpleasant part of the job - the `fatting' of the skin. On the flanks beside the tail and under the `armpits' are deposits of fat, and these must be removed. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,61</i>)

### fatwa
 
 <i>n.</i> ban

>  When the government's tobacco fatwa comes into force this December, the outside tables at the Wellington Brewing COmpany (WBC) will truly come into their own. <i>(Jul 10, 2004,Dominion Post,E15</i>)

### favourable country
 
 <i>n.</i> Country whose appearance indicates a prospectof gold. See also (kindly) country. [goldmining]

>  The miner, quickly recognising these characters, uses them in his search for ore-bodies, and calls any rock exhibiting them `favourable' or `kindly' country. ... The cause of the ore is the cause of the `favourable' country, and it is not correct to say that `favourable' country (in the sense used by the Hauraki miners) is the cause of ore-deposition. place in New Zealand during Captain Cook's first visit in 1769. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/115</i>) [NOTES: (Morgan)]

### faw-faw
 
 <i>n.</i> an outsider, a week-end resident

>  But there are plenty who are ambivalent about the onslaught of outsiders - nicknamed "faw-faws" by one local tradesman for the sound of their braying laughter - and the assumption that locals should be grateful for their prewsence. <i>(Feb 9, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,3</i>)

### FBT
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Fringe Benefit Tax

>  "I've seen the introduction of GST, FBT (Fringe Benefit Tax), the Employment Relations Act, the RMA (Resource Management Act), and the change from the old Labour Department to the Occupational Health and Safety regulations, " he says. <i>(Aug 10, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

### FDI
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Foreign Direct Investment

>  The absurdity of the GDP-based taregt, was nicely illustrated by the Boston Consulting Group's report "Building the Future: Using Foreign Direct Investment to Help Fuel New Zealand's Economic Prosperity", released with the Prime Minister's speech that opened Parliament. The report is less economic analysis than a public relations effort to get us to adopt a strategy of increasing FDI. <i>(Mar 23, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

### fearnought trousers
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Many a Man got a wet Jacket: but the good Magellanic Jackets & Fearnought Trowsers were found excellent in this hard blowing weather, & every man provided himself with them ... <i>(Nov 30, 1772,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/186</i>)

### feather
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>JENNINGS</b> We feathered our tent - the 12 x 14 & got tea ready. <i>(1880,Diary of an Expedition to the Table Land,2</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Feathering: After heading sheep some dogs will wait - or feather - before attempting to `lift' or `pull' them (see below). This has a quietening effect on sheep. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,168</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> A fern(-leaf) frond. [Cf Prince of Wales feathers.]

>  <b>MONEY</b> He cut `feathers' or small branches of ferns with his tomahawk for us to lie on. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,62</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (any) money (esp. in negative expressions: not have a feather to fly with: to be broke or penniless)

>  If Sandow when he struck Hastings didn't have a flag to fly, he likewise didn't have a feather to fly with, and it is no doubt because of his deplorable state of impecuniosity that he contracted to pay `Milky' a fiver a week, ... <i>(Nov 21, 1908,NZ Truth,179/5/7</i>)

>  <b>Lee</b> I haven't a feather left. <i>(1937,Civilian into Soldier,101</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs] to pause before attempting to move sheep on, after heading them.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Feathering: after heading sheep some dogs will wait - or feather - before attempting to 'lift' or 'pull' them. This has a quietening effect on sheep. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,168</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> feather: a dog feathers his sheep when handling them very, very gently. This moment occurs after he has come 'on' his sheep and has paused to gain their confidence before lifting them. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,30</i>)

### feather box
 
 <i>n.</i> = waka huia, a box for keeping huia feathers in. [Nf W3.]]

>  A Maori treasure box perhaps a century old has been presented to the Dominion Museum in Wellington. `Called a wakahuia, the box is a type commonly referred to as a `feather-box',' said Dr T. Barrow, keeper of the Maori and Pacific collection ... <i>(Sep 4, 1963,Dominion,1</i>)

>  A pleasant surprise was the presentation by Mr J. McEwen of a new trophy, a feather box he had carved in a Nga Puhi design, using a piece of wood brought back from Niue Island. ... This box, the Whaka Huia trophy, will be competed for annually. <i>(Dec 1966,Te Ao Hou,57: 31</i>)

### feather dinks
 
 <i>exclam.</i> ?An elaboration of fair dink(um)s. [(?obs)]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The Australian asseverations `dinkum', `fair dinkum', (honest, `straight') have produced in New Zealand the meaningless variant `feather dinks'. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,91</i>)

### feather fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pneumatopteris</i> (formerly <i>Dryopteris</i> or <i>Cyclosorus</i>) <i>pennigera. </i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Dryopetris pennigera</i> <i>(1908,AJHR,C-14</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Survey Waipoua Kauri Forest]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And). <i>Dryopetris pennigera</i> feather-fern <i>(1915,AJHR,C-6</i>) [NOTES: Plant Covering Kennedy's Bush (Canterbury)]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Quite distinct from these is the tall FEATHER FERN (<i>Cyclosorus pennigera</i>) common [116] in light forest close to streams ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,115,1929 ed.</i>)

### feather out
 
 <i>v.</i> of a lead, vein, to extend in a feathery manner, growing wider and thinner, sometimes eventually petering out to nothing (perhaps assoc with peter out). [(goldminers' spec)]

>  Throughout a distance of 800ft from the mouth of the drive these crosscuts show the vein to have an average width of 40ft, but from here southward a gradual `feathering out' longitudinally is apparent. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.4 (NS) 120</i>) [NOTES: (Fraser and Adams, Coromandel)]

>  It is noteworthy that the reefs did not persist up to the surface, but feathered out in the breccias. <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.15 (NS) 79</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Waihi-Tairua)]

### feathered
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  A tremendous deluge of south-east rain [October 1864] prevailed ... so it was not surprising that the Rookery River was in high flood and feathered when the coach arrived from Hampden ... <i>(1914,History of North Otago,168,1978 ed.</i>)

### featherfew
 
 <i>n.</i> a medicinal herb

>  Samuel Marsden's (1820) list for the Kerikeri Mission Station does include a few culinary herbs, but only two medicinal ones: "Featherfew" or feverfew used to combat headaches and rue, a poultry conditioner. <i>(Feb 2000,NZ Historic Places,76/35</i>)

### feathers
 
 <i>n.</i> [probably "Prince of Wales' Feathers"]

>  <b>JENNINGS</b> When we returned it was dark - the six tents were all pitched and supplied with "Feathers" (fern in this instance). <i>Ibid.</i> 3 These bunks took up the whole of the tent floor - on them the "Feathers" were placed (red birch twigs) feathered upwards and quills underneath so that the surface when completed showed only leaves & soft twigs. <i>(1880,Diary of an Expedition to the Table Land,2</i>)

### Featherston Street farmer
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Wellington) Cf Queen Street farmer.]

>  Other people involved as `Featherston Street farmers', in syndicates of more than six people in such things as farms and kiwifruit horticultural units, are also liable for big tax bills when they want to sell these properties. <i>(Aug 9, 1972,Dominion,1/1</i>)

### feathertop
 
 <i>n.</i> a perennial grass imported from Africa, but widespread in New Zealand.

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> FEATHERTOP (<i>Pennisetum villosum</i>) Perennial Africa grass with ... fairy large seed-heads of striking appearance. ... common near Napier ... but still scarce or absent in most of New Zealand. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,49</i>)

### feathery grass
 
 <i>n.</i> toetoe

>  <b>STARK</b> <i>Cortaderia toetoe</i>, known as Feathery Grass, is a true grass found mainly near the coasts, around swampy places, or near streams. <i>(1979,Maori Herbal Remedies,101</i>)

### feature
 
 <i>n.</i> (of landscape i.e.)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> It was on Mt Patriarch [Marlborough], a high feature of rock and shingle above 2000 feet, that the last scabby sheep was killed by the `scab hunters'. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,56</i>)

### fed
 
 <i>adj.</i> short form of fed up.

>  <b>HYDE</b> .. to the accompaniment of bloody poppers, I'm fed! <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,191</i>)

### Federated Farmers
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand farmers' organisation, formerly known as NZ Farmers' Union.

>  Federated Farmers has polled farmers twice and met groups all around the country, always with the same conclusion... "Absolutely no doubt about it,: says Paddy Briscoe, vice chairman of the Feds' dairy section. <i>(Apr 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  As a union, we Federated Farmers are as wet as 10 scrubbers. <i>(Mar 9, 1967,NZ Farmer,45</i>)

>  .. she was also a guest speaker at the Women's Division Federated Farmers (now Rural Women) conference. <i>(Jul 8, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  But in the 1980s the political face changed with New Zealand heading into a free economy and under Rogernomics, Federated Farmers decided not to use any more SMPs. <i>(Sep 24, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,Farming Today Supplement 4</i>)

>  Mr Polson, a sheep and beef finishing farmer from hill country near Wanganui, is in his second year of presidency of Federated Farmers. <i>(Aug 24, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,15</i>)

>  Hertnon seems to live in some 1950 Federated Farmers women's division fantasy where all females are gagging to ensnare his puny form in the insidious bonds of matrimony. <i>(Nov 18, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  Federated Farmers... Represents 22 provinces. Formed in 1946 with the Dairy Farmers' Federation and a sheep owners' organisation... There can be no greater irony than Federated Farmers' enthusiasm for the new world being moulded by the Government. <i>(Dec 6, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

### Federated Mountain Clubs
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A major campaign had to be mounted by organisations such as the Federated Mountain Clubs to retain a reasonable degree of public involvement in park management. <i>(May 31, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

### Federation of Labour
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If we are to judge on the attitude adopted by the Federation of Labor(?) - the Socialists - towards Professor Milli's overtures of peace to divided toilers, instead of any unity having been accomplished, open rupture has developed, and the high priests of the Federation of Semple are hot on the trail of the little Yankee Professor. <i>(Oct 7, 1911,NZ Truth,328/4/5</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In 1908 there was a local strike [on the West Coast] and shortly afterwards the miners formed a Federation of Labour to which, within a few years, were affiliated most of the miners and waterside workers. The 1912 constitution of the `Red Federation' began with the preamble of the IWW ... <i>Ibid.</i> 280 The more militant unionists were unhappy about their regimentation in the Federation of Labour, which was formed in 1937, or the restriction of their action by compulsory arbitration and the Government's `stabilization' policy. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,198</i>)

### federation wheat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... Federation wheat developed in 1902 by William Farrer ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,151</i>)

### Federationist
 
 <i>n.</i> A red-fed, supporter of strikes in 1912 Waihi Strike.

>  Startling events took place here this morning as the arbitrationists were on their way to work. The calling of the word `scab' was the cause of it all, for when the word was uttered a serious collision took place between the workers and the federationists. <i>(Nov 12, 1912,NZ Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1912 Nov 12 NZ Herald (Auckland) in 1985 Bromby Eye Witness History 104]



 
 <i>n.</i> a supporter of political feder ation in NZ

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> To appreciate the reasons why many New Zealand politicians were federationists, it is necessary to glance back to a time when the colony's reputation was very different. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,209</i>)

### feed
 
 <i>v.</i> To lay out rabbit-food (on a block), to accustom rabbits to the same food as a poison-bait. [rabbiters']

>  <b>MILLER</b> One professional poacher I heard of used to watch a rabbiter `feed' his block - that is, to cut up pieces of carrot along the intended poison lines so that the rabbits would get used to the feed before the strychnine was applied - and then, after two feeds, he would step in one night ahead of the rabbiter with his own poison and make a kill. <i>(1946,There Was Gold,130</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The rabbits are then `fed'. Carrots, cut with a knife, or minced, or even cut up in a box with a spade, are scattered along the furrow, or `line'. ...The feeding is usually done three times, and the rabbits eat up the carrot each time. On the fourth night the poison is laid ... <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,60</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The area to be poisoned is first `fed' twice with straight oats and then poisoned. <i>(1973,Big Country,56</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a solid, heavy meal (modern coll, esp. a restaurant or hash-house fried meal). [(usu. a feed) 1984 16-17 F E10 Pakuranga Coll. 22B FEED [F5M8] -->1984 14 F E122Wgton Girls C. 22B]

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> He still comes down to the wharf occasionally to burgle a feed of dabs. <i>(1998,On the Next Tide,64</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> And he in less than half-an-hour/ Had such a pile of Johnnie-cake,/ That on the embers he did bake/ ... And went down with a relish high,/ With steaming tea and liver fry; -/ And that by men [sc. shepherds] so much in need/ Was well pronounced `a glorious feed'. <i>(1883,Castle Gay,177</i>)

>  I hated it there. My first time I hid in the dunny for about an hour. But she usually gave me a good feed - savs or snarlers, and sometimes a whole chip of strawberries. And fizzy drink instead of cordial. <i>(1988,University Entrance Board Bursaries Exam,6</i>)

>  [Kororareka] boasts a very respectable club, who gave us a great feed. <i>(Apr 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,216,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... after an astonishing feed on wood-hens and fern-root, they curled themselves up ... and fell asleep. <i>Ibid.</i> 86 Mr Handyside ... very kindly gave me a `shakedown' and a good feed, and the next morning I started on all the fresher. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,40</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> I have lost my way, and I am tired, and hungry. Can you give me a feed, and a shake-down for the night? <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/x/105,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> I'll shout you some real Kelvin Grove,/ Call at Alma, I'll give you a feed./ <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,33</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS & REEVES</b> He [the swagger] asked a `feed' from many a boss. <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,28</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> They were allowed admittance to the kitchen and a `feed.' <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,290</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> For God's sake send an empty-bellied joker for a feed to Terrace End. <i>(1903,Bill's Philosophy</i>)

>  A weeks hard graft at the wash-tub ... with three good feeds a day thrown in. <i>(Aug 26, 1905,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> I put the nips into the fellow in charge for a feed. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>) [NOTES: 1935 A.E. Strong WW1 scene in Partridge Slang Today 287]

>  <b>HYDE</b> Starkie provided his brother with a good feed: corn-cakes, fried vegetables mashed up with tinned salmon, floury scones, and the bacon crisp. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,49</i>)

>  Cripes, ... I'll go up ... and have some beers with the boys and talk about the footy and afterwards we'll have a bonzer feed at the Greek's on Seddon Street. <i>(Oct 1949,Here & Now,31</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> No, he didn't want a feed, he said; he's just got up from an `almighty binder,' thanks just the same. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,32,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> I have had a good feed ... <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,87 (? 37)</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> (Bill Henderson) Those blokes were either a bit lazy when it came to cooking up a feed, or they didn't like ... living on their own. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> growing grass; food, especially for livestock

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> [grass] ... we walked on ... to Captain D....'s station, which is one of the best ... having sufficient timbered land and a large open plain covered with good feed. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,24</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> There is no feed on this side of the creek at present - it was burnt ... and the grass will not grow again till Spring - ... <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,48,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> We shall have a splendid autumn ... with lots of `feed.' [for sheep] <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,270</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> We had for the summer of 1860, a bright season of continued drought; and though there was still feed for the stock, the whole face of the country was dried and parched. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,140</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> [sc. animal] Besides this, they let the cattle, for which there is very little food in the Teramakau valley, recruit here, since it is a grassy place, near ... Hokitika where feed grows. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,82</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> [New Zealand Speech] [on New Zealand speech] Pasture is `feed', herd and flock alike become `mob' ... <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,403ff</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> The 80 ewes and lambs appear to be making ... no impression on the feed. <i>(1939,Farmer's Wife, The,21</i>)

>  [nb not food] Lambs ... while ewes are being shorn ... are being wigged or eye-clipped ... Through eye clipping, wool blindness is avoided; a wool-blind sheep is seriously hampered on run country in its search for feed and consequently does not thrive. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,349</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Aerial topdressing has put an entirely different complexion on hill country farming. By aerial topdressing, and only by this method, can enough feed be produced to carry sufficient stock to beat secondary growths, ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,258,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> You may have been the last person out in a certain paddock, and may be asked, how's the feed? meaning, grass. <i>(1964,Doctor in the Mountains,30,1974 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp.use] grass, crop or other pasture.

>  Cattle should be removed from ergotized paddocks and be given a complete change of feed. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,253</i>)

>  On fat lamb farms topdressing now will improve the feed position. <i>(Apr 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,349</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> .. in the bush margin there was a bit of feed ... <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,89</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> You could not sell any stock in Marlborough as no one had any spare feed ... <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,69</i>)

>  Andrew Ralph says the farm was 'fairly short of feed' when the study began. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,61</i>)

>  It's pretty hard to get cattle finished through winter. The feed just doesn't have the guts to it somehow. <i>(Apr 2001,Growing Today,62</i>)

### feed bank
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) conserved pasture or other land cover [in case of drought or flood.] (See [banked](../B#banked))

>  <b>WHITE ET AL</b> The carryover of standing feed into the winter as a feed-bank is an important practice. <i>(1999,NZ Pasture and Crop Science,149</i>)

>  The ewes were on a one-day shift in an effort to get a bank of feed built up in front. <i>(Jul 28, 1983,NZ Farmer,12</i>)

>  Apply some nitrogen fertiliser in October - to build up a feed bank that carries you through into January/February. [bank of feed] <i>(Nov 2001,Lifestyleblock.co.nz,Dalton Pasture management</i>)

### feed budget
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [pasture management] to calculate future pasture grazing supplies.

>  I'm constantly feed budgeting. <i>(Oct 29, 1998,NZ Farmer,9</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a calculated supply of pasture feed and conserved grass.

>  Feed budgets are used to determine drying off dates and winter stock feed allocations. <i>(Apr 30, 1992,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  She is an accountant and, among other farm tasks, helps Bryn set the feed budget. <i>(Jun 1994,NZ Dairy Exporter,114</i>)

>  A feed budget involves an estimation of the feed requirements for all the stock on the farm. <i>(Nov 1997,AgFact,212</i>)

>  The arithmetic involved in calculating a feed budget is not difficult or complicated. <i>(May 3, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert Farmer</i>)

>  Have a feed budget done and do what is required to achieve it. <i>(Jun 7, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

>  He recommends a feed budget devised from the number of stock on the property, monthly feed requirements and the cost of buying supplementary feed. <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,Aug/Sept 10</i>)

>  Barns adds 15 per cent to feed budgets to ensure a buffer. <i>(Nov 2001,Dairying Today,34</i>)

>  Farm manager wanted for an intensive bull beef farm. Must be able to do feed budgets. <i>(Mar 16, 2002,Dominion,47</i>)

### feed budgeter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer with a careful feed conservation plan. (See [feed budget](../F#feed-budget))

>  Feed budgeters in the days before feed budgeting was a common practice, they made a success of the 280-cow operation ... <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,34</i>)

### feed budgeting
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the use of a calculation to forecast and conserve the pasture requirements of stock.

>  I love feed budgeting and analysing things and finding out what animals have achieved with weight gain. <i>(Sep 4, 1997,NZ Farmer,23</i>)

>  That's for people who want to be herd managers and covers feed budgeting and planning and other skills. <i>(Nov 1999,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  Exactly the same applies to feed budgeting. Traditionally most feed budgeting has been done in the head and on a fairly short term basis ... <i>(May 3, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert Farmer</i>)

>  Situations vacant- Seeking a conscientious couple who enjoy milking high quality cows, have an interest in feed budgeting and enjoy the challenge of maximising production. <i>(Jun 16, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,39</i>)

>  Feed budgeters in the days before feed budgeting was a common practice, they made a success of the 280-cow operation ... <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,34</i>)

>  .. they attribute the significant increase in milksolids from their two herds to their practice of feed budgeting. <i>(Dec 20, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### feed conversion
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the extent or rate at which an animal produces weight and condition from food.

>  <b>MAF</b> Satisfied stock have better feed conversion and higher ovulation rates <i>(1989,Report,106</i>) [NOTES: Economic Evaluation of Agroforestry in Otago]

### feed cover
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a supply of grass, clovers or other pasture growth.

>  When feed covers are declining weaning provides a method of reducing total feed demands. If feed covers are good, then weaning can be delayed to better match feed demand to supply. <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Farm Engineering,Knowledge Base</i>)

### feed crop
 
 <i>n.</i> [(farmers')]

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> `We could do with rain all right,' he said. I won't get a strike with any feed crops otherwise.' <i>(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,20</i>) [NOTES: (Takahe Man]

### feed down
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to graze a crop or sward heavily.

>  It may be stated that winter grazing of lucerne has never been practised at Ashburton. If growth is fed down in March, the stands are practically dormant during winter ... <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,386</i>)

### feed flavour
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the tainting of butter and cheese [from specific plant feed in the diet of dairy cows.]

>  While it is pretty well recognised that feed flavours as such will develop in butter, and can be described as characteristic flavours, feed flavours that arise through contamination are a different matter...a dirty milking-machine can create feed flavour. Experiments have isolated the bacterium which causes turnip flavour independently of the purely feed flavour. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,382</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> In the early thirties the pasture section of the then Plant Research Bureau, the Dairy Research Institute and the Morrinsville Dairy Factory collaborated in feed-flavours investigation of the Waikato pastures where the flavours were most intense. There was no doubt about the unpleasantness of these feed flavours in the spring, particularly in home-made farm butter. <i>(1970,Grasslands of NZ,336</i>)

### feed off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to graze.

>  .. chou moellier may be used with success when fed out to cattle and fed off to sheep. <i>(Feb 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,118</i>)

>  It was new ground broken up last spring, and put into swede turnips, which were fed off by cows. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,346</i>)

>  Cereal sown in the autumn should be fed off before growth becomes too long. <i>(Jun 30, 1934,NZ Journal of Agriculture,372</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> This [sowing in breaks] enables the crop to be fed in breaks, each of which should last for seven to ten days, with a fresh break at its maximum feeding value becoming available as the previous one is fed off. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,19</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> With kale, as with the other winter supplementary crops, it is customary to feed off in breaks. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,22</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> When the paddock has been fed off, dry stock are turned in, or the mower used, to clean up any roughage. <i>(1961,Agriculture,305</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> Sometimes the wheat is fed off by stock before harrowing. <i>(1961,Agriculture,327</i>)

>  <b>JANES</b> Years before, the supplejack, broadleaf, koromiko and other similar trees were well fed off. <i>(1994,Great New Zealand Hunting Stories,141</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  The turnip-crop is invariably fed-off by sheep intended for freezing. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,118</i>)

>  The turnip-crop is generally fed off by sheep intended for freezing. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,88</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I had sowed summer and autumn crops to supplement the pasture, and as soon as I was able to feed these off I did much better. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,33,1975 ed.</i>)

### feed on fresh air
 
 <i>phr.</i> feed (stock) on fresh air (and scenery)

>  [On benefits of shipping `choosy' lambs to works] Old ewes can be fed on fresh air and scenery, as people say, and they'll survive. <i>(Nov 22, 1985,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Farming Session from King Country ? Nigel]

### feed out
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> A few [Mt Eden] prisoners were employed `feeding out' (distributing rations) or cleaning ... <i>(1989,Punishment & Politics,167</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to distribute silage, etc., to animals in the field

>  <b>CASEY</b> `Won't you have them [sc. cows] running short of feed?' `Well ... Nobody's got cow-tucker to burn. I started feeding out before the end of April.' `How are you off for hay, then?' `Just about cut out, what the hell do you think?' <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,156</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The use of feeding out (food to stock) appears to be local. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,175</i>)

>  He was convinced that.,. the best thing was "to put on a lot of stock so the paddocks.. would be eaten out, and then feed out on as soon as possible." <i>(Jul 1959,NZ Dairy Exporter,39</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to provide supplementary feed to stock.

>  .. chou moellier may be used with success when fed out to cattle and fed off to sheep. <i>(Feb 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,118</i>)

>  The general remedy is stocking heavily and feeding out in the paddocks. <i>(Oct 2, 1926,Hawera Star,16</i>)

>  Double sided big bale feeders - 3 point linkage for the farmer who prefers to feed out both sides. <i>(Feb 22, 2002,Dominion,27</i>)

>  .. the man was feeding out deer just before noon. <i>(Jul 16, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,3</i>)

### feed pad
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a solid-based area where farm stock receive supplementary feed.

>  .. there has been emerging and increasing use of specific concrete for the purposes of dairy sheds, feed pads and silage pads. [feedpad; feed-pad] <i>(Jun 11, 2002,Wairarapa Times-Age,Farming Supplement  4</i>)

>  Leave the fancy bits like ration balancing, feedpads and feed-out wagons alone - they cost more than they're worth. <i>(Aug 1996,NZ Dairy Exporter,134</i>)

>  An existing shed was shifted to a site adjoining the feed pad. <i>(Aug 29, 1996,NZ Farmer,13</i>)

>  They [farmers] did not have to accompany cows to the milking shed or feed pad, because when the gate opened, the cows went through on their own. <i>(Oct 27, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,3</i>)

>  225ha flat to rolling with steeper sidling. 240,000 kg MS last season with feed pad. <i>(Oct 27, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)

>  I had a feed pad and I needed to get the feed into it. <i>(Nov 2001,Dairying Today,19</i>)

>  Disposing of effluent produced on the feed pad can cause problems for farmers. <i>(2001,Dairying Today,30</i>)

>  Excellent on-farm facilities include a 50-AS Herringbone shed with cup removers, a 350 cow feed pad and large calf sheds. <i>(Feb 2, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,23</i>)

### feed pinch
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a temporary shortage of pasture for stockfood.

>  The twin-bearing hoggets were very lightly spread through the bull-finishing block, eliminating any feed pinch for these hoggets. <i>(Nov 4, 1999,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

>  I've been calving later because of the feed pinch in spring. <i>(Apr 1980,NZ Dairy Exporter,11</i>)

>  Calving will start for both on Aug 15 next because of the spring feed pinch and a poorer submission rate in this season's mating. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,27</i>)

>  The feed pinch time under early lambs comes at any stage from August to mid-September. <i>(Aug 8, 1996,NZ Farmer,5</i>)

>  .. if they get in a feed pinch, that fat cow will start losing weight and run the risk of acidosis. <i>(Sep 2001,Growing Today,64</i>)

>  That will enable stock to be finished faster and killed at any stage if there is a feed pinch. <i>(Jul 2004,Country-Wide,6</i>)

### feed someone the fat
 
 <i>phr.</i> to chaff; barrack; sling off at. [ca1958 July 15 T. Edwards Forest Service trainee (Wellington) We fed him the fat till he just about jumped at us.]

### feed up
 
 <i>v.</i> It feeds (one) up; it makes (one) fed up. [a 1958 Feb heard by R. Mason at Hokitika]

### feed wedge
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) conserved pasture stock feed. (See [feed bank](../F#feed-bank))

>  Though pasture growth was spectacular in May, the farm's feed wedge was more like an erratic staircase. <i>(Aug 1984,NZ Dairy Exporter,7</i>)

### feeder
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1940 Havelock Put its feeder on]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Again there was no indication how it had got there as there was no sign of a feeder or wash anywhere, the sides running back into yellow clay rubble or `drift' as before. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,47,1963 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer who grazes or supplies stock with feed.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The Pullens are great feeders and their stock is a credit to their management. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,120</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ibid. 142 The Fishers are noted feeders and the aim is that everything that goes off the place is really prime. <i>(1971,In the Wake of the Axe,142</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Feeders: This term generally referred to the Challenge feeders on the stamp-hoppers. See Challenge feeders. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,273</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> Challenge feeders on the stamp-hoppers. See Challenge feeders. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),321</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EVANS</b> On this mill he worked as a straw-walloper on the straw-stack, forker on the sheaf-stack, feeder on the mill, and later as cook. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,273</i>)

>  <b>OGILVIE</b> [reference 1880] About a dozen men were needed to operate such a [threshing] mill. The engine driver who was the boss, the `feeder' who fed in the sheaves, three men at the bags, three who forked the sheaves to the `feeder', a cook, a `water joey', and a `chaffie'. <i>(1971,Moonshine County,151</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Trough or stretcher from which horses feed.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> FEEDER: Trough or stretcher from which horses feed. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,186</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 186]



 
 <i>n.</i> a worker on a flax-stripper.

>  <b>CARR</b> When the flax arrives at the mill it is weighed and credited to the cutter. The `bench loader' then takes two or three bundles and puts them in a barrel (heavy end down). He then takes the longest leaves and places them on a bench convenient to the `feeder'. ...[44] The `feeder' takes each blade separately and places the butt end into the `stripper' which takes the green outer covering off. ... The fibre as it leaves the stripper is caught by the `fly boy', who collects enough to make into a hank, which he places convenient for the `shaker'. The latter, with a motion like cracking a whip, gets rid of as much rubbish as possible, after which he places the hank handy for the `washer'. This man places the fibre in a vat, through which ... clean water is running. <i>(1913,Country Work,43</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [of sale stock] weaned and artificially milk-fed.

>  10 feeder calves for sale. <i>(Oct 27, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,45</i>)

>  Stock Report. Feeder bull calves $225. <i>(Dec 21, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  The market for in-milk and close-to-profit dairy cattle was stable at Inglewood on Monday. Feeder Friesian bulls $60-$155, feeder Hereford-cross heifers $80-$160. <i>(Nov 1, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

>  Inglewood sale: 10.45 a.m. Feeder calves. <i>(Sep 10, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,21</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a contributing creamery that provides cream for a butter factory.

>  The factory has seven feeders, creameries being established at Momokahi, Kohi, Fordell, Brunswick, Westmere, Upper Tutaenui, and Silverhope. <i>(1900,Hawera Star</i>) [NOTES: Hawera Star 28 4 ?page No missing.]

### feeder calf
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A good variety of feeder calves were on offer at Country Livestock's weekly sale held at Clareville last Friday. <i>(Apr 28, 2004,Wairarapa News,13</i>)

### feeding face
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a surface allotted to stock in silage, haylage or grain self-feeding systems.

>  <b>BARTON</b> A fundamental rule in self-feeding silage is the provision of 8 inches of feeding face for each animal if 24-hour access is given. <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,78</i>)

>  <b>BARTON</b> If there is limited access, there must be more feeding face ... <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,79</i>)

### feeding off
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A really proper investigation of the plants affording [158] regular sheep-feed on the montane tussock grassland is badly needed, and for this purpose the fencing-off and feeding-off of small areas is necessary. <i>(1916,TrNZI,48/157</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the eating out of pasture.

>  A portion of the area has been enclosed for feeding off by ewes and lambs. <i>(Feb 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,118</i>)

>  The field-pea being an annual plant, if fed off thoroughly it is not suitable for further feeding-off.[sic] <i>(Sep 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,250</i>)

>  The experience of Mr A J Jackett with the feeding-off of the blue-lupin crop by sheep is worth quoting. <i>(Oct 20, 1926,NZ Journal of Agriculture,260</i>)

>  Feeding off is a feature of the fat lamb and wheat growing areas of Canterbury .. Provided the feeding off is done by large mobs of sheep as quickly and evenly as possible, when the soil is in good, dry condition ... <i>(Apr 15, 1944,NZ Journal of Agriculture,248</i>)

>  On good land where spring growth is vigorous light feeding off of cereals with sheep will prevent lodging, increase tillering, and improve the yield. Hard feeding off, especially on the lighter land, where grazing may not be advisable at all, tends to reduce yields. Feeding off is generally carried out in August if the ground is sufficiently dry .. After feeding off the crop should be tine harrowed to loosen the surface. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,76</i>)

### feeding out
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) distribution of hay or extra stockfeed to farm animals [usually in winter.]

>  I think that in many cases feeding out is left too late in the season. <i>(May 20, 1933,Hawera Star,7</i>)

>  The sandy gumland hill pastures are showing continual improvement through the feeding out of hay during the winter to the stock. <i>(Jan 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,17</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> There were never many men on the station at the weekends, but the tractor drivers took turns to stay on Sunday to help with the feeding-out. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,95</i>)

>  Farmers not wanting to build a pit can opt for wrapped silage or baleage depending on their needs and feeding out machinery. <i>(Oct 19, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)

### feeding pad
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a solid-based area used for feeding supplementary feed to stock.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A concrete feeding pad runs the length of each rack and the shed is so situated that it serves two paddocks. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,190</i>)

>  Many people talk of loafing pads or feeding pads. They are different .. A sawdust pad is a stand-off or loading pad. You cannot feed cows on it. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,32</i>)

>  The feeding pad can accommodate many - not all - cows in wet spells. <i>(Jun 1995,NZ Dairy Exporter,7</i>)

>  We have an area of land we use as a feeding pad. It's stony ground which suits feeding supplements. <i>(Jul 1, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  Besides specialising in Rotary Cowsheds we also build feeding pads. <i>(Nov 15, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,21</i>)

### feeding paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced portion of a farm where feed is brought to stock. (See [sacrifice paddock](../S#sacrifice-paddock))

>  <b>AYSON</b> The custom was to drag each calf to a stake placed a little way off the track taken by the cows on their way to the feeding paddocks. <i>(1937,Thomas,43</i>)

>  On dairy farms it is easy to cart hay into a hay barn and distribute it from there to the feeding paddocks, but on large foothill farms .. it is a totally different matter. <i>(Mar 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,251</i>)

### feeding value
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the extent to which particular strains of stock feed will support stock. (See [grazing-value](../G#grazing-value))

>  Lucerne is rapidly becoming recognized by farmers on the rich Marlborough flats, where much of the land is composed of accumulated river-silt deposits, as a fodder-crop of unexcelled feeding-value. <i>(Feb 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,105</i>)

>  It requires to be fully recognized that a given weight of good mixture containing plenty of clovers will have a much greater feeding-value than double the quantity of rough grass. <i>(Oct 20, 1926,NZ Journal of Agriculture,268</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> This enables the crop to be fed in breaks, each of which should last for seven to ten days, with a fresh break at its maximum feeding value becoming available as the previous one is fed off. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,19</i>)

>  It may be possible to maintain the feeding value of Matua prairie grass pastures ... [feeding-value] <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,35</i>)

### feeding-down
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Even on land recently grazed very little sign of actual feeding-down can be seen, but certainly sheep do much better on land where there is an abundance of this grass than on land where it is scarce. <i>(1916,TrNZI,48/157</i>)

### feedout wagon
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an implement used for distributing hay, other dry matter and silage to stock.

>  Now a silorator and feed-out wagon are used to take the maize to the cows. <i>(Jun 25, 1981,NZ Farmer,27</i>)

>  Leave the fancy bits like ration balancing, feedpads and feed-out wagons alone - they cost more than they're worth. <i>(Aug 1996,NZ Dairy Exporter,134</i>)

>  For Sale: feedout wagon (cub) 3 tonne. <i>(Jul 21, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,39</i>)

>  .. larger farms and herd numbers mean a move to heavier equipment, bigger feed-out wagons ... <i>(Oct 26, 2001,Dominion,21</i>)

>  Feed-out wagons are proving a very popular item in farmers' shopping baskets this year. [feed-out wagon] <i>(Apr 9, 2002,RD1.com,#36 1</i>)

### feedroom
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm storeroom for grains and other stock feed.

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> There it stayed .. beside the feed-room door. <i>(1923,Dove's Nest, The,1</i>)

>  <b>PARKER</b> Once a rat got into the feedroom which is supposed to be rat-proof. [feed-room] <i>(1937,Jack and Boxer,18</i>)

### feeler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  About the cattle [for the West Coast diggings], I take 50 head as a fealer and if they answer I take a hundred. <i>(Mar 26, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,170,1950 ed.</i>)

### feeler fish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>FEELER FISH Bathypterois longifilis </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,124,1984 ed.</i>)

### feet
 
 <i>n.</i> = running feet (of kauri)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> 'About three-quarters of a million feet [of standing kauri] ... easily got out.' <i>(1905,Toll of the Bush,66,1985 ed.</i>)
