 <i>n.</i> (See [whitau](../W#whitau))

### feijoa
 
 <i>n.</i> (the tree)

>  <b>BURTON</b> One of the earliest introductions of feijoas to this country was in 1908 when live plants were brought across from Australia. The feijoa is now in commercial cultivation in New Zealand, mainly in the Bay of Plenty, but until about the 1960s the plant was not highly regarded. <i>(1985,Delectable Fruits Cookery,67</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> The feijoa, Feijoa sellowiana, is useful as an ornamental and a hedge plant as well as for its fruit. <i>(1986,NZ Garden Book,232</i>)

>  So good, indeed, that a few years back every second home seemed to have a feijoa bush in the garden. <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,10</i>)

>  He saw Constance's figure standing beside the feijoa tree. <i>(Jan 14, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  The Feijoa. Feijoa sellowiana. is virtually unknown worldwide as a fruit crop. It originates from South America, from the high altitudes of Western Paraguay, Southern Brazil, Uruguay and Argentina. Although it has been cultivated in New Zealand for over 50 years, it is a relatively new export crop. <i>(Jul 1936,Orchardist NZ,216</i>)

>  Though they are quite wind-hardy, feijoas can be hard-pruned. <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,10</i>)

>  Feijoas make very pretty specimen trees. <i>(May 20, 1949,Southern Cross (Wgton),8</i>)

>  Moray, who was showing me around, ran a souvlaki stall in Takaka, as well as growing feijoas. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,120</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (the fruit) [1986 a U.S. food-writer's written remarks: p.c.Tui Flower NZ Woman's Weekly (14 Jan 1987) Feijoas - Also known as the pineapple guava ... a flavor with pineapple and spearmint overtones.]

>  Did you know that Feijoa is almost more uniquely "Kiwi" than Kiwifruit? It's true! And Hubbard's is, of course, uniquely New Zealand! So marry the two up and you have got "Forever Feijoa". <i>(2000,Hubbard's 'Clipboard',2</i>)

>  Feijoas naturally fall to the ground on achieving maturity. <i>(Apr 1979,Orchardist NZ,69</i>)

>  It's that season again when all those feijoas fall to the ground and you promise yourself that you'll use them rather than let them rot. <i>(May 2000,Cuisine,173</i>)

>  How do I make sparkling Feijoa wine? <i>(Dec 1985,NZ Gardener,15</i>)

>  The Feijoa, Feijoa sellowiana, is virtually unknown worldwide as a fruit crop. It originates from South America, from the high altitudes of Western Paraguay, Southern Brazil, Uruguay and Argentina. Although it has been cultivated in New Zealand for over 50 years, it is a relatively new export crop. <i>(Jul 1986,Orchardist NZ,216</i>)

>  As the flesh of feijoas discolours rapidly when it is exposed to the air, peeled fruit can be kept light golden by being put into water to which a little salt, vinegar or lemon juice has been added. <i>(1955,Journal of Agriculture Cookery Book,168</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> A native of South America, the feijoa is grown in many parts of the world only as an ornamental plant. Favourable conditions in New Zealand, however, produce sizeable fruit of considerable commercial and culinary potential. <i>Ibid.</i> 147. Here is a New Zealand adaptation [or trifle] using feijoas, which are unknown in English cooking. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,146</i>)

>  Ray Hollis knows of sdeveral different ways to enjoy the syrupy, tangy taste of feijoas - in desserts, relishes, or even made into wine - but his favourite is to bake them in a pie... Natives of Brazil and Uruguay, feijoas were first introduced to New Zealand as seed in 1908... Now New Zealand is at the forefront of research on feijoas and is probably the only country that exports the fruit. <i>(Dec 13, 2002,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  When I was a kid on the other side of the world, the food I missed most was hokey pokey icecream. Now, on the verge of leaving again, I know it'll be feijoas... Feijoas taste pretty good raw... Their rich flavour goes a long way, which is good if you're short on either feijoas or cash - you can bulk out a feijoa crumble and even that chutney with apples at a third of the price. <i>(May 16, 2000,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> She baked a banana cake and then a Pavlova and covered it with tinned feijoa slices and cream and sprinkled hundreds and thousands over that. <i>(1991,Always the Islands of Memory,135</i>)

>  If your feijoa is not fully ripe and no one has told you that it's best scooped with a spoon rather than bitten into, you will wind up with a mouthful of gritty, sour pulp. <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,10</i>)

>  <b>LAW</b> Feijoas, tamarillos, peaches and most other fruits can be used in this yummy pie. <i>(1988,Dessert Cookbook, A,119</i>)

>  You should not eat a feijoa until it falls on the ground of its own accord. <i>(May 20, 1949,Southern Cross (Wgton),8</i>)

### felix
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SORENSON</b> FELIX - A trade name for a canned pet food comprising various poor-demand fish species and possibly filleting residues. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,23</i>)

>  [species name] mixed fish .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] roughs, rubbish fish, felix <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### fell
 
 <i>v.</i> [(absol.)]

>  Underbush before breakfast and then felled all day. I found a pigeon on its nest in a mahoi <i>(Jan 6, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/141</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

### Fell engine
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also Fell locomotive](../A#also-fell-locomotive))

>  <b>McKEON</b> It was the job of the Fell engines on the downward journey to grip this rail and prevent the train from gathering too much momentum. <i>(1971,Fruitful Years, The,15</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... the exasperated huffing of the Fell engines hauling the slow creaking train up through Price's and Siberia and Summit with the pungent smoke seeping under the doors and people coughing in their handkerchiefs. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,19</i>)

>  A Fell engine has wheels running horizontally and vertically on the tracks for extra grip on steep slopes. The last surviving Fell engine is currently in a Feathertson museum and would be used in the project... New engines would have to be built for the project as the Fell would not be appropriate for all trips on the line. <i>(Aug 29, 2002,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  A Fell engine being put into place to control the slow journey down the Incline... Those busy, noisy little engines with their distinctive double 'chuff' ready to pull us up again. <i>(Apr 2003,NZ Memories,No. 41/71</i>)

>  The incredible sound, soot and sight of a Fell engine working 'all out', especially when experienced from the open platform on the carriage immediately ahead of the engine, is not easily forgotten. <i>(May 2005,NZ Geographic,No 73 / 20</i>)

>  The only remaining example of the original fell engines, which has been restored and is displayed at the Fell Museum in Featherston, might be brought back into use... There were originally six fell engines on the line, built in Britain and assembled at Petone. Five were sold for $200 each and cut up for scrap. <i>(Sep 21, 2002,Dominion Post,F3</i>)

>  A project to restore the track, and get a Fell engine running on it as a tourist attraction, is being worked on by a group of railway enthusiasts. <i>(Nov 15, 2002,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

### Fell locomotive
 
 <i>n.</i> [John Barraclough Fell (an ancestor of H. Barraclough Fell of echinoderm and philological fame?), Englishman, b. 1815, patented the Fell system in 1863.] (See [Fell engine](../F#fell-engine))

>  The Wairarapa now has a rail link with the West Coast, a tunnel through the Rimutakas, which replaced the old Fell locomotives that used to cog their way over the range. <i>(Nov 19, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  Catching the train seemed the proper mode of travel for my first visit to Featherston's Fell Locomotive museum. ...[19]In fact, by the time Fell's locomotives started service on the Rimutaka incline in 1878, the technology behind his invention had already fallen from favour in engineering circles. <i>(May 2005,NZ Geographic,No 73 / 18</i>)

>  The famous Fell locomotives which literally claw their way at six miles an hour up the centre rail of the Rimutaka railway incline will be missed by the engine-drivers when the new tunnel is opened. <i>(Sep 8, 1954,NZ Truth,12</i>)

>  When the Carterton Show Special goes over the Hill and returns on the evening of October 29, the world's very last main line Fell locos will be retired, very honourably indeed. They have toiled mightily for almost 80 years. <i>(Oct 17, 1955,Evening Post,10</i>)

### Fell railway
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Fell railway incline is an incredibly important piece of engineering heritage. <i>(Aug 29, 2002,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  The unique Fell railway system - developed by Englishman John Fell - helped locomotives grip the rails on the 1 in 15 grade. <i>(Nov 2002,Footnotes,No. 18/6</i>)

>  Plans are now under way for the magnificent engines to once again steam their way up what was the longest-running fell railway in the world... The fell patent system was developed by English engineer John Fell. In addition to conventional wheels, it equipped locomotives with wheels that horizontally gripped a centre rail, providing extra traction and braking. <i>(Sep 21, 2002,Dominion Post,F3</i>)

### fella
 
 <i>n.</i> [->1951 16+ F 23 Marlborough C. 29 FELLA -->1951 15 M 9 St Bede's, Chch 29 FELLA -->1960 18C F B12 Horowhenua C. 29 FELLA -->1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 29 FELLA (2) -->1953 13 M A1 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 14 M A2 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 14 M A2 Thames DHS 28 FELLA -->1953 14 M A3 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 14 M A4 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 14 M A6 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 14 M A6 Thames DHS 28 FELLA -->1953 14 M A10 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 14 M A10 Thames DHS 28 FELLA -->1953 14 M A12 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 14 M A13 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 14 M A15 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 14 M A16 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 14 M A17 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 15 M A22 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 15 M A26 Thames DHS 28 FELLA -->1953 15 M A27 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1953 15 M A28 Thames DHS 11 FELLA -->1984 16 M E77 Pakuranga Coll. 28 FELLA -->1984 17 M E97 Pakuranga Coll. 29 FELLA -->1984 17 M E107Pakuranga Coll. 11 FELLA -->1984 18 M E118Pakuranga Coll. 29 FELLA -->1984 14 F E122Wgton Girls C. 28 FELLA -->1984 14 F E120Wgton Girls C. 28 FELLOW]

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> It's a beautiful bay but there were problems there. Fellas were getting shot, pulling out other guys' pots. <i>(1998,On the Next Tide,57</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Then got a blanket. Coupla books. By then all the fellas gone. Day-shift jokers, y'know. So goodoh to beaut there [sunbathing].' <i>(1978,Murder in Fancydress,135</i>)

>  On the train's a fella, come north to see his whore. <i>(Mar 13, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  [Kahikatea] always look to me a little like a squad of army fellas wearing busbies. <i>(Oct 2000,Growing Today,68</i>)

>  I requested pink carnations and white lilies for the coffin... I wanted Kirsty's "old fellas" to take her into the church and her "young fellas" to take her out and onward as a symbol of her youth. <i>(Jun 10, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  She came up to my daughter and myself, and said, 'What you fellas having, eh?' I said, 'What do you reckon?' <i>(Jan 27, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  "There's a few fellas on maraes all over the place who'll read this," he wheezes, "who'll go, 'Bloody hell! This is about us.'" <i>(Mar 8, 2003,Dominion Post,F12</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> If that Maori fella can do it, then so can I.<i>Ibid.</i> 55 He's just like the fulla you know who lives next door. <i>(2000,Maori Heroes,115</i>)

>  In the 80s Dick Frizzell made the tiki and the Four Square man the same fella. <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>)

>  Two Maori fullahs play music while you eat - they did "Ten Guitars", they did "Lucille". <i>(May 25, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>

 <i>n.</i> A barrier of any of a variety of materials which either protects or demarcates (typically a boundary fence) or encloses an area of of land. Often used or combined with a qualifyings epithet which distinguishes the composition or purpose. For (pioneer) fence types see basket-fence, bush-fence, ditch and bank fence, ditch and sod fence, dog-leg(ged) fence, hedge and ditch, log-fence, log and stake, paling-fence, pig-fence, picket-fence, post and rail, post and wire, post and stake, rail-fence, ring-fence, scrub-fence, seven-wire, snow-fence, sod-fence, stab-fence, stake-fence, stone fence, stub-fence stud-fence, taiepa (fence), wattle fence, wire fence. See also rabbit (-proof). Cf. fencing terms dropper, (spinning-)jenny, standard, strainer, stringer, waratah. [AND has entry at NETTING FENCE. chk DNZE at N] [[Of particular relevance to NZ pioneer farming history. `Bad fences make bad neighbours.' cf OED2 5.]]

>  Fence: (1) I suppose a fence is strictly a made fence of wire or rails, and a hedge a growing fence, but nine country people here out of ten speak of a gorse fence. (C.) ... <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
 <i>v.</i> to erect fences

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> H.P. & Mr.Stoddart gardening - John fencing - <i>(Aug 26, 1856,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,48</i>)

>  <b>T.C. KIERNAN</b> T.C.K. and Hughie fencing all day at the horse paddock. <i>(Jan 2, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Jan 2 Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 122 [ not Chudleigh; not Rives, Phillips; ? Beattie ECR]]

>  Fence: ... (2) to erect or (rarely) mend fences. (C.) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> `There's one or two good-looking housemaids here.' ... `Thinking of giving the fence a run', `And so are you if I know anything.' <i>(1976,Travelling Man,21</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Construction will involve clearing, laying and erecting a conventional 7 wire post and batten boundary fence including floodgates and gates. <i>(Mar 4, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,21</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Construction will involve clearing, laying and erecting a conventional 7 wire post and batten boundary fence including floodgates and gates. <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,21</i>)

### fence dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog tethered to a boundary in order to keep sheep from crossing. (See [boundary dog; dog shepherd; gate dog](../B#boundary-dog;-dog-shepherd;-gate-dog))

>  With the ghost of a departed bark, the old fence dog shambled out, as he became aware of my approach. <i>(Jul 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,278</i>)

### fence feeding
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the feeding of farm stock from feeding racks along fencelines.

>  Where only a few hoggets are being fed, the racks may be shifted more easily than when fixed to a fence. Indeed, 'fence feeding' which was so popular a few years ago when more or less permanent hay racks were put up along the fence lines, often results in permanent damage being done to good permanent pastures. <i>(Sep 15, 1937,Weekly News,83</i>)

### fence off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but sign. in NZ farming] to ring-fence, or to sub-divide land by either a temporary fence (break) or a permanent fence.

>  These two varieties were fenced off in the same way as the rape, and both plots were eaten off at the same time, the same mob of sheep and the same lambs being used. <i>(Mar 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,303</i>)

>  It was intended to fence off small areas and graze pigs for a certain period. <i>(Nov 21, 1927,NZ Journal of Agriculture,329</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> One other method of controlling bracken is to fence off and mobstock with cattle. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,195</i>)

### fence-breaker
 
 <i>n.</i> an animal which breaks through fences. [(? nonce farming)]

>  <b>ELWELL</b> He knew [where] Geddes' old horse, our old friend the fence-breaker, was feeding. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,218</i>)

### fence-puller
 
 <i>n.</i> ? Staple puller, or wire strainer. [[some unidiomatic use in this author]]

>  <b>ROGERS</b> Those blokes walking out to the gig with a fence-puller in one hand and a cross-cut in the other; <i>(1960,Long White Cloud, The,91</i>)

### fence-strainer
 
 <i>n.</i> [[get cits]]

### fenced-off
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of farm areas] subdivided or fenced.

>  The area over which the sow grazed was a fenced-off portion of a cow paddock.. <i>(Aug 15, 1939,NZ Journal of Agriculture,117</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> At that time there were only seven fenced-off blocks on the whole place, little different from any other similar run. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,42</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>

### fenceline
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the siting and direction of a fence.

>  <b>BOYD</b> The fence line was somewhere near present day Charles Street. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,28</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> He was ever forward ... in taking the heaviest end of a log if a fencing line was being cleared, ... <i>(1889,In the Shadow of the Bush,10</i>)

>  .. hay racks were put up along the fence lines ... <i>(Sep 15, 1937,Weekly News,83</i>)

>  .. the ewes have to be tied to a fenceline. <i>(Apr 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,336</i>)

>  Stable gully beds facilitate the maintenance of stock crossings and fencelines. <i>(Jun 1958,NZ Journal of Agriculture,560</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> He'd just as soon sleep in a tent along the fence-line. <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,106</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Fence line: The actual position of the fence. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,28</i>)

>  Local authorities are already experiencing the outcome of values clashing across neighbouring fencelines. <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,April/May 6</i>)

>  Teams competed to string straight electric fencelines, class wool ... <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,Aug/Sept 8</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> But he knew how they [wild horses] crossed a fenceline when, for instance, they wanted to get at better grazing. [fence-line; fence line] <i>(2001,Riding Country,134</i>)

### Fencepost.com
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a multi-functional world-wide-website of Kiwi Dairies and later of Fonterra.

>  David Walker, a spokesman for Kiwi Co-operative Dairies' $10 million Fencepost.com, said a decision on which website survived must take into account the traffic carried by each. <i>(Apr 26, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

>  Big New Zealand agricultural Internet site Fencepost.com this week announced an alliance with the innovative Woolnet system of wool sales.. In effect Fencepost.com is competing with the rival dairy site set up by New Zealand Dairy Group, RD1.com, as only one of the two is expected to survive the dairy industry's mega-merger. <i>(May 10, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,14</i>)

>  We use Fencepost.com and Live.ex to buy cows without going through an agent. <i>(Aug 31, 2001,Dominion,Supp 28</i>)

### fencer
 
 <i>n.</i> One who erects fences; one employed to erect or mend farm or station fences. ????? get from Chudleigh if not already have [AND 1881]

He is also a very good fencer, and an excellent helper in the garden. <i>(Apr 13, 1850,Report of Auckland Agricultural Society</i>) [NOTES: 1850 Apr 13 Report of the Auckland Agricultural Society in 1853 Swainson Auckland 140]

>  <b>HARPER</b> He has established quite a comfortable home under the flanks of Mt. Peel, with ... several men in his employ, shepherds, sawyers, fencers, ... <i>(Sep 1, 1857,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1857 Sep 1 Harper Letters from Canterbury (1914) 21]

>  <b>COOPER</b> ... splitter and fencer [? for post-and-rail fences] <i>(1857,NZ Settlers' Guide,125</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> ... a couple of men for contract work (splitters and fencers if you have tolerable bush). <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,88</i>)

>  Wilson down with posts to Fencers. <i>(Jan 24, 1866,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> T.A.P. went to fencers ... <i>(Jul 16, 1867,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,109</i>)

>  Fencers digging well at men's hut. <i>(Jun 20, 1868,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>T.C. KIERNAN</b> Took the camp over from fencers' camp. <i>(Jan 28, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Jan 28 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 125]

>  [first use by Chudleigh] Took a final walk to the contract fencers and pointed out the lines of deposit to the carters and departed for Hamilton. <i>(Oct 12, 1880,Chudleigh Diary,291</i>)

>  <b>INGLIS</b> The whare with its idle group of Crimean-shirted ... stockmen ... bullock-punchers, horse-breakers, fencers and general rouseabouts ... came back to us. <i>(1887,Our NZ Cousins,163</i>)

>  <b>ACTON-ADAMS</b> I presume we must look forward to ... putting a married fencer there who will build a stock-yard. -- <i>(Sep 6, 1905,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1905 Sep 6 ACTON-ADAMS Letter in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 92]

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Fossicker, fencer, rouseabout, and rabbiter he'd been,/ From Riversdale to Naseby every hamlet he had seen;/ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,62</i>)

>  <b>LOVELL-SMITH</b> [on coaching in the 1870s] On the return trip in the morning, Ned was stopped by some irate fencers, who declared in strong language that during the night he had flattened out some chains of iron standards. <i>(1931,Old Coaching Days,124</i>)

>  Fencer: Man employed to erect and mend fences. (C.) <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Some of the old fencers were wonderfully strong .. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,104,1954 ed.</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: fencer <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Old Jack, who was employed as a fencer in the off season, worked on the Lake for just on thirty years ... <i>(1952,High Country Journey,108</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Stock and the farmer change the appearance of the countryside: the surveyor's trig station, the engineer's stopbanks along the rivers on the plain, shelterbelts of pine and macrocarpa and the work of the fencer change the scenery. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,171</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Sometimes they'd share [the hut] with a fencer or musterer. <i>(1982,Wild Pig,43</i>)

>  We had a couple of contract fencers staying for a while who had a Landrover. <i>(Feb 2004,NZ Memories,No. 46/53</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a horse

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> `This fellow is a corker, reach wonderful, and a born fencer. A grand colt, and must win Steeplechases.' <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,171</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep

>  <b>DUFF</b> I bought her [an old ewe] in the sale-yards with [23] other odds and ends, and she was in charge in twenty-four hours. Fortunately she is not a fencer. <i>(Feb 21, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,22,1961 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep that attempts to escape through fences.

>  <b>DUFF</b> I bought her in the sale-yards with other odds and ends, and she was in charge in twenty-four hours. Fortunately she is not a fencer. <i>(1951,Shepherd's Calendar,23</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used first in Aus.] a person who erects and mends farm fences.

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> Took a final walk to the contract fencers <i>(Oct 12, 1880,Chudleigh Diary,291</i>)

>  The manpower at present consists of the manager (who is also the head shepherd) a married general hand, a married shepherd, four single shepherds, a married fencer and a cowboy. <i>(Jan 28, 1965,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> The fencers fenced, the paddock men mustered paddocks <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,73</i>)

### fencible cottage
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The little fencible cottages built for the men and their families were at first double units of kauri weatherboards with newspapers pasted over the inside to keep out the draughts. <i>(1983,Of Toffs and Toilers,143</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> [Hdg] Fencible Cottages... A new regiment to be called the Royal New Zealand Fencibles was to be formed in 1846, and the conditions of enlistment were posted in every camp in Britain. Two of the cottages provided for these settler-soldiers are at Motat. <i>(1976,MOTAT,86</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [fencible cottage](../F#fencible-cottage))

>  Some years ago, Housing New Zealand decided to pepper-pot a long-settled area of Otahuhu with new houses, thereby introducing people of a different culture into an area whose residents were in some cases descendants of the 1848 fencible families and tangata whenua. <i>(Jan 20, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

### fencible settlement
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Howick and the three other fencible settlements were established as a shield across the southern approaches to Auckland. <i>(Jan 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 67/14</i>)

### Fencibles
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>G. GRAHAME</b> Civilian labour continuing scarce, ... I have been compelled to employ [natives] at all kinds of work, - in rafting timber for the erection of cottages for the New Zealand Fencibles. <i>(Sep 23, 1847,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Nov 23 Letter (Auckland) in 1849 Power Sketches in NZ 283]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> During the year 1847, ... about 500 veteran out-pensioners, constituted into a regiment of New Zealand Fencibles, have been sent to Auckland at public expense, with their wives and families; ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,376</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The corps known as the Royal New Zealand Fencibles, ... was a body of veteran soldiers converted into military settlers, established through Governor Grey's efforts as a protection for the southern frontier of the Auckland Settlement. It consisted of discharged British soldiers, about three-fourths of them pensioners, and all men of approved character and physique. The corps was enrolled in England in 1847. The term of service in New Zealand was to be seven years (most of the soldiers became permanent settlers); ... on arrival in the colony each Fencible was given possession of a two-roomed cottage and an acre of land ... The members of the corps were required to attend six days' drill in the spring and six in autumn, and to attend church parade every Sunday, fully armed, for inspection. ... The settlements in which the pensioners were established were Onehunga, Otahuhu, [451] Panmure, and Howick; these places were practically founded as villages by the Fencibles. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/450</i>)

>  <b>NORRIS</b> After Hone Heke's rebellion ... in 1845, military settlements to provide a bulwark between Auckland and the Maoris had been established, at Onehunga, Otahuhu, Howick and Panmure. These settlers were military pensioners, called `Fencibles' and their villages were to form a ring of husbandsmen trained to the use of arms; a self-supporting guard. <i>(1963,Armed Settlers,36</i>)

>  <b>BUCKLEY</b> The first families [of military settlers] reached Auckland in August 1847 on the Ramilies from Tilbury. ... Known as the `Fencibles' - soldiers liable only for home service - they were provided with a house and land at the outpost settlements. <i>(1983,Of Toffs and Toilers,143</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> The Fencibles were pensioners from various regiments of the British Army, all of whom had seen over 20 years of active service in India, China, Afghanistan, or other countries. Although no longer fit for the rigours of overseas campaigns, they were all under 48 years of age, of good physique and character, and quite fit for the garrison duty which was theirs when they came to New Zealand in the years 1847-8; a few following in 1852. <i>(1976,MOTAT,86</i>)

>  The vibrant history of the fencibles and their Maori neighbours is very much alive at the village... <i>(Jan 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 67/14</i>)

### fencing
 
 <i>n.</i> any material used for fencing; fence-making

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Several natives on the beach from Waikari who had brought Tacopa for fencing. <i>(Jun 21, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,135,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> There was no wood for building and fencing for miles, or even firewood. <i>(1830,Letters & Journals,474,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Set two parties on getting posts for fencing, and some to saw fencing. <i>(Nov 6, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,263,1961 ed.</i>)

>  This day the Men employed fencing and clearing ground for a Garden. <i>(Nov 27, 1837,Piraki Log,61</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Fencing and ditching cost little, as labourers were numerous in comparison with the demand for labour; ... <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/61,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> I want a man for about two months to do all sort [sic] of station work - a little fencing sod work and post and rails - <i>(Oct 13, 1861,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Oct 13 Butler to Tripp in 1960 Maling Samuel Butler 56]

>  Fencing all the day and finished all the places that can be mended with sods, the remaining ones must have rails. <i>(Sep 13, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,58,1958 ed.</i>)

>  About [pound]1.3/- per acre for fencing if done in blocks of not less than 640 acres. <i>(Aug 2, 1875,Chudleigh Diary,237,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> After a spell of `quill-driving' it takes one a week or so to get into one's stride of splitting, fencing, bullock-punching again; ... Meanwhile, one can make good money at hard graft. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,61,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> The next afternoon, two men ..., whilst engaged in fencing behind Mr. Hay's house near Papakura, were fired upon by a party of the enemy. <i>(1879,Waikato War,55</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) battens and posts. [occ. wire.]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Gilbert was much impressed with the beauty of the birch [beech] trees .. observing that there was a 'deal of good fencing in them'. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,91</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Later, broadleaf was the timber chiefly used for fencing, because it was much less inflammable than totara. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,104</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> Packing fencing in tussock land. <i>(1946,Pastoral NZ,55</i>)

>  <b>CHINN</b> I remained with my brothers for some time, felling bush and cutting fencing for settlers in the district. <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,50</i>)

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> We put up three-quarters of a mile of this fencing. <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,166</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Chas and I put up post and rail fencing ... The first Overland Mail to the West Coast went through today. They had to borrow a horse from Enys to go on one stage. <i>(Jul 4, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,192,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... there was quite an excitement in ... seeing the changes the flood had made, and the mischief it had done to the fencing; - this was in process of being repaired. <i>(1868,Station Life in NZ,216,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> [Refce 1836] burning some of the fencing. <i>(1889,Missionary Life and Work,32</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Boys fencing and digging. <i>(Aug 16, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,327,1961 ed.</i>)

 <i>v.</i> [(obs ?)]

### feral
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  In real life, a town like that would be full of racist senior citizens, violent ferals living out their Mad Max fantasies and other assorted white trash. <i>(Aug 25, 1999,Capital Times,8</i>)

>  Okay, mate, I thought to myself, I can see where you're coming from - something about ferals and coal smoke that the cappucino drinkers can smile over. <i>(Jun 4, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> wild

>  The pinot noir ($45) is a delicately spiced berried wine with what Larry describes as "feral", as opposed to "wild" flavours.. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  We scatter goats and feral cats on the road to Raetihi, turn a corner and suddenly, majestically, there's Mt Ruapehu in the distance - its piping running all the way beneath us to the Whanganui. <i>(Sep 2002,AA Directions,18</i>)

>  By the turn of the 19th century, Hauturu's original population of tieke had become extinct - wiped out by feral cats... in the 1880s, the notorious tomb-robber and collector of rare bird skins Andreas Reischek reported Maori dogs and feral pigs wandering free all over the island. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/23</i>)

>  [Hdg] Fifty furry ferine felines found... We had long been concerned about the effect of feral cats on native bird-life, the health of people walking in the area and the risk of infection to domestic cats. <i>(Feb 19, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),3</i>)

>  The feral geese managed to stay out of range, sidling along steep slopes and complaining and they went. <i>(Jul 16, 2005,Dominion Post,E18</i>)

>  They were, Frame wrote, "feral children who ran wild, did not come when called, pulled faces and said bum and fart and f***" <i>(Sep 7, 2002,Dominion Post,F13</i>)

>  There was a time, just before goats became valuable, when I had to stop the car to allow a herd of feral goats to cross the road. <i>(Dec 11, 2004,Dominion Post,E17</i>)

>  Any one of those MPs might go feral on Shipley. <i>(Oct 3, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> wild, aberrant

>  The car parks are becoming quite feral places. <i>(Dec 10, 2004,Dominion Post,A14</i>)

>  She managed the unpredictable Tamihere's exit from cabinet while avoiding having him go feral and quit Parliament, forcing a by-election. <i>(Nov 7, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A12</i>)

>  While he sports nautical tattoos and looks slightly "feral", he enjoys what the city has to offer - the chance to play music. <i>(Oct 23, 2002,Capital Times,5</i>)

>  Waikato also has a long and dubious history of playing the man at the bottom of the ruck... It's a sort of feral Taranaki. <i>(Oct 27, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)

>  Mr Dunne spoke scathingly of Winston and his "feral inbreds" at a PR company's political breakfast this week. <i>(Sep 13, 2002,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  At times it felt like I was on one of those Landmark personal courses, except that this one had gone feral. <i>(Aug 11, 2002,Sunday Star Times,7</i>)

### feral-game
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The situation arose from a contaminated feral-game meat scare, and a press statement by the Minister of Agriculture threatening a ban on feral-game exports, which put a stop to most feral-game processing. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/2</i>)

### Fergie
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a Ferguson tractor.

>  <b>TINSLEY</b> The little Fergie tractor bucked as the last of the three cans was rolled off the moss-covered trailer. <i>(1985,Cheese Punchers' Ball, The,30</i>)

>  Now, when I take the Fergie up the farm, tears pour down as I remember what those poor animals went through. <i>(Jan 1989,NZ Dairy Exporter,10</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> The Fergie's wheels spun uselessly for a while, but the tail twisting eventually got her in. I explained that the old Fergie wasn't even one cowpower. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,123</i>)

>  I use the Fergie for the same jobs any farmer would .. lifting, carting, spreading metal, carting fencing materials and weed spraying. <i>(Jul 27, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,21</i>)

>  Apart from the ever-popular 'Fergie,' there are many tractor choices. <i>(2001,Lifestyle Farmer,Oct/Nov 54</i>)

### Fergusson's kelpfish
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Chironemus fergussoni</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Fergusson's Kelpfish <i>Chironemus fergussoni</i> (Hector) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,408</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 408]

### fern
 
 <i>n.</i> as a quasi-adj., or in loose combination or collocated with names of land features 'covered with fern, ferned'; esp. fern-flat, fern hill, fern ridge. cf. fern country, fernland. [nb AND fern-gully 1889]

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Walked over fern hills, to a small village called Taupo; and on, through swampy plains, to Mangonui ... <i>(Aug 21, 1843,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Aug 21 Selwyn Journal in 1845 NZ Part II (Church in the Colonies VII) 12]

>  <b>DONALDSON</b> ... away over the fern-flats. <i>(1860,Bush Lays and Rhymes,8</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> He conducted us ... through the bush, where we found a good blazed line, which brought us ... to the open terraced fern flat ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,13</i>)

>  The track we followed ran along a long valley lying behind high fern hills, ... <i>Ibid.</i> For miles and miles now there was an unbroken stream of soldiers, bullock-drays, artillery, packhorses and orderlies meandering over the plains and fern ridges of the sacred Maori delta. <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,73</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Flats are alternating along the course of the river with fern-hills. <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),295</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `We then had a stroll round until time to turn in to our fern bed under canvas, the novelty of which my friend enjoyed immensely.' <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,47</i>) [NOTES: >c1880s an anonymous tourist in 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 47]



 
 <i>n.</i> as simplex, or as common fern applied to a plant of open spaces, usu. <i>Pteridium esculentum</i>, =bracken; often {?occas.] to <i>Paesia scaberula</i>, ring fern (q.v. ?2.?. below); occas. to <i>Blechnum spp. </i>. See also bracken, fern-tree, hymenophyllum, lycopodium, para, polypody, ponga, Prince of Wales feathers, spleenwort [prob out], Cf. fire in the fern; land of ferns. See further 1989 Brownsey and Smith-Dodsworth New Zealand Ferns and Allied Plants. (See [also tree fern](../A#also-tree-fern))

>  `... instead of bread they eat the roots of a kind of Fern <i>Pteris crenulata</i>, very like that which grows upon our commons in England.' [fn] The common N.Z. bracken fern, <i>Pteridium aquilinium</i> var. <i>esculentum</i> (Forst.) <i>(Oct 21, 1769,Banks Journal,1/416,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>1/282</b> Firn(?) roots they likewise heat ... than beat them out flat ...; these firns[sic] are much alike if not the same as the Mountain ferns in England. [fn] Bracken, Pteridium aquilinum, var. esculentum. <i>(Mar 1770,Cook's Journals,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> [Savage talks about root of fern, but does not use fernroot] The fern grows here in great abundance <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),8</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>ELDER</b> Those parts which are not cultivated are generally covered with fern. <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,59</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Sep 6 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 59]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> [sc. bracken] The fern is an invaluable production to these people, who subsist in a great measure on the roots of it, from which they prepare a sort of bread. As it grows here in the greatest abundance, they may be always sure of an inexhaustible supply. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),142</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> ... covered with fern excepting two or three small patches ... some parts swampy and covered with reeds. Towards the close of the day we passed through 2 large wood separated from each other by a tract of open country ... <i>(1820,Journal,20</i>)

>  The hair seal, generally resort to places where there is plenty of fern growing and here they breed; ... <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,105,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MATHEW</b> I found, however, that what has the appearance of Downs is in reality covered with Fern, so thick and strong as to be almost impenetrable, ... added to which it is entangled ... with the vines of the wild Convolvulus, which grows everywhere in the greatest luxuriance, so that to traverse it is all but impossible. <i>(Feb 27, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 27 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 64]

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> The country about Tawranga for about ten miles inland is almost a perfect level covered with fern; ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,31,1952 ed.</i>)

>  Natives and self off exploring, when we found a patch of fern, which we quickly began rooting up to examine its quality. <i>(Feb 7, 1848,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Feb 7 Brunner Nelson Examiner in 1952 Brunner 74]

>  <b>SENIOR</b> The fern (bracken) is dense. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,211</i>)

>  <b>FROUDE</b> (And) <i>Pteridium esculentum</i> <i>(1886,Oceana</i>)

>  (And) <i>Pteridium esculentum</i> <i>(1912,TrNZI,44</i>)

>  <b>DOBBIE</b> (And) <i>Pteridium esculentum</i> <i>(1921,NZ Ferns</i>)

>  <i>Fern</i>: This name covers any <i>fern</i> growing outside the bush. The two chief kinds are bracken (Pteris) and the shiny green kind (Blechnum). (C.R., p. 3). <i>(Oct 14, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  FERN. A general term for heath associations dominated by bracken fern (<i>Pteridium esculentum</i>). <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> Fern-seekers would have little or no sport in the depths of the Bush, but just inside, it is deeply carpeted with Ferns. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,54</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> We will ramble together in search of some of our New Zealand ferns. <i>(1882,Out in the Open,51</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> [Mr Buchanan] remembered walking along a track made by wild pigs through tall fern and flax to the site of the present homestead. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,7</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE IN WOOD (1959)</b> In cultivating fern land, the first operation is to clear away the fern. <i>(1849,From New Plymouth to New Plymouth,138</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Chimney stumps stood amongst ragwort and tutu, which killed cattle, and always the intractable fern, which had been waiting outside the fence all the time. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,223</i>)

### fern clubmoss
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>fern clubmoss n </i>: an epiphytic Australasian fern ally (<i>Tmesipteris tannensis</i>) with large lanceolate green leaves that grows on trunks of tree ferns <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,839</i>)

### fern country
 
 <i>n.</i> land on which bracken or <i>Paesia</i> spp. is growing or has grown; or land fit only for growing fern, or which has grown fern from earliest times

>  The greater part of that block was originally swampy and fern country. <i>(Aug 21, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... we ... got on an open plain, fine foot Path, through fern country undulating and quite pleasant for the Feet. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),62,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> .. many swamps had to be crossed, during our passage through an [95] open fern country, the valleys of which were clothed with a coarse wiry grass, called by the natives wiwi. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/94</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [Kapiti neighbourhood] consists of open grass or fern country, interspersed with swamps and marshes easy of drainage. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,88</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> The land for reclamation in New Zealand may be divided into three kinds: (1) The fern country, (2) the bush country, and (3) the flat or swampy country. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,230</i>)

>  `Fern-bird' is rather a misnomer nowadays, for, whatever the habit of the utick may have been, I have never yet, though riding over hundreds of miles of fern-country ..., observed the bird in this kind of herbage. <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/373</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Fern country is found throughout New Zealand but becomes more plentiful towards the North. Here there are large areas covered with the bracken (<i>Pteridium esculentum</i>). <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,6,1940 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> It was open fern country for the most part, extending from the foothills of the Ruahine and Kaweka ranges to the ocean. <i>(1926,Tutira,116</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land growing mainly fern or that reverts quickly to fern once it is grassed.

>  It is a charming little spot, surrounded by bush on three sides and with pretty, but worthless fern country on the other. <i>(May 5, 1888,Egmont Star,9</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> Of the fern country, that upon a limestone formation is the best and least expensive to break in. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,230</i>)

>  500 acres flat, balance good strong fern country, well-watered, partly fenced. <i>(Aug 2, 1912,Hawera Star,7</i>)

>  In the North Island light fern-country is often ploughed in September, as the young fern-shoots are very vulnerable. <i>(Aug 20, 1924,NZ Journal of Agriculture,125</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Fern stain - sheep grazing on fern country in spring or early summer may gather up this powdery material in the fleece. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,89</i>)

>  It will deal with another class of hill country for which there appears to be good scope for economic development in the district - bracken-fern country. <i>(May 22, 1969,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMSON</b> I am referring not to the areas of fern country that one sees in certain parts, but to country that has been bush and then grassed according to the usual methods and has subsequently been allowed to go back into this state. [fern-country; bracken-fern country] <i>(1981,Sheep Farming in the NZ Bush,5</i>)

### fern face
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a steep hillside covered in fern.

>  <b>SWAN</b> Today, however, the fern face was deserted so I wandered back to Terry's via a fast-running creek. <i>(1995,Chasin' Bacon,69</i>)

### fern flat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DONALDSON</b> ... away over the fern-flats. <i>(1860,Bush Lays and Rhymes,8</i>)

### fern hook
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a curved instrument for cutting fern.

>  He had had no valuable horses and expensive implements in those days - only one old nag and an axe and a slasher and a fern hook. <i>(Apr 29, 1932,NZ Dairy Exporter,69</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... as for the fern, it was up to him, he could please himself, although it was usually reckoned a fern-hook was the best; but he could see how he got on and stick to the slasher if he preferred. <i>(1975,Man of England Now,29</i>)

### fern moss
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Much the most attractive of the others [umbrella mosses], and the largest, is the FERN-MOSS (<i>H[ypopterigium] filiculaeforme</i>) which selects swampy forest floors or shady stations on the margins of forest streams. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,69,(1929) ed.</i>)

### fern post
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) ponga log or trunk [used for early building construction.]

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> Their habitat is the new house of fern posts ... <i>(Aug 8, 1854,BLANK ENTRY,154</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

>  <b>ATKINSON</b> The fern post house is no longer used as a dwelling house but contains D's forge ... <i>(Jun 4, 1856,Diary,209</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

### fern range
 
 <i>n.</i> . [fern covered hills?]

>  On Tuesday a fire started in a fern range, which ran on to a kauri bush owned by Mr. R. C. Smith, one of whose sons was on horseback close to where the fire started. <i>(Dec 31, 1886,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  With between 500 and 600 armed men in high spirits, excellent condition, and inflamed with the desire to revenge the death of their friends, we march twenty miles over hill, gully, and fern range, burn some whares, kill an ancient cripple, and return declaring that no enemy is to be found. <i>(Dec 4, 1868,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  The road lay over a number of fern ranges. <i>(Feb 12, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  His residence is situated on the saddle of a high fern range, seemingly the least attractive part of his property, but as you draw near you are at once struck and surprised with the vigorous growth of flowers and various shrubs, vegetables, &c ... <i>(Mar 4, 1893,Northern Advocate,3</i>)

### fern rat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Well then you see the opinion to which I incline is that our rat - whether the true Kiore Maori or not - is an indigenous rodent, the same which Professor Hutton calls the Mus maorium, and which we may familiarly name the Fern Rat, in reference to its usual habitat. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/206</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  It appears that the rat in question is not the common Norway rat, but an indigenous rodent known as the "fern rat". <i>(Jan 30, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

### fern root
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> Among the Maoris, as an article of food, fern root held a most important place in their social economy. <i>(1882,Out in the Open,82</i>)

>  When food was scarce, Maori ate fern root, which caused constipation. <i>(Jun 17, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

### Fern Shoots
 
 <i>n.</i> suggested name for women's hockey team (Black Sticks) [See Evening Post 25/9/2000, p. 4]

### fern stain
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a brown stain on the fleece-wool of sheep caused by the powdery spring growth on bracken fern (Pterdum esculentum).

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> fern stain - sheep grazing in fern country in spring or early summer may gather up this powdery material in the fleece. <i>(1968,Growing Better Wool,89</i>)

>  <b>SQUIRE</b> Fern Stain - wool stained by the spores of bracken fern. <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,20</i>)

### fern tree
 
 <i>n.</i> = tree fern, usu. Cyathea and <i>Dicksonia</i> spp.

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Last Sunday our people observed that the natives took the Stalks of the Ferntrees, broiled the whole & eat the inner-pulp. [ed. fn] <i>Cyathea dealbata</i> Swartz. <i>(Apr 23, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/263</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> `... they roast, or rather bake the root and part of the stalk of the large Fern tree, in a great hole dug for that purpose.' [[fn] prob. mamaku, <i>Cyathea medullaris</i>. (Also <i>fern root</i> on same page =bracken.] <i>(Feb 25, 1777,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb 25 Anderson Journal in 1967 Cook Journals 3/812]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The fern-tree, that ornament to the forest in these regions, was to be seen in many places growing with great luxuriance. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/232</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Another very peculiar feature in wood and forest-scenery of New Zealand is the growth of the palm and the fern-tree, the latter of which is a very remarkable plant. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),17</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We passed through several woods filled with fern trees and some very curious plants - ... <i>(Mar 15, 1839,Journal,2/74</i>) [NOTES: 1839 Mar 15 Taylor Journal [TS:ATB] 2/74]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> They are intermingled with fern-trees, especially Cyathea medullaris and Cyathea dealbata; while the cabbage-palm (Areca sapida) grows in the deepest recesses. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/223</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> We tried a species of fern tree called kakote, but it is far from palatable. <i>(1847,Great Journey,30</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> The luxuriant beauty of the New Zealand forests almost equals that of the tropics, indeed the presence of the graceful fern-trees, and elegant nikau palms, gives them quite a tropical aspect. <i>(1847,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes from a Journal in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 121]

>  .. clearing away all the ugly undergrowth from a number of beautiful fern trees ... <i>(Feb 11, 1855,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/156,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Jane M. Atkinson)]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The pith of the stem of the fern-tree (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>) ... [is] eaten. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/156</i>)

>  In the decoration of the [Otago Horticultural Society] show-room, the graceful young Red Pine, and the Ti and Fern Trees, ranked first among our native trees. <i>(Jan 19, 1861,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  Well and good, we plant ourselves not too much opposite one another off the road behind the fern trees, and wait. <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,19</i>) [NOTES: 1864? Von Tempsky Memoranda (TS ATB) 19]

>  Sometimes ... the outside was formed of hard fibrous slabs cut from the stout red-brown fern-tree, Wekiponga[sic] (<i>Dicksonia australis</i>); ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/35</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> The palm fern-tree was there, with its crown of graceful bending fronds and black feathery-looking young shoots; ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/6/102</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> There is the cabbage-tree palm, with bare shank and top-knot; the nikau palm, with weird and wondrous frondage; the lancewood, ...; the fern-tree, .... There is the twisting, squirming rata; the gaunt ... kahikatea; the golden kowhai; the dark velvet-covered rimu; the feathery red tawai; the perfumy mangiao; ... <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/187</i>)

>  <b>BROOKES</b> ... whitewood, fern trees, bush karaka ... puketea. <i>(1892,Frontier Life,20</i>)

>  Some people speak and write of them as `tree ferns' ... this is of course loose and misleading. There are fern-trees on my section up to fifty feet in height, and at least one of them - the wheki variety, is a hard-bitten fellow ... The unobservant are apt to lump all fern-trees together, as pongas, or, in the vernacular, `bungers'. <i>(Mar 10, 1920,Quick March,38</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... the slopes are black-pencilled with the stumps of the wheki, a fern-tree whose butt is as hard as ironbark and almost as indestructible. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/130</i>)

>  <i>Fern-tree n.</i> a large fern with a stem like a tree trunk; a tree-fern.-fern'-tree gul'ly n. a shady gully with its sides thickly covered with tree-ferns. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,4</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> There were no fern trees in the nearest bush, so the construction of the huts [c1840] was of a decidedly wattle-and-daub nature, but all were roofed with thatch. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,5</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> [The early runholder's home] ... might be of stone, clay, brick, earth, timber, canvas, ferntrees, or wattle-and-daub. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,20</i>)

>  <b>DEWAR</b> The principal building material used [on the Southland bush farms c1900] was fern-tree, known locally as bungie, upright on some walls, horizontal on others ... <i>(1953,Chaslands</i>) [NOTES: 10 and passim]

### fern was set aflame, the
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  It was "too late!" - a phrase which has been oft and mournfully repeated since "the fern was set aflame" in 1860. <i>(May 17, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

### fern-breaking
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the destruction of bracken fern by stock [known as hoof and tooth treatment.]

>  There are about 5 and a half winter stock units an acre on the effective area, 3 and a half on the total area. And that doesn't include summer-grazed stock which recently included 3000 ewes (for fern-breaking) and 400 dairy-beef bulls. <i>(Aug 10, 1972,NZ Farmer,69</i>)

### fern-bundle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The small <i>cray-fish</i> in myriads bred,/ With sunk fern-bundles lifted from the Lake:/ Next pounded <i>fern-root</i> pounded to a cake,/ ...; and the choicer roots,/ .../ The <i>kumara</i> [u-acute] and <i>taro</i>. [a-acute] <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/184</i>)

### fern-burning
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) the burning of fern in open country to destroy the cover of hostile Maori

>  On the 13th of February Jackson and I had sent our two subalterns with 50 men fern-burning on the Pironghia. <i>(1864,Von Tempsky Memoranda,94</i>)

### fern-clad
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Fern-clad gullies are the resting place for all manner of vehicles, from war-vintage saloons to four-wheel drive landrovers. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,108</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> clad with fern [fern-clad [OED2 1841], also fern-fringed [OED2 1842], fern-girt]

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> I did not dream then, that I should spend so many nights ... in the bush or upon the fern-clad hills of New Zealand. <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),296</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> On that morning the open country stretched away in vast fields of fern, and Nature reigned supreme. It is fern-clad now no longer, but green fields gladden the eye; the white gleam of the farmer's homestead dots the landscape, there are villas on the height, and cottages on the shore. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk2/5/99</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> To the right ... lie wave upon wave of manuka, gorse, and fern-clad hills, ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,212,also 1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> On the Sunday they set out across the big scrub and fern clad towards the Wakararas. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,118</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Long were the hours spent in the saddle, through the fern-fringed roads, seeking stock, or properties for sale, for the district was closely settled. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,160</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Past fern-girt gully and gurgling creek, with a wealth of bush beside;/ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,103</i>)

### fern-crushed
 
 <i>adj.</i> applied to pasture land on which the bracken has been crushed.

>  <b>WALLACE</b> In the New Zealand climate fern-crushed pasture-land of good medium quality will support one to three sheep per acre. [Previous paragraph decribes putting sheep on burnt-over fern, to eat new young shoots.] <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,231</i>)

### fern-crusher
 
 <i>n.</i> an animal used to crush bracken fern

>  Cattle are often looked upon as more efficient fern-crushers than are sheep on land suitable for cattle. <i>(Jun 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,435</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an animal used to destroy secondary growth fern and bracken on new pasture. (See [hoof and tooth treatment](../H#hoof-and-tooth-treatment))

>  Cattle are often looked upon as more efficient fern-crushers than are sheep on land suitable for cattle. <i>(Jun 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,435</i>)

>  .. cattle are generally regarded as the more efficent fern-crushers on land suitable for them. <i>(Jul 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> By the end of the 1870s the best land in the central coastal plains and hillcountry from Napier to Takapau had been freeholded, pedigree stock were joining the pioneer 'fern crusher' flocks ... <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,99</i>)

### fern-crushing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WALLACE</b> <i>Fern crushing</i>. Of the fern country, that upon a limestone formation is the best and the least expensive to break in. <i>Ibid.</i> 245 <i>Cattle</i> [bold] are required for jungle or fern-crushing ... <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,230</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The first care of the settler was to increase his area of grass by the operation known through Hawke's Bay as `fern-crushing' or `fern-grinding,' - words ominous of the part played by the unfortunate sheep, and which will be described later. It is sufficient now to state that after fire had cleared the tangled bracken growth, the ground was surface-sown and kept clear by browsing sheep. As the greatest growth of fern took place during late spring, it was then impossible to have too many sheep. Every squatter in Hawke's Bay was in the 'eighties `fern-grinding,' so that in those times sheep could not be bought at that season of the year. The result was that every sheep likely to survive the winter was kept, however old and however fleeced. It was at least a pair of jaws, a beast that could bite bracken. <i>(1926,Tutira,134</i>)

>  <b>BELSHAW</b> Hence beef-cattle are used for `fern-crushing', that is, for keeping in check the undesirable second growth of bracken fern over large areas of the North Island hills. <i>(1947,New Zealand (various authors/titles),40</i>) [NOTES: 1947 Clark in Belshaw New Zealand 40]

>  <b>RICKARD</b> An operation that seems to have been peculiar to the hill country of Hawke's Bay was the strenuous one known as fern-crushing. [ref. Guthrie-Smith <i>Tutira</i>.] <i>(1967,Strangers in the Wilderness,47</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> But there were no cattle coming up to listen. ... They'd been in there fern crushing, hundreds of acres of bracken. Men'd just walked off that land in the slump but those cattle were breaking it all in again. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,46</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) using stock or machinery to crush bracken fern prior to burning and seeding for pasture [or to check its secondary growth.]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> Fern-crushing. Of the fern country, that upon a limestone formation is the best and the least expensive to break in. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,230</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The first care of the settler was to increase his area of grass by the operation known through Hawke's Bay as fern-crushing or fern-grinding ... <i>(1921,Tutira,134</i>)

>  Fern crushing is at times severe on stock, and they should not be left on too long ... <i>(Jul 15, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,8</i>)

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> Another really extensive cultural practice that modified the New Zealand landscape was 'fern-crushing' - the replacement at the cost of continued effort, of the natural Pteridium (bracken fern) heath with artificial grassland. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,161</i>)

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> Another really extensive cultural practice that modified the New Zealand landscape was fern-crushing. <i>(1973,Hold This Land,5</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> He needed some cattle for fern-crushing, but he thought of himself as a sheep farmer. [fern-grinding] <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,75</i>)

### fern-flower
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Drosera binata</i>

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The Fern-flower or Sundew ( <i>Drosera binata</i>) ... vacated the barren edges on which perforce they had been confined. <i>(1926,Tutira,169</i>)

### fern-grinding
 
 <i>n.</i> [? rare or obs.]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The first care of the settler was to increase his area of grass by the operation known through Hawke's Bay as `fern-crushing' or `fern-grinding,' - words ominous of the part played by the unfortunate sheep, and which will be described later. It is sufficient now to state that after fire had cleared the tangled bracken growth, the ground was surface-sown and kept clear by browsing sheep. As the greatest growth of fern took place during late spring, it was then impossible to have too many sheep. Every squatter in Hawke's Bay was in the 'eighties `fern-grinding,' so that in those times sheep could not be bought at that season of the year. The result was that every sheep likely to survive the winter was kept, however old and however fleeced. It was at least a pair of jaws, a beast that could bite bracken. <i>(1921,Tutira,134</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the crushing and eating of bracken fern by sheep.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The first care of the settler was to increase his area of grass by the operation known through Hawke's Bay as fern-crushing or fern-grinding <i>(1921,Tutira,134</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Every squatter in Hawke's Bay was in the 'eighties 'fern-grinding'; sheep could not be bought at that time of the year. <i>(1921,Tutira,139</i>)

### fern-herald
 
 <i>n.</i> the catchfly (See [fern-pilot](../F#fern-pilot))

### fern-palm
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> The tree-fern, here called `fern-palm', abounds in all the scrubs. <i>(1859,Rambles at the Antipodes,108</i>)

>  <b>FROUDE</b> (And) <i>Rhopalostylis sapida </i> <i>(1886,Oceana</i>)
