 <i>n.</i> the catch-fly (See [also fern-herald](../A#also-fern-herald))

>  <b>HUTCHINSON</b> `fern-pilot' or `fern-herald', as the catch-fly is sometimes called. <i>(1947,At Omatua's Fireside,121</i>)

### fern-pounder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> It is possible that there may yet be heard the thud of the fern-pounder in some of the secluded valleys ... <i>(1882,Out in the Open,83</i>)

### fern-root cake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Made fern-root cakes, &c. <i>(Apr 6, 1849,Torlesse Papers,72,1958 ed.</i>)

### fern-root ground
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Sixty Tuhourangi fern-root grounds are identified. <i>(Dec 27, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),121</i>)

### fern-root pounder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> We came to the conclusion it [the stone artefact] was a fern-root pounder. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,16</i>)

### fern-rooter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) wild pig. (See [Captain Cooker; Irish Merino; grunter; tusker](../C#captain-cooker;-irish-merino;-grunter;-tusker))

>  There were numbers of wild porkers, all young and fat fern-rooters, how savoury! <i>(May 24, 1922,Northlander</i>) [NOTES: 1922 Northlander 24 May (VUW Fildes Clippings 461a/262)]

### fern-run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a property that is difficult to develop, due to dense growth of fern and scrub. (See [starvation country](../S#starvation-country))

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The energy .. of certain settlers in southern Hawke's Bay was proving that fern-runs could be made to pay, at any rate in good soils and in dry districts. <i>(1921,Tutira,123</i>)

>  We have a saying on the East Coast that it takes three men to make a fern-run, and .. there is scarcely a property but which has passed through three hands, but where the first two were ruined .. It is no metaphor to say the fern hills of the East Coast are strewn with broken hearts. <i>(1880,NZ Parliamentary Debates,36 377b</i>)

### fern-sparrow
 
 <i>n.</i> fernbird, cf swamp-sparrow

>  <b>BULLER</b> We are standing on the banks of the Horowhenua Lake ... a pair of Papango ... are floating on the placid waters; ... a solitary Pekapeka is flitting silently overhead, chasing ... the minute insect-life upon which this bat subsists; ... and the melancholy note of the Fern-Sparrow, calling to his fellows among the rushes, has grown languid and finally died away; ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/98</i>)

### fern-strangler
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Dracophyllum scoparium</i>, a kind of grass tree which strangles tree-ferns by slow constriction

>  [Potts gives the reason for the name - strangling of tree-ferns by slow constriction.] <i>(1888,NZ Country Journal,12/197/8</i>) [NOTES: nf W3]

### fern-tree
 
 <i>adj.</i> constructed of tree fern trunks

>  But at least they turned into the comparative safety of the bush track, and just as morning dawned, they reached the fern-tree whare. <i>(Dec 21, 1893,Otago Witness,9</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> A man named Adam Hunter built a fern-tree whare at Green Bushes ... <i>(1937,Thomas,130</i>)

>  <b>DEWAR</b> [(usual in this book) =TREE FERN] ... they were to come to know every tree ... within a radius of a mile of their fern-tree house there <i>(1953,Chaslands,10</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> We could see that the ferntree house was now too small for our growing family. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,67</i>)

### fernbelt
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Razorback was still busily feeding in the fernbelt. <i>(1984,Razorback,31</i>)

### fernbird
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Bowdleria punctata</i> [Also called grass-bird, ma[a]t[a]a(ta[a]), swamp-sparrow, utick.]

>  Fernbirds are still common on the New Zealand mainland, but there they are secretive - unlike the confident Snares birds. They have an unusual way of feeding, employing their tail feathers as a prop while using one or other of their legs to lift up twigs and leaves to look for insects and spiders. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/74</i>)

>  Our utick, or fern-bird, heard everywhere in swampy ground ... has become very rare. <i>(1908,TrNZI,18/125</i>)

>  SPHENAECUS PUNCTATUS, Q. & G. - Fern Bird. ... The peculiar chirp of the <i>fernbird</i> is yet to be heard among the tall fern. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/125</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  `Fern-bird' is rather a misnomer nowadays, for, whatever the habit of the utick may have been, I have never yet, though riding over hundreds of miles of fern-country ..., observed the bird in this kind of herbage. <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/373</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> [in 1873 English name COMMON UTICK only; changed to FERN-BIRD for 1888] SPHENOEACUS PUNCTATUS. (FERN-BIRD.) ... <i>Maori names</i>. Mata, Matata, Kotata, Nako, and Koroatito. ... It frequents the dense fern (<i>Pteris aquilina</i>) of the open country, ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/59</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Fernbird</i>. New Zealand birds of the genus <i>Sphenacus</i>. Also called Grass-bird. and New Zealand pipit. There are three species. The Fern Bird <i>Sphenoecus ounctatus</i>, Gray. Chatham Island F.-b.-<i> S. rufescens</i>, Buller. Fulvous F.-b.- <i>S. fulvus</i>, Gray. <i>(1898,Austral English,143</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> <i>Sphenaeacus punctatus</i> - Fern Bird - Reed Sparrow. <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,202</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Mata ... Sphenoeacus punctatus, fern-bird. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,215</i>)

>  <i>Bowdleria punctata Quoy & Gaim. Fern-bird. As with so many other species, the diminution in the numbers of the fern-bird is at first sight easily explicable.</i> <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/91</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> There were sandpipers and waders along the Molyneux and pukaki and fern birds were to be found in the swamps. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,120</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> [Stewart Island birds include] the fern-bird (<i>Bowdleria fulva</i>), the bell-bird (<i>Anthornis melanura</i>), South Island Robin <i>(1940,Rakiura,323</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> [Mata (fernbird) extinct: last seen in the late 1880s] <i>(1940,Te Waimate,229</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> FERN-BIRD (<i>Bowdleria punctata</i>). A small brownish striped and speckled bird with curious tail feathers, ... This bird has a curious double-note cry which sounds like `U-tick.' It is the Matata of the Maoris. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,86</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [Pascoe's note to Douglas's <i>Birds of South Westland</i>] The Grassbird [The Fernbird, <i>Bowdleria punctata</i>] ... <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,262</i>)

>  fernbird n [fern + bird] : a small passerine bird ( <i>Bowdleria punctata</i>) of New Zealand that frequents marshy ground and is becoming rare <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,839</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> FERNBIRD <i>Bowdleria punctata Other names</i>: Matata, Tataki Thrush (Foveaux St.). <i>(1996,Birds of New Zealand,205</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Fernbird - mata, matata, nako, potata, <i>Bowdleria punctata</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> There are, or rather were, six races of fernbird ...: North Island (<i>Bowdleria punctata vealeae</i>), South Island (<i>B. punctata punctata</i>), Stewart Island (<i>stewartiana</i>), Codfish Island, west-nor'-west of Stewart Island (<i>wilsoni</i>), Chatham Island (<i>rufescens</i>- extinct for fifty years), and Snares (<i>caudata</i>). They are flimsy-feathered birds, living inconspicuously in manuka scrub or clumps of bracken ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,277</i>)

>  <i>fern bird n.</i> N.Z. bird inhabiting fern lands; grass bird of N.Z., the pipit. Maori name is <i>mata, matata, or kotata.</i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Ma[a]ta[a] ... ma[a]ta[a]ta[a], n. Bowdleria punctata,fern-bird. </i> <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,185</i>) [NOTES: should this be 1971 or 1917?]

>  a native bird which lives in raupo swamps and is notable for its fern-like tail feathers; matata <i>(1979,Heinemann Dictionary of NZ Quotations</i>)

>  <i>fernbird n.</i> a New Zealand swamp bird with a fern-like tail. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,409</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 276; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 180]]

>  Fernbird <i>Bowdleria punctata</i> (QUOY & GAIMARD, 1830) ... North Island Fernbird <i>B.p. vealeae </i> KEMP, 1912. South Island Fernbird <i>B.p. punctata</i> (QUOY & GAIMARD, 1830) ... Codfish Island Fernbird <i>B.p. wilsoni</i> STEAD, 1936. Stewart Island fernbird <i>B.p. stewartiana</i> OLIVER, 1930. Snares fernbird <i>B.p. caudata</i> (BULLER, 1894). EXTINCT Chatham Island Fernbird <i>B.p. rufescens</i> (BULLER, 1869). OTHER NAMES: <i>Swamp thrush, grass bird, matata.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,274</i>)

>  As a result of their efforts they [Nukuhou Saltmarch Care Group] now regularly record up to 60 fernbirds in the monthly surveys. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,49</i>)

>  When [he] was looking for a fern-bird whose notes he had heard.. he saw six ferrets disappear in a rabbit hole. <i>(Apr 6, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  Fern birds for the wetlands, and whiteheads, bellbirds and parakeets for the forest are likely to be the next arrivals while kereru (wood pigeons) and tuis are also sometimes seen. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Dominion,9</i>)

>  Fern birds are to be found in good numbers around the harbour's edge, along with the New Zealand pipit. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/4</i>)

### ferndom
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large farmland area growing native ferns.

>  <b>HOBHOUSE</b> The whole remainder .. is either forest, ferndom or sheeprun. <i>(May 8, 1861,Letter</i>)

### fernery
 
 <i>n.</i> [OED 1840-1969 (Sydney)]

>  <b>FRAME</b> The roses in the rose arch and the ferns in the fernery. <i>(1960,Owls Do Cry,21</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> fern vegetation or fronds in the mass, esp. as decoration.

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... the Procesh Day lorry decorated with fernery like our tanks on a triumphal procession through liberated San Giorgio ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,226</i>)

### fernfighter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer breaking in land from native bush or fern.

>  I can't claim to be a female pioneer, from the waybacks, or a fernfighter from Te Kuiti, or the mother of ten, or anything of that kind ... <i>(Oct 30, 1926,NZ Dairy Exporter,41</i>)

### ferning
 
 <i>n.</i> collecting (native) ferns in the bush.

>  <b>BUTLER</b> It was with the daughter of this lady that I went `ferning'. <i>(1886,Glimpses of Maori Land,88</i>)

>  Ferns and Ferning [title of a local news item] <i>(Feb 4, 1897,NZ Mail,34</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The next day was very fine. Mrs Brown proposed that they should go ferning. ...[4] The girls spent a very happy day, and got a great many nice ferns and some beautiful moss. <i>(1974,Undiscovered Country,3</i>) [NOTES: 1898 K. Mansfield (`Enna Blake') The High School Reporter (Wellington Girls' High School) in 1974 Gordon (ed) Undiscovered Country 3-4]

### fernland
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) country that carries bracken fern or is most likely to revert to fern secondary growth. [formerly mainly as fern land]

>  .. good crops can be raised from all kinds of land in New Zealand; but that the fern land requires a good deal of cleansing, before it is fit to receive seed. <i>(Feb 27, 1841,NZ Gazette,2</i>)

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> .. we emerged into a little bit of open fern land. <i>(Apr 3, 1851,Journal,84</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Emerged from bush on undulating fern land burned about 2 months. <i>(Jan 14, 1852,Diary,122</i>) [NOTES: in Beattie (1947) The Pioneers Explore Otago]

>  <b>RICHMOND</b> Of bush I almost think we have enough, but I would gladly buy fern land with a proportion of bush. <i>(Jun 19, 1855,Letter,166</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

>  <b>PAUL</b> Fern land requires no previous labour (except burning off) to prepare it for the plough. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,97</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> .. the summer fallow is, however, the only fallow which sweetens fern land. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,II 316</i>)

>  For Sale - 50 acres of first-class fern land at Tuakau. <i>(Nov 1882,Northern New Zealand Settler,65</i>)

>  The fern lands of Auckland cannot be brought into good arable condition without being stocked for some years with cattle. <i>(May 14, 1883,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  For Sale 496 acres heavy fern land and ploughable. <i>(1909,NZ Farmer,361</i>)

>  <b>WILSON & FRASER</b> In the early days the weka or maori hen was common in tussock and fern land ... <i>(1936,Book of the Pioneers, The,82</i>)

>  There are two main types of fernlands in New Zealand - natural fernland, that is, land that carried fern before the first Europeans landed in the country, and artificially-induced fernland, or land which became covered in fern after the destruction of the forest .. Certain parts of Banks Peninsula are natural fernlands which have been in fern for a considerable period. <i>(Jul 15, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,9</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> Thus while the sites of most early mission stations lay in scrub or fernland, there was little difficulty in keeping cattle or raising pigs and goats. [fernland; fern-land] <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> land covered, or once covered, with fern, mainly <i>Pteridium esculentum</i> or <i>Paesia scaberula</i>. [See also <i>fern country</i>. Compare bush-land.]

>  Bush land is best: it is all humbug what is said about fern land being so good. <i>(Mar 12, 1842,Letters from New Plymouth,28,1843 (1968) ed.</i>) [NOTES: (1842 Mar 12) 1843 (1968) Letters from New Plymouth 28]

>  C. a working man on fern land. <i>(1942,NZ Journal,194/200</i>) [NOTES: (Saunders, Nelson) 1842 NZ Journal (1843) 194/200]

>  <b>FOX</b> The facilities, however, which fern land offers, compared with bush or flax land, have induced a large proportion of the settlers to prefer it for their first operation. <i>(Dec 1, 1843,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1843 Dec 1 Fox to Wakefield in NZ Company Reports 1844 14/121]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Onwards, through gently undulating fern-land, interspersed with bushes of <i>phormium tenax</i> and an occasional dragon-tree, we again found belts of forest-land. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/9</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The chief part of the land ... yet cultivated near Nelson is flax or fern land. <i>Ibid.</i> 375 [Cost of clearing land] Fern-land, 10s. to 1l. 10s.; Wood-land, 3l. to 10l. per acre, ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,258</i>) [NOTES: 1848 July 29 Colonization Circular No. 8, Fifth Edition (Land and Emigration Commissioners) in 1848 Wakefield Handbook for NZ 258]

>  The forest we passed through was not continuous ... so that every two or three miles we emerged into a little bit of open fern land. <i>(Feb 23, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/84</i>) [NOTES: (H.R. Richmond)]

>  <b>FITTON</b> BUSH LAND AS COMPARED WITH FERN [heading] `Almost all the crops ... have been taken from what is called fern land ... But ... I know that when I left New Plymouth district ... the bush land crops of wheat were deemed inferior ... to those of open fern lands; ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),101</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Fresh fern land is at first infertile through what we call `sourness'. ... Grass land is less subject to `sourness'; and Bush land not at all. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/331 [spg caps] Bush land is richer than either Fern or Grass land; <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/330</i>)

>  <b>WILLOX</b> The `Bush' or forest land ... becomes most speedily productive, but `Fern' land, that is the land covered with fern and shrubby plants is ... ultimately the most profitable. <i>(1860,Handbook of New Zealand,80</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> Not a tree interrupted the monotony of the fern-land. <i>Ibid.</i> 319 The road ... leads across open fern-land. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),254</i>)

>  The hills ... are of much lower elevation, and there is a larger extent of fern-land. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/144</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  ... open fern or tea-tree land. <i>(1871,TrNZI,3/167</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> The fore-ground is open fern land, partially broken and intersected by a fresh-water stream and one or two shallow gullies. <i>(1879,Waikato War,56</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> The grey scoria houses stood out on the bleak, high ground, unsheltered by a single tree, and all around was fern-land. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,62</i>)

>  The search was a very strenuous ordeal, for the party were led through heavy bush, tangled scrub, flooded creeks, and rough fern land before they eventually came on a lonely and almost inaccessible spot, where Tahi Kaka said the body of Freeman would be found. <i>(Apr 22, 1911,NZ Truth,305/5/1</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> There is more or less a fair share of fern and bracken land right throughout New Zealand ... <i>(1915,NZ Sheepfarming,115</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> The fern and manuka land refused to grow either corn, grass, or root crops, and most of our six hundred acres consisted of fern land. <i>(1935,Robert Maunsell,136</i>) [NOTES: 1935 J.W.Stack More Maoriland Adventures in Robert Maunsell (1938) 136]

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Immediately after the destruction of the forests fernland established, but, with continued burning, grassland began to spread ... <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,152</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> BUSH LAND AS COMPARED WITH FERN [heading] `Almost all the crops ... have been taken from what is called fern land ... But ... I know that when I left New Plymouth district ... the bush land crops of wheat were deemed inferior ... to those of open fern lands; ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),101</i>)

>  <b>RIDGWAY</b> [New comers] are puzzled by what is said about <i>fern-lands</i> and <i>bush-lands</i>. <i>(1860,Voices from Auckland,66</i>) [NOTES: 1859 Sep 6 J.C. Firth in 1860 Ridgway Voices from Auckland 66-67]

### Fernland
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand

>  <b>GREY</b> The hillside there was covered with a wonderful growth of the tree ferns, which plant has given New Zealand the name Fernland; ... <i>(1926,Angler's Eldorado,29</i>)

### Fernlander
 
 <i>n.</i> a New Zealander

>  ...we dashed off to South Africa, and later to Europe and the Middle East. We went variously as Fernlanders, Maorilanders and En Zedders, and the grim experiences of 1915 added Diggers and Anzacs to the list. <i>(Jan 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 45/14</i>)

>  <b>STONE</b> By defeating the Fernlanders the Springboks were justly proud in having gained for their country the Rugby supremacy. <i>(1938,Rugby Players Who Have Made NZ Famous,89</i>)

### Fernleaf
 
 <i>n.</i> (attrib. uses)

>  It lay just off the cross-roads - the little Fernleaf Cemetery. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,106</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> <i> `Righto, start up!'</i> Onward, men of the Second New Zealand Expeditionary Force; onward, as our fernleaf badges say, on against the foe; ... <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,17</i>)

>  When Miss Jessie Pope met the men of the Fernleaf Forces, their good fortune was not exclusively hers. <i>(Mar 14, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/14 : 40</i>)

>  What strikes me most about the Fernleaf crowd is their beautiful humility respecting the merits of their own country. <i>(Jan 17, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,I/10 : 223</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> A New Zealander, esp. a NZ soldier in WW1. [(?obsolesc. mainly pl)]

>  <b>FRASER & GIBBONS</b> <i>FERNLEAVES, THE</i>: A familiar term for the New Zealanders. (From the New Zealand badge). <i>(1925,Soldier and Sailor Words,93</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>mad money</i>, return fare, it being very generally believed by the New Zealand troops (<i>Fernleaves</i> or, as they preferred to call themselves, <i>Diggers</i>) that every English girl infallibly carried her return fare in case her soldier friend became <i>mad</i>, i.e., acted with excessive freedom of manner; <i>(1935,Slang Today,285</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 285]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Fernleaves</i>. New Zealanders; esp. N.Z. soldiers: military coll: 1915. F. & Gibbons. Ex the badge of the N.Z. soldiers. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,271</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> New Zealanders were <i>Fernleaves</i> and <i>Kiwis</i> [to Australians]. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,49</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> The kiwi, like the fernleaf, is now a New Zealand national emblem and provided the name by which Dominion servicemen were popularly known during the war. (The term `Anzac' correctly applies to veterans of World War I.) Appropriately enough, the nickname `kiwi' has also been applied to grounded airmen throughout the Allied air forces. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),8</i>)

>  <b>PUGSLEY</b> Indeed it was as New Zealanders that other nationalities identified us [in WW1], not as `Otago' or the `Canterbury boys' but as `Fernleaves' or `New Zealanders' and it made sense to do so ourselves. ... [fn] `Fernleaf' and `Fernleaves' - the slang term for New Zealanders. `Kiwi' was not used. According to Brophy and Partridge in <i>The Long Trail</i>, Kiwi meant a ground-duty air force man. A `Kiwi King' was any officer fussy about spit and polish, derived from the brand of Kiwi shoe polish. <i>(1984,Gallipoli: the New Zealand Story,13 and fn</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Fernleaf</i> New Zealander, from the badge worn by WWI soldiers; replaced by `Kiwi'. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,45</i>)

>  Call them "Cornstalks" or "Fernleaves" - all out for a fight - But don't call them Anzacs for that isn't right. <i>(Nov 15, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/6 : 127</i>)

>  With these words, Wiri Fernleaf executed a few steps of the Highland fling on the duck-walk. <i>(Feb 14, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,I/12 : 282</i>)

>  First Fernleaf:... I had a lot of trouble getting mine [sc. riding breeches] off a dead Ossie on Gallipoli. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,134</i>)

### fernroot beater
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A patuaruhe, or fernroot beater, made of maire (<i>Santalum cunninghamii</i>), another strictly Northern Island tree. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/61</i>) [NOTES: (Haast)]

### Fernrooter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This Fernrooter's off to the front. <i>(Nov 6, 1915,Wanganui Chronicle,7</i>)

### Ferns
 
 <i>n.</i> NZ troops

>  Our troops were actually known as 'the Ferns'. Even at Gallipoli, they weren't known as the Kiwis, but the Ferns. There is a military tradition for the fern. <i>(Oct 3, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> short for Silver Ferns q.v.

>  [Hdg] Fantastic ferns set for world thriller. <i>(Feb 28, 1999,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

### ferntights
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) mustering boots with bush sprigs.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They wore what were known as ferntights, boots which, instead of the ordinary hobnails, has bush sprigs. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,245</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> A bushman always wore a special type of boot known as `ferntights', named because the upper part of the boot was sewn inside the lower leather, preventing sharp stakes from penetrating the seam and tearing the stitching. Round-headed hobnails were replaced with with square-headed `bushman's sprigs'. ... It was essential ... to have a good firm footing and `bushman's sprigs' helped to prevent us losing our foothold on a slippery bank. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,61</i>)

### ferny
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Calm Rotoru[u-acute]a's ferny strand./ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/125</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The going in most parts was fairly difficult, the valley floor, as in nearly all of the [Southland] National Park, being very ferny, with thick undergrowth. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,125</i>)

### ferrule
 
 <i>n.</i>

### fert
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) fertiliser. [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

### fertiliser history
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the record of fertiliser application to a particular area or property.

>  .. Excellent fertiliser history. <i>(Jun 1995,NZ Dairy Exporter,121</i>)

>  Well subdivided, very well fenced and raced. Good fertiliser history. <i>(Nov 11, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

>  .. sundry sheds and dog motels, all in good order. Good fertiliser history. <i>(May 12, 1993,NZ Farmer,33</i>)

>  Grass everywhere, excellent fertiliser history ... <i>(Nov 11, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

>  Excellent fertiliser history with top stock performance. <i>(May 5, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,21</i>)

>  Consistent fertiliser history, 36 bale [sic] rotoflow cow shed. <i>(Jun 23, 2001,Dominion,39</i>)

>  This property has it all. Flats, rolling views, good fertiliser history, deer fences, deer complex. <i>(Nov 23, 2001,Manawatu Evening Standard & Property Guide,56</i>)

>  Consistent fertiliser history with new or near-new fencing. <i>(Dec 7, 2001,Manawatu Evening Standard & Property Guide,40</i>)

### fertiliser sick
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of soils] treated with an unbalanced mix of fertilisers.

>  Liming corrects 'fertiliser sick' soils. <i>(Apr 29, 1932,NZ Dairy Exporter,19</i>)

### fertility harrow/s
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a light-weight type of harrow used for spreading manure.

>  The Harrow that is proving ideal for use on 95 per cent. of our pastures is the Fertility Harrow. The light-weight job has short knife-edged spring steel teeth which give a clean surface .. For this work, the fertility harrow has probably the most efficient manure spreading device ever built .. The Fertility harrows are sufficiently light in draught ... <i>(Jul 14, 1940,NZ Journal of Agriculture,12</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [grass](../G#grass))

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced grazing area, predominantly growing fescue.

### Festival of Romance
 
 <i>n.</i> an annual Christchurch Festival

### fettler
 
 <i>n.</i> a man who cleans up castings in the railways workshops [(railways) ca1977 Nov 11 (Woburn) (Wayne Reed)]

### few bricks short of a load, a
 
 <i>phr.</i> not very bright

>  <b>CRANNA</b> Sounds like this dame's a few bricks short of a load, mate. <i>(1989,Visitors,105</i>)

### few de joy
 
 <i>n.</i> early Imperial soldiers' alteration of feu de joie [(hist.)]

>  <b>GODLEY</b> A Royal Salute ... 21 guns ... then a flourish of trumpets, and a discharge of small arms or as the soldiers here write it and call it a `few de Joy' on the flat. <i>(May 7, 1850,Letters,48,1951 ed.</i>)

### few in, a
 
 <i>phr.</i> (informal) elliptical for a few beers, an understatement for `many'; esp. .bd;have, get a (good) few (in)

>  Apart from a night at home after a few in.. Slim Withers had never been what a professional man would call really a "crook" <i>(Aug 15, 1923,Aussie (NZ Section),Vol / Page III</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> When he's got a few in he goes berko. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,Vol  /  Page 163</i>)

>  The sub: `But didn't `Jimmie' shape up to Boys?' Witness: `No, he couldn't he had a few in.' Jimmie grinned. The sub: `Did you have a few in, too?' Witness: `Oh, no.' [Re a street brawl over grog]. <i>(Apr 22, 1911,NZ Truth,304/6/2</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... the pair of us had a good few in. <i>(1946,That Summer,63</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1964,Collected Stories,299</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Pearson Glossary]

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> `a few beers': `I met Joe and we had a few beers' means probably that they drank solidly from Friday night to Sunday. few: with a few in, after several drinks. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,182</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> The landlady ...said he had obviously been drinking. That was the way he behaved when he was half-cut. She insisted he was no trouble. ... Sometimes he did knock a few back. <i>(1984,Murder in Vain,83</i>)

### few quiet ones
 
 <i>phr.</i> a few beers, alcoholic drinks

 <i>n.</i>

### fibro
 
 <i>n.</i> fibrolite [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Two streets away they found Edgar, an elderly man, sitting in a deckchair in his Victor Meldrew pyjamas, on the front lawn of his fibro bungalow. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,33</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> shortened form of fibrolite (See [fibrolite](../F#fibrolite))

>  Our bach is built of the classic galvanised iron and fibro. <i>(Jan 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

### fibrolite
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim Cameron says there is no entry in DNZE for <i>fibro</i>, "I believe on the ground that the word was used in Australia but not NZ". But see Eldred-Grigg's cites re 1940s Christchurch.]

>  <b>GEE</b> They held a fibrolite letterbox lid to shelter Sidgy's face. <i>(1992,Going West,105</i>)

>  At No 14, a scruffy hedge hides the fibrolite bach and its legendary army hut. <i>(Nov 18, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  Today there's a scattering of weatherboard-and-Fibrolite homes, a historic green-roofed church, a marae, and not much else. <i>(May 2002,NZ Geographic,577/86</i>)

>  One [photo] is of a very Kiwi-looking girl and boy (clothes, hairstyles) in front of a fibrolite bach. <i>(Jun 26, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  My Piha is focussed on a humble little one-room fibrolite bach with a longdrop in the bush at the back. <i>(Feb 1999,North & South,130</i>)

>  Morrison ushers us into primly-mowed backyards, time-worn public bars, fibrolite baches and silver service tea-rooms - the land that cappucino culture forgot. <i>(Feb 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  Gordon McLauchlan has called it the only fibrolite literary memorial in the world: the humble book-crammed bach.. where writer Frank Sargeson held court for 50 years. <i>(Dec 12, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

>  <b>GEE</b> Just beyond ... are the huge fibrolite storage sheds of the Apple and Pear Marketing Board. <i>(1972,In My Father's Den,75</i>)

>  <b>HUTCHINGS</b> Bill Schmidt, the dag crusher ... remained faithful to his fibrolite bach, and ... one suspects, true to Elvis Presley. <i>(1987,Tall Half-Backs,86</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> It was pretty awful, a fibrolite house, very small, cramped and grubby. <i>(1987,Oracles and Miracles,218</i>)

>  <b>LAY</b> The second ... related to several streets of look-alike fibrolite and tile houses which were set out in a grid a mile or so from the barracks. <i>(1988,Fools on the Hill, The,85</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> ... the toitoi scratched against the fibrolite wall of the bach. <i>(1988,Rainshadow,102</i>)

>  In the space between her pink house and fibrolite garage, she has put up canvas awnings and plastic roofing, converting it into a large conservatory. <i>(Sep 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  One [photo] is of a very Kiwi-looking girl and boy (clothes, hairstyles) in front of a fibrolite bach. <i>(Jun 26, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> 'It's these fibro walls,' Jaz said. 'They're too thin.' <i>Ibid.</i> 249 So in the end it was quite a big party, there must of been about thirty people, all jammed into our little fibro bach. <i>(1987,Oracles and Miracles,241</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  You must get a permit from the Building Controller to use Fibrolite because there is no iron, and a permit from the Iron Controller not to use anything because there is no Fibrolite. <i>(Nov 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,40</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> The lushnes of so much parkland looked incongruous by contrast with the bareness of the half-hidden houses, houses clad with drab weatherboard or thin sheets of fibrolite. <i>(1995,Mum,62</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> That tiny fibrolite dwelling, set in the subsistence garden that Frank cultivated like a small farm, became a literary kingdom. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,152</i>)

>  He loved our fibrolite, our vertical lawn, our shaky bridge. <i>(Apr 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> A terrible crackling roar and the tap ... went through the wall in a shower of flying fibrolite. <i>(1981,Fish Factory,169</i>)

>  That [the bach] strapped down a bit of paradise with corrugated iron and lead-head nails, fibrolite and flat-heads; that it achieved its redemption through ugliness. <i>(Apr 26, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

>  Forty sheets of grey fibrolite, / Twenty-five lengths of corrugated iron <i>(Feb 10, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

### fibrous plaster
 
 <i>n.</i> building material consisting of plaster impregnated with fibres of e.g. hemp [Jim writes : probably international but not in NSOD, Collins or Chambers. In Macquarie 1981.]

### fiddle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYSON</b> Boiling down works had also been started at Green Island. Merinos were boiled down for their fat and pelts : and as the hind legs of merinos contained no fat they were given away. Jock Graham did a roaring trade in Dunedin by purchasing carrots in the Taieri and tying three of them to a hind leg of a sheep. This was known as a fiddle and he would hawk them through the streets and dispose of them for sixpence. <i>(1937,Thomas,153</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Car dealers have a collection of jargon they use on the yard, so if you are thinking of taking up selling cars as a career, there are a few terms you should know. ... If you would like a car with a radio, ask if it has a `harp' or a `fiddle.' <i>(Jan 22, 1986,NZ Herald,2/1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>v.</i> to crosscut logs, or use a crosscut saw (on logs) singlehandedly. Cf fiddler; fiddling saw.

>  <b>REED</b> [of crosscutters] ... his mate `fiddled' the remaining wood - that is, sawed it single-handed. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,163</i>) [NOTES: >[?ca1900]]

>  FIDDLE To crosscut logs using a singlehanded saw; syn. BOB. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### fiddleback
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASSEY</b> Fiddleback A rippled grain pattern which catches the light and gives a piece of smooth timber the appearance of being corrugated. <i>(1987,Woodturning in New Zealand,93</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [Is this purely Aust??]

>  fiddleback, fiddler beetle variety of beetle, with marks on wings resembling openings of a fiddle. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),615</i>)

### fiddlehead
 
 <i>n.</i> (a fish)

>  On March 2nd, a fish called `Fiddle Head', was caught outside the Heads. It measured 6 ft. long by 4 ft. broad, and weighed 2 cwt. <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/488</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]



 
 <i>n.</i> the curled frond-tip resembling the scroll of a violin.

>  <b>PICKERING</b> [caption] Fiddlehead, <i>Dicksonia squarrosa. </i> <i>(1988,Hills, The,39</i>)

>  More palatable are the young fiddleheads of many species, especially the hen and chickens fern, kiokio (<i>Blechnum novae-zealandiae</i>), gully fern (<i>Pneumatopteris pennigera</i>, shining spleenwort (<i>Asplenium oblongifolium</i>) and the shield ferns (<i>Polystichum richardii</i> and <i>P/vestitum</i>). <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/77</i>)

>  Other vegetables already appearing on restaurant plates are lightly steamed fiddle-heads, or young green shoots of the hen and chickens fern (Asplenium bulbiferum) and the native spinach. <i>(Apr 30, 2006,Dominion Post,E12</i>)

### fiddler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Trygonorhina fasciata</i> ... The Fiddler. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,127</i>)

>  Fiddler, <i>Trygonorrhina fasciata. </i> <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/101, fig. 4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1940 at least: T. Dennehey On the West Coast, a man who marks where the log is to be cut as it comes from the bush, before it goes to the breaker-down.]

>  <b>MALFROY</b> The fiddler should be able to sharpen his own saw, and generally use his brains in the selection of lengths for current orders. <i>Ibid.</i> 31 It is the usual practice in this country to send logs to the mill in their full length, and have the cross cutting into merchantable length done at the mill skids. This is commonly called `fiddling', and a good fiddler can save a lot of waste ... <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,28</i>)

>  So I hopped over his side of the bench and called Bob Stewart the fiddler to come down and tail out. Andy stuffed up the back to do the fiddling. ...[p. 212] ...Peter York was fiddling (that's the job of cutting the logs into lengths so they could effectively be broken down). <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,210</i>)

### fiddley
 
 <i>n.</i> A one-pound note [rhyming on fiddly-did]. [ca1953 Wellington and elsewhere in NZ: T. Benseman. rhyming slang]

>  <b>TURNER</b> Baker lists a few examples of rhyming slang from Australia, among them ... fiddley did for quid, whence fiddley profit', ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,119</i>)

>  fiddley ... n. (Sl.) pound note [From rhyme on quid]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

### fiddling
 
 <i>n.</i> [(sawmilling)]

>  <b>MALFROY</b> OPERATION COSTS. ... MANUFACTURE :- 1. Sawmill - (a.) Fiddling or crosscutting logs at mill ... <i>Ibid.</i> 31 [sawmillers'] It is the usual practice in this country to send logs to the mill in their full length, and have the cross cutting into merchantable length done at the mill skids. This is commonly called `fiddling', and a good fiddler can save a lot of waste ... <i>Ibid.</i> 38 Fiddling .. The operation of crosscutting by one man, usually done at the mill-skids. <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,8</i>)

### fiddling saw
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  When the loco arrives at the bull skids, the logs are hauled off by two winches, cut to the required length by the fiddling saw, then kicked to the Pacific bench which does the "breaking down". <i>(Jul 25, 1928,NZ Free Lance,7</i>)

### fiddly-did
 
 <i>n.</i> a quid [rhyming slang]

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Remember asking a joker ... if he'd shove a fiddly-did on the double for me. <i>(1964,Watersiders,133</i>)

### Fiducia
 
 <i>n.</i> a tram with only front and rear doors (the last generation of Wellington trams)

>  In Wellington, 24 Fiducia Class (Trust, Confidence, Reliance and Assurance) trams were built to cope with the crowds at the Wellington Centennial Exhibition, 1940, a design most popular with commuters till trams ceased to run. <i>(Oct 2003,NZ Memories,No. 44/40</i>)

### field day
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an on-farm meeting or seminar demonstrating new or reliable methods or technologies.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Unfortunately I had only just missed a field day at Paparatu ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,32</i>)

### Field Hut
 
 <i>n.</i>

### field mouse
 
 <i>n.</i> a house mouse when found out of doors (not a vole as in Britain).

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The field mouse of England is a vole, not a true mouse, but here the common house mouse is called a field mouse when it occurs out of doors. So also any rat occurring in streams is called a water rat but the English water rat is a vole, not a true rat. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>) [NOTES: (Wall NZ Speech)]

### field tile
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  field tile n. N.Z. an earthenware drain used in farm drainage. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,412</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 182]]

### Field's Express
 
 <i>n.</i> A local train running between Wellington and Palmerston North, leaving Wellington about 6 p.m. Also Field's. [FIELD, William Hughes MHR Otaki 1900-1911. Born Wanganui 1861, died 1944. Have not yet confirmed Field's Express which has disappeared from the railway literature nor that this is the right Field. Have not yet located an obituary.]

>  Mr. Field (Otaki) asked the Minister of Railways, Whether the practice of taking off at Paekakariki the only comfortable first-class carriage attached to the train known as "Field's express" leaving Thorndon at 5.55 p.m. .. may be discontinued. <i>(1924,NZ Parliamentary Debates,CCV/1217</i>)

>  With his father, a civil engineer, he [William Hughes Field] made the original exploration of "Field's Track" between Wanganui and the Karioi plains. <i>(Dec 14, 1944,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Long after those of us who knew him personally have passed away, [his name] will be known to a much wider section of the public - to travellers between Palmerston North and the Capital city -because of "Field's Express." <i>Ibid 87 </i> I can recall the fight he put up for the train we now refer to as "Field's Express." <i>(1945,NZ Parliamentary Debates,CCLXVIII/86</i>)

>  To Mr. Field was due an improvement of the railway service between Palmerston North and Wellington, "Field's Express" being named after him. <i>(Dec 14, 1944,Manawatu Evening Standard,4</i>)

>  <b>HOY</b> The former service comprises a Friday night train to Palmerston North, stopping at most stations including those within the suburban area. The latter is a morning and evening return train from Wellington on Sunday. These three trains are all that is left of a twice-daily service which served the district from 1923 to 1958, and which was known as the Field's Express after the Member of Parliament for Otaki (Mr. Field) who campaigned for their introduction. <i>(1972,West of the Tararuas,107</i>)

>  <b>HOY</b> W.H. Field, M.P. Field's Express may have been named for him; his brother who served before him as M.P. for Otaki, referred to him as Willie. <i>(1978,Hutt County Council Centenary,206</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> They travelled by Field's Express (named after W.H. Field) to Otaki and then hired a truck to take them to Otaki Forks, where they camped for the night. <i>(1994,Tararua : the Story of a Mountain Range,133</i>)

### Fieldays
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a national agricultural exhibition and festival held annually in early winter at Mystery Creek, Waikato since 1974.

>  There were only six recipients of the NZ Farmer Inventions Award at last month's Fieldays. <i>(Jul 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

>  THE FIELDAYS might seem an odd choice of venue for sales staff from an ivestment fund, but on reflection it is appropriate. <i>(Jun 18, 2006,NZ Catholic,D5</i>)

>  Fieldays, held yearly at Mystery Creek near Hamilton, is New Zealand's premier agribusiness event, showcasing the latest in agricultural products and services and bringing buyers and sellers together to create an agricultural hypermarket. <i>(Jun 18, 2004,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

>  Home of the National Horticultural Fieldays <i>(Jun 12, 2001,Ephemera,Post Office Slogan (Waikato MC)</i>) [NOTES: also seen in television advertising for this event]

>  The unashamed purpose for being at Mystery Creek is the Hard Sell - this year over 500 companies exhibited at the Fieldays. An estimated $20 million is spent in the province on food and accommodation during Fieldays week. <i>(Jul 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  Sales at this year's Mystery Creek Fieldays topped a record $64 million, organisers say. Fieldays general manager Barry Quayle said the figure was $16.1 million or 33.5% higher than the previous year. <i>(Oct 5, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,14</i>)

>  I would like to congratulate Fieldays for choosing Landcare as the theme for 2002. <i>(Jun 2002,Taranaki Daily News,6</i>)

>  One of the earlybirds.. has been coming to the Fieldays since 1986... At the Fieldays, interest has been steady from farmers. <i>(Jun 13, 2003,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  [Hdg] It's National Agricultural Fieldays time again, and organisers are confident of another bmuper year... Last year a boom in the diary industry meant farmers came to town to spend and there were record sales at the Fieldays... Fieldays is also attracting interest from overseas. <i>(Jun 14, 2002,Dominion,9</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [Cf forty-acre farmer.]

 <i>n.</i> [Cf forty-acre farmer.]

Free grants to each settler, consisting of a village allotment of a quarter-acre and a suburban allotment of twenty acres. (ii.) Fifty-acre sections at five shillings per acre. <i>(1940,Rakiura,243</i>)

### fifty-fifty sharemilker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer who owns a dairy herd and shares equal profits from herd production with a landowner.

>  From workers to managers and right through to 50/50, this is a very important time to get the relationship with the owner off to a good start. [50% sharemilker] <i>(Jun 6, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,16</i>)

### fifty-fifty sharemilking
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the equal sharing of profits between a herd owner and a landowner. (See [half share-milking](../H#half-share-milking))

>  <b>GORDON</b> .. in time each man was able to buy his herd and go into a 50/50 sharemilking agreement. "if they're on a good farm, with good facilities, 50/50 sharemilking in big herds is inequitable to the owner. It's sad, but there are less 50/50 agreements around now ..." <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,155</i>)

>  50/50 sharemilking opportunity commencing 1 June 2001 .. 50/50 sharemilking opportunity will become available June 1, 2001 as our current sharemilkers have bought their first farm. <i>(Dec 16, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,39</i>)

### Fifty-one
 
 <i>n.</i> 1951, alluding to the Waterfront Dispute

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Things haven't been the same [on the waterfront] since, 'Fifty- one, you know. <i>(1964,Watersiders,24</i>)

### Fifty-one bust-up
 
 <i>phr.</i> the 1951 waterside dispute

>  <b>DAVIS</b> `... I came down to the waterside. The old union. Never been off it since, except for the six months during the 'Fifty-one bust up.' <i>(1964,Watersiders,108</i>)

### Fifty-oner
 
 <i>n.</i> a) a member of the 1951 Watersiders' Union strike b) a member of the reconstituted watersiders' union in 1951; a new union member (not a member of original union which was locked out). [Note two opposing definitions need sorting out]

>  Jock Barnes was a trade unionist and a fifty-oner to the end. <i>(Jun 1, 2000,TV3,News</i>)

>  A trade union farewell for 1951 watersiders' leader Jock Barnes, who died last week will be held in Auckland's Trades Hall... It has been organised by "51ers" and prominent trade unionists Bill Andersen and Frank Barnard. <i>(Jun 4, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A4</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Even Shirty Williams the 'Fifty-oner forgot his ulcers and managed a smile. <i>Ibid.</i> 70 `It wasn't a strike [in 1951 in the waterfront] then?' I asked. `No. She was a lock-out, Henry. We had a disagreement with the bosses and told 'em only work a forty-hour week till she was settled. The bosses said: `Right ho, go on penalty the whole perishing lot or you - and stay on penalty.' `Then the 'Fifty-oners moved in and took our jobs. Rotten cow.' <i>(1964,Watersiders,47</i>)

### fig
 
 <i>n.</i> an old word for (plug of) tobacco, surviving in prison use. Common in early days among traders, sailors and later on stations, etc., surviving as prison usage in Mount Crawford, Wellington, to at least the 1940s: replaced generally by plug. sc. Of tobacco. .ho:a. ?????

>  <b>YATE</b> (Griffiths Collection) Fig of tobacco <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),127</i>)

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> The gude-man gave us a basket of fine Potatoes for a few figs of Tobacco, ... <i>(Apr 21, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 21 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 122]

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> Thus, if you give a fig of tobacco, you may be sure they will ask for a pipe; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 57 et passim ... a fig or two of the `weed.' <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,43,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> ... I reward him for his hospitality with a few figs of tobacco, and pursue my way northward. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,9</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Even sulky Te Hira would not grudge us that, in return, of course, for a fig of tobacco. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk3/4/165</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The payment ... consisted of ... 100 figs of tobacco, four greenstone pendants, ... two `roundabouts' (loose brightly coloured blouses for the ladies), and one axe. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,33</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> Panakareao consented to sell on receipt of 80 blankets, 30 hoes, 30 iron pots, 30 pairs scissors, 10 shark hooks, 12 spades, 100 pipes, 30 adzes, 40 axes, 40 plane irons, 2,000 fish-hooks, 50 combs, 600 figs tobacco. <i>(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,42</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> surviving in prison use, a (packet of) prison tobacco [ca1941-1945 [Riverside Community] Still used Mt Crawford Prison, Wellington at least.]

>  <b>JUSTIN</b> `A fig?' `A packet of boob - prison, to you - tobacco. Haven't you learnt that yet?' <i>(1973,Prisoner,29</i>)

>  <b>BERRY</b> `I got six figs for that load of rubbish' Fig was a curious word, an Americanism that had some how filtered into N.Z [sic] penal institutions and survived. It wasn't slang in the sense that screw and boob were. There was no other word for the cellophane packet that contained around fifty grammes of offcuts and substandard tobacco supplied by the companies. Nobody spoke of a packet of tobacco - it was always a fig. <i>(1980,First Offender,152</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> fig an ounce of tobacco <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,96</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Fig. (n) Packet of prison tobacco. A US tobacco-grower's term in the 1830s. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,248</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> native fig, New Zealand fig. <i>Schefflera digitata. </i> [907 CKI, 1908 CWK, 1915 CKB (Andersen)]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) [Schefflera digitata] ????? native fig <i>Schefflera digitata </i> <i>(1915,AJHR,C-6</i>) [NOTES: Notes on the Plant Covering of Kennedy's Bush ... Canterbury]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Notes on the Plant Covering of Kennedy's Bush ... Canterbury <i>(1907,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Kapiti Island (And)]

>  (And) <i>(1908,AJHR,C-14</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Waipoua Kauri Forest]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> (And) <i>Mesembryanthemum australe</i> fig-marigold <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The</i>)

### fight
 
 <i>n.</i> a war party; early missionary English translation of Maori taua `war party' (frequent in early MSS, letter and journals). [(hist.)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> ...the natives came running, and exclaimed, Ka puta te taua, that is, the fight is in sight, or those for that purpose. <i>Ibid.</i> 205 Went to Kororarika to see the Chiefs about the fight. ... [ed fn] `The fight' is a translation of taua much used by the missionaries and early settlers. Both the Maori word and the translation mean a `war party' and are used whether the `fight' is a mere local skirmish or a full-scale tribal war. <i>(Aug 23, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,70,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: check also entry for 1827 Aug 23]

>  [=taua[????]] this day we was put in suspense at dusk ... five Boats came into the heads ... we first took them for the fight coming from Cloudy Bay but they proved to be boats from Otago ... <i>(Jan 9, 1838,Piraki Log,64</i>)

>  <b>WILY & MAUNSELL</b> ... and the tribe will often deem it to be due to his rank to bring `a fight' as they call it, against him, and as a demonstration of regard, strip him of all the property they may find about him. <i>(Nov 2, 1839,Robert Maunsell,61,1938 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Since we left, the fight, as it is called, has passed this way but without any battle taking place. <i>(1839,Journal,2/129</i>)

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> The fight we had been in quest of had made their appearance at nine that morning and had been at long balls with the Pa all day and were just then retired. <i>(1839,Letters & Journals,1/145</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Great preparations are making [at Taupo] for the `fight' from Paripari, which is expected here daily; ... and the natives are busily engaged in preparing fire-arms and other weapons. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/111</i>)

### figure-eight
 
 <i>n.</i> [(obs prison slang)]

>  <b>HYDE</b> Twenty-one days on bread and water in the Dummy. Seven days in `figure eights'. ... The `figure-eight' is a mild version of the French Foreign Legion's beloved torture, le crapaud. For a period of hours each day, ranging from two to four, the prisoner's arms are doubly handcuffed across the small of his back, wrists and elbows forced together. No leglocks are used. He can sit, stand, or lie as he pleases. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,65</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### filefish
 
 <i>n.</i> leatherjacket

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Fam. Plectognathi. - The <i>Pakirikiri</i>, (balistes,) file fish, is distinguished by two strong spines at the commencement of the dorsal fin, in shape like the letter V, both fronting the head, and standing out from the fin, these are elevated at pleasure, some fish have only one. The Pakirikiri is abundant in the Bay of Islands. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,624</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> File Fish Name given in New Zealand to the <i>Monacanthus rudis</i>, Richards, family <i>Sclerodermi</i>; in New South Wales to species of the genus <i>Balistes</i>. The first of the spines of the dorsal fin is roughened in front like a file. <i>Balistes maculatus</i> is the `Spotted File-fish' of Sydney. It is closely allied to the genus <i>Monacanthus</i>, called <i>Leather-jacket</i> (q.v.), which is much more numerously represented in Australasia. <i>(1898,Austral English,145</i>)

>  <i>Cantherines scaber</i> (Forster). Leather-jacket (trigger-fish, file-fish). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/418</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> ROUGH LEATHER JACKET, TRIGGER-FISH or FILE-FISH (KOKIRI) <i>Parika scaber</i> (Bloch and Schneider) ... This interesting fish has a number of other vernacular names, such as File-fish, and all with a bearing on its construction. The name Leather-Jacket is due to the fancied resemblance to a North American lumber jacket, not unlike the dark brown coloured jumpers used by hikers. The word rough is attached to the name Leather Jacket by fishermen, because the scales are furnished with sharp points which make the skin so rough that one's fingers cannot be rubbed freely in a direction from tail to head without losing part of one's skin, hence the name File-fish. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,374,2nd ed. ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> ROUGH LEATHERJACKET ... <i>Other names: Novodon convexirostris</i>; triggerfish and filefish are used, but should rightly be given to a family of tropical fishes of similar appearance to our leatherjackets; kokiri (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,293</i>)

>  <i>file fish n. </i>New Zealand fish, like the leather-jacket. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),615</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> LEATHERJACKET [<i>Navodon scaber</i>] ... The names filefish and triggerfish are sometimes used, but more properly belong to a related group of tropical fishes. The Maori name kokiri is still in use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,24</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> LEATHERJACKET (a) Scientific name: <i>Navodon scaber</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Creamfish (fillets); Leatherjacket. (c) Other common names: Triggerfish; Filefish. (d) Principal localities where taken: Auckland, Tauranga, Tasman Bay. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,33</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> FILE-FISH: <i>see Leatherjacket </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,100</i>)

>  [species name] leatherjacket .. [scientific name] <i>Navodon scaber</i> .. [other name(s)] cream fish, filefish, kokiri, triggerfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al</b> [index to common names 283-293] filefish 2314, 2325, 2333 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### fill
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>McARA</b> Fill or filling: The material, usually soft rock or clay from near the surface, which was tipped down passes into the various filled stopes, to fill the space from which the ore had been removed and which otherwise might collapse if left unsupported. Some sand filling (tailings from the battery) was used successfully in the Grand Junction Mine. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),321</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  fill: Good shearers `fill' their shears. This is important because the number of blows can be considerably reduced, but it has to be done without `bogging' which makes a second blow necessary. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/24</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### fillet
 
 <i>n.</i> a thin piece of wood used to separate timber when stacked. =sticker, =strip. [(forestry and timber-merchants')]

>  <b>JEWELL</b> Fillets; Sometimes called a `sticker' or `strip'. Is a strip of timber or board 1"x 1" or 2"x 1" placed between layers of boards in a pile and at right angles to the boards for the purpose of separating them and facilitating the circulation of air. <i>(1948,Accounting in the Timber Industry,128</i>)

>  FILLET 1. n. A small dimension piece of timber placed at right angles to the boards in a pile of timber stacked for drying. syn. STICKER, STRIP. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>MASSEY</b> Fillet A wood spacer used to separate timber whilst air-drying. <i>(1987,Woodturning in New Zealand,93</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to stack timber for drying.

>  <b>LYON</b> I also arranged the timber should be filletted or stacked with lathes between and left to dry, ... <i>(1952,Faring South,196</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [NZism]

### filmy fern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A few, like species of the filmy fern <i>Trichomanes</i>, consist of branching filaments and more closely resemble a fuzz of green algae than a fern gametophyte. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/69</i>)

>  Only the alpine shield fern (<i>Polystichum cystostegia</i>), bladder fern (<i>Cystopteris tasmanica</i>), mountain kiokio (<i>Blechnum montanum</i>), thousand-leaved fern (<i>Hypolepis millefolium</i>), finger ferns (<i>Grammitis poeppigiana</i> and <i>G. givenii</i>), one filmy fern (<i>Hymenophyllum villosum</i>) and two clubmosses (<i>Huperzia australiana</i> and <i>Lycopodium fastigiatum</i>) are found primarily in this habitat <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/78</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> ... it is time to lower our gaze and begin to make acquaintance with the <i>Hymenophyllaceae</i> or filmy ferns <i>(1882,Out in the Open,60</i>)

>  Filmy ferns look almost too fragile to be outside and kidney ferns crown into the crooks of branches. <i>(Jan 3, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

### fin
 
 <i>n.</i> (Of a sailing scow) a small centreboard [[? fin-class yachts: or Finn]. -->1939]



 
 <i>n.</i> [Cf finner.] (See [whale](../W#whale))

>  With the true whales (Balaenoidea)[sic] it has the following characters in common: - Baleen elongate and of fine quality; chest and belly smooth; tympanic bones rhombic; cervical vertebrae anchylosed; mandibular ramus without a coronoid process. To the finners (or finbacks) [fn. Whalers often speak of right whales as finners or fin-fish, from their yielding baleen or `fin', as they term it. [HWO note: is he implying that right whales are true whales but are also (?mistakenly) called finners by whalers: I don't think so.]] it had the following points of resemblance:- Body elongate and slender, the proportion of head being less than one fourth [253] of the length; has a dorsal fin; pectoral fin lanceolate, moderate, and with four fingers; scapula elongate, with coracoid and acromion processes. Its peculiar characters are the small number of vertebrae, 44; the short mandibles and remarkably broad straight ribs with feeble vertebral articulations. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/252</i>) [NOTES: (Hector Whales)]



 
 <i>v.</i> Of a whale, to die.



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> baleen

>  <i>(1874,TrNZI,7/252</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [Borrowed from Australia] financial and holding (description of the state of being well supplied with cash) <i>(1941,NZ Slang,61</i>)

>  financial adj. and adv. possessing cash. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),8</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To have a supply of money to hand. .in 4;Richie's shout, boys. Richie's financial, he had a good win today.' <i>(1962,Australian & NZ English [TS],343</i>)

>  financial ... 3. Austral. & N.Z. inf. having money; in funds. 4. Austral. & N.Z. (of a club member) fully paid-up. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,415</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 280; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 183]]

### finback
 
 <i>n.</i> [includes fin whale. cf finner] (See [finner](../F#finner))

>  The agreement follows, on the whole, familiar lines, and under it it will not be lawful to take .. fin whales of less than 55ft <i>(Jun 10, 1937,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the leviathans, who sport in shoals around these shores, the para paraua [a's-acute], or <i>sperm-whale</i> (Physeter macrocephalus); the tohora, or <i>right-whale</i> (Balaena bysticoetus); the mungu nui, or <i>black physeter</i>; the<i> sun-fish</i> (Diodonmola); <i>fin-back</i> (Balaena physalis); the <i>musculus</i>, or large-lipped whale; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/323</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called para[as-acute] <i> paraua </i>[a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale, <i> mungu nui </i> (black physeter); right whale, <i> tohora </i> (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis); sun-fish (diodon mola); large-lipped whale (balaena mysticetus); large-lipped whale (balaena musculus); hump-back (balaena gibbosa); shark, <i> mango </i>(squalus); hammer-head (zygaena); pilots, (scomber); <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202 fn</i>)

>  This was an entire and perfectly fresh finback whale, which had apparently been cast upon the beach by the violence of the gale which we had experienced a few days previously. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 248]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Of <i> Whales </i>, there are the <i> Sperm (Physeter Macrocephalus)</i>, the <i> Humpback (Balaena gibbosa), </i>the <i> Fin-back (Balaena physalus)</i>, the <i> Pike-headed (Balaena Boops)</i>, the <i> Large-lipped (Balaenopterus musculus)</i>, and the <i> Black </i> or <i> Right whale (Balaena Antipodum).</i> <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,153</i>)

>  Finners (or Fin-backs) [fn] Whalers often speak of right whales as finners or fin-fish, from their yielding baleen or `fin' as they term it. <i>(1874,TrNZI,7/252</i>) [NOTES: Hector `NZ Whales']

>  The whale and its calf were `finbacks' of no commercial value. <i>(1879,TrNZI,34</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy)]

>  <b>GREY</b> The season of 1925, just ended, had netted him some fifty-odd whales, mostly finbacks. <i>(1926,Angler's Eldorado,27</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> [glossary] <i> Finbacks </i> - Another name for finners or whales of the genus <i> Balaenoptera </i>. The finbacks are distinguishable by a small triangular dorsal fin. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,227</i>)

>  [On the voyage out] There was a cry of `a fish, a fish' how very odd thought I but ... there was a fish ... this creature which was a Finner and consequently not near the size of a Whale proper. <i>(Jul 2, 1837,Journal of Ensign Best,89,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [?from finbacks] ... shingles, N.Z. [per] 1000, 6s. to 8s. ... whalebone [per] ton, 135l. to 140l. - finners, 40l. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,180</i>)

>  RORQUALUS (Sp.,) Trigger-fin, Razor-back, Sulphur- bottom (to be distinguished from the Finner, which is properly the <i> B. Marginata </i>.) <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/25</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  The blue whale (<i> Balaenoptera musculus </i>) is the first of the four species or rorquals, or finners, to be mentioned. It is the largest species of whale, and, indeed, of all animals, living and extinct. ... The common finner (<i> Balaenoptera physalis </i>) may be thus defined: Dorsal fin triangular, low, at commencement of last fourth of body; ... <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/133</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  <b>MONRO</b> The animal [sc. finback whale] has been named by Cuvier the <i> Rorqualus Australis,</i> or in English, the Rorqual of the Southern Seas. It is very little known to zoologists; and it is only within a few years that its existence has been ascertained by the scientific. The Rorqual of the Northern Seas, it will be recollected, is the largest creature in existence, attaining sometimes to a length of 120 feet, with a circumference of 40. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson <i> Examiner </i> July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 248]

>  The blue whale (<i> Balaenoptera musculus </i> ) is the first of the four species or rorquals, or finners, to be mentioned. It is the largest species of whale, and, indeed, of all animals, living and extinct. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/133</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  <b>GRADY</b> <i> Rorqual species </i> - A furrowed whale with rows of long corrugations or folds in the skin of its throat and chest. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,228</i>) [NOTES: [glossary]]

### finback (whale)
 
 <i>n.</i> finback whale, finfish. [finback whale, aka finner;]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ?finner; ?finback

### find
 
 <i>v.</i> of a pig-dog, to find (wild pigs). Cf finder.

>  (Interview Murphy/Crump) Murphy [a pighunter]. Jimmy at the present time is the pick of the pack as a finder. The other three dogs were finding and baling ... <i>(Apr 19, 1962,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> keep (someone in food) (cf. all found)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Mr. Evans, who finds his men in everything <i>(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/109</i>)

### find (the) colour
 
 <i>phr.</i> to discover gold [goldmining] (See [also colour](../A#also-colour))

>  <b>WALKER</b> (Dunstan diggings) prospected [sic l.c.] about 2 miles down the bank of the river, opened a hole. Tuesday 9th bottomed the hole, found colour, ... <i>(Jan 8, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> I saw the bushmen whispering together, and examining(?) the rocks in some places, ... One of them had been a digger, and was pronouncing an opinion that this creek was very likely to prove a `home of gold' some day. ... At this time of the year the water is very shallow, ...; and they talk of having `found the colour' (of gold) in some places. <i>(Oct 1867,Station Life in NZ,186,1874 ed.</i>)

### find another gear
 
 <i>phr.</i> to find extra speed or strength.

>  <b>McGILL</b> she's/he's found another gear sporting c/p for horse or person accelerating, often when competition appears to be at full stretch. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,100</i>)

### finder
 
 <i>n.</i> a pig dog trained to find wild pigs. Cf holder.

>  Pig-dog types are many ... The writer's [J.P.Lewin's] considered classification is straight finders, straight bailers, straight holders and straight killers. ... Inside it we have finder-bailers; bailer-holders; finder-bailer-holders; bailer-holder-killers and holder-killers. <i>(Dec 1951,Here & Now,15</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Rip was developing into a good little finder ... He'd cast out ... in all directions until he found a pig. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,170</i>)

>  <b>MAST ERS</b> [sc. dog] ..., Fay, our finder, started running round whimpering and looking for a place to climb the bank. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,194</i>)

>  What about giving the lowdown on terms like bailing and finding ... Could you describe the things a good pig dog's required to do? ... There's two types of finders. There's the wind finder and the track finder. The wind finder will take a pig first ... If you've got a good wind finder with you and you're working the right way into the wind, if there's a pig lying anywhere up to 1000 or 1500 yards away he'll be away like a shot ... With a track finder the pig might be on the move and he'll leave a lot of scent on the undergrowth. The dog will immediately pick that up and he'll stick his nose on the ground ... He'll tail off after the pig and stop him some way, nip him in the leg or somewhere else. <i>(Apr 19, 1962,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>) [NOTES: (Interview Murphy/Crump)]

>  <b>BILLING</b> Sark threw [the dog] half a sausage. `That's my finder,' he said. `He'll find a pig anywhere ...' <i>(1971,Statutes,97</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> [The pighunters] had Sandy and Dark, both finders and bailers, to thank for that. <i>Ibid.</i> 156 Pigdogs are classified into three groups: finders, bailers, and holders. The name of each type indicates perfectly what they are expected to do. <i>(1982,Wild Pig,40</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> [sc. a pigdog] But a finder and bailer like Rusty was usually a much lighter animal and blessed with a few more brains. <i>(1984,Razorback,17</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> At the very outer edge of my hearing were sounds, vague and indistinct yet full of portent. That rousing battle cry, the rallying call of the finders, muted now, yet with its message crystal clear, had come wafting on the freshening breeze. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,145</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pig dog used for tracing wild pigs [either a track finder or an air finder.]

>  <b>AXBEY</b> Two of us were hunting near Waverley; my mate had a couple of holders and a finder, while I still had just the one. <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,10</i>)

>  <b>MCCONOCHIE</b> Our old dog was a first-class finder, but was never known to hold. <i>(1966,You'll Learn No Harm from the Hills,14</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> Some people have a finder and then they have bailers and they might even have dogs known as holders. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,122</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Murray's top finder - a huntaway named Rug - teamed very well with Peter's crack team of pig dogs. <i>(1993,Station Country I,20</i>)

>  <b>BEACH</b> Nig became an excellent finder and had the technique of being able to bail where he found, a quality not many dogs have. <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,13</i>)

### finder-bailer
 
 <i>n.</i> a pig-dog able to find and hold a wild pig until the pighunter arrives

>  Joshua Katua says a top finder-bailer could set you back $5000... Finder-bailer? That's the dog who can sniff out a boar, track it down, and bail it up, standing off and barking, until the hunter's holder dogs arrive, or the hunter. <i>(Feb 1998,Mana,No. 20: 36</i>)

### finding dog
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> A good finding dog was frequently no good at holding, as he would probably simply run round and round the pig ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,254,1954 ed.</i>)

### Findon haddock
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [haddock](../H#haddock))

>  <b>SENIOR</b> There is another fish somewhat resembling [the haddock] cured and sold in Dunedin as the Findon haddock. This is in reality a red cod, and is beautifully coloured. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,298</i>)

### fine
 
 <i>adj.</i> [goldmining]

>  The gold [from Waipori] is very fine, but very bright and pure. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and ReSources,20</i>)

>  The gold occurring in the quartz was very fine, and it was considered that the coarse alluvial gold found in the creek-beds could not have been shed from the lodes discovered. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/12</i>)

### fine combing
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep] with fleeces with a fine micron.

>  The champion ram of the section was the winning mature fine combing sheep shown by R C Todhunter. <i>(Nov 22, 1951,NZ Farmer,31</i>)

### fine gold
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> According to a test ... the Nelson gold averages 89 per cent fine gold, and 0,[sic]145 per cent fine silver. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),105fn</i>)

### fine up
 
 <i>v.</i> of the weather, to become fine; to improve. [ca1925 (OED 1888 rare, sailors?) -->R. Mason notes that she has heard this expression occasionally used since 1925. -->ca1954 Marlborough Sounds]



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to achieve finer fleecewool through breeding.

>  Romney breeders are confident the micron of the clip can be lowered by selecting within the breed. There is much talk about using Merino genetics to fine up the clip ... <i>(Sep 18, 1997,NZ Farmer,26</i>)

### fine wool
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep with particularly fine micron wool.

>  Up at Amberley, four year old fine wools made over $8. <i>(Apr 11, 1968,NZ Farmer,32</i>)

### fine-wool
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Fine-wool sheep, expecially Merinos, are much slower than crossbred sheep. Wrinkles, dense wool, trimmings, all have a bearing on shearing. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,3</i>)

### fine-wool sheep
 
 <i>n.</i> [(breeds)]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> In this country, Merinos, Corriedales and halfbreds are classed as the fine-wool sheep, and all the other breeds in the long-strong wool or mutton class. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,3</i>)

### fine-woolled
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... the average weight of a fine-wooled fleece may be correctly stated at from 3-1/2 lbs. to 4 lbs. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,123</i>)

>  FOR SALE BY PRIVATE CONTRACT 1,544 FINE WOOLLED SHEEP now depasturing on that portion of Banks Peninsula lying between Gibbies Station and Kaituna. <i>(Apr 5, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

### finfish
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [whale](../W#whale))

### finger fern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Finger ferns (species of <i>Grammitis</i>, hanging spleenwort, sickle spleenwort (<i>Asplenium polyodon</i>), lance fern (<i>Anarthropteris lanceolata</i>) and fork ferns (species of <i>Tmesipteris</i>) are also common on tree-fern trunks or hanging from the branches of forest trees. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/76</i>)

>  Asexual reproduction is not confined to the sporophyte generation. Gemmae are also formed on the gametophytes of several ferns (<i>Grammitis</i> species) or some filmy ferns (<i>Trichomanes</i> species). <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/69</i>)

>  Only the alpine shield fern (<i>Polystichum cystostegia</i>), bladder fern (<i>Cystopteris tasmanica</i>), mountain kiokio (<i>Blechnum montanum</i>), thousand-leaved fern (<i>Hypolepis millefolium</i>), finger ferns (<i>Grammitis poeppigiana</i> and <i>G. givenii</i>), one filmy fern (<i>Hymenophyllum villosum</i>) and two clubmosses (<i>Huperzia australiana</i> and <i>Lycopodium fastigiatum</i>) are found primarily in this habitat <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/78</i>)

### finger gully
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a very narrow gully leading into a larger gouged or eroded area.

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> They [gullies] start 4,000 feet up as narrow finger gullies at the base of the hollow talus screes. <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,75</i>)

### finger gullying
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the forming of finger gullies.

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> Shallow finger gullying occurs in clean sloping orchard land in the Bay of Islands ... <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,21</i>)

>  <b>CUMBERLAND</b> The overgrazed range .. the finger gullying of lower slopes .. these features are repeated in the Clutha valley ... <i>(1944,Soil Erosion in New Zealand,86</i>)

### finger joint
 
 <i>n.</i> [1989 Charles Ransom notes: Joint, finger - This term is enshrined in a NZ Standard but not found elsewhere -- not sure what the term would be abroad.]

>  Joint, finger- joint made between two pieces of timber by gluing their ends together with mated fingers of the same shape and size. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,66</i>)

### finger-waggy
 
 <i>adj.</i> admonishing

>  ...it ended up being a confusing and rather finger-waggy exercise. <i>(Oct 28, 2004,Dominion Post,B10</i>)

### fingerprinting
 
 <i>n.</i> an identification process

>  Scientists at HortResearch have come up with the way of identifying our apples through a dna testing, or "fingerprinting", process. <i>(Mar 27, 2004,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

### fingers and toes
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CROOKES</b> There are numerous families of slime fungus in New Zealand, but, unfortunately, they are not particularly easy to find, as they are rather small, and one must look for them in just the right kind of place, and choose moist, damp weather. Of course, they are not confined to the bush; one of them may be discovered in vegetable gardens, for it is parasitic on cabbages, causing the disease known as `fingers and toes.' <i>(1926,Plant Life in Maoriland,176</i>)

### fingerstones
 
 <i>n.</i> marbles

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Knucklebones (K-34) was played in both Europe and Polynesia, independently at first. In the nineteenth century distinct European [177] and Maori versions were known. More recently, the two types have merged, and today the Maori children, who are mainly responsible for perpetuating the game in New Zealand, include in their games movements borrowed from the European tradition. ... In most of the provinces the game was known as Knucklebones, and it is widely reported as having been played by New Zealand children of European origin until about 1900. Occasionally it was known as Chucks, Chuckstones, Chuckystones, Fingerstones, Hucklebones, Jacks, Jackstones, Knuckles, or Knucklestones. Children still played it here and there throughout the country in 1949, but they knew little about it until plastic knuckles were imported in 1954 in large quantities and revived the game. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,176</i>)
